Maersk · MAERSK.B-DK · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Maersk

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 46,544 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces are the decisive part of this case, and over the model horizon they are negative. Maersk's 2026 earnings are being set by temporary capacity absorption (Red Sea detours, congestion, Hormuz) on top of resilient but pulled-forward demand. Each of those supports is either reversing (the Red Sea) or episodic (congestion, weather). The opposing force, an orderbook of 39-44% of the fleet delivering mostly in 2027-2028 into an industry with near-zero scrapping and 26-30bn of net cash, is contractual and certain. Demand, regulation and macro are second-order modifiers. The Consumer should model a cyclical downturn that starts in Q4 2026 or Q1 2027 and troughs in 2028, with timing rather than direction as the main uncertainty, and with an explicit upside scenario of renewed disruption. The external picture is therefore strongly negative for an investment case spanning 18 months to four years, even though the next one or two quarters remain strong.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0222C-0540C-0735C-0935C-3550C-3935
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F2219th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Competitive intensity: to what extent and in which direction it is changing

Judgement: Competitive intensity is low today because capacity is absorbed. It is set to rise sharply over the next 6-18 months; the direction is unambiguously toward more intensity. The structure has not changed (the top five carriers hold 65.4% of capacity). What changes is the capacity balance, and this industry's conduct is volume-first whenever capacity is loose.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Today: fleet utilisation 96%; port congestion absorbing about 8.5% of the fleet (about 3m TEU), and typhoon-driven queues tying up 12.6% in August 2026; global schedule reliability 49.9% in August; spot rates more than double pre-conflict levels. Carriers are not competing on price because they have no space to sell.
  • Coming: Suez transits doubled from Q1 2026 to 50-54% of 2023 levels. Gemini has moved six services back to the Suez route. CMA CGM restored regular Suez sailings in Q3. Morgan Stanley puts the capacity released by full normalisation at about 7% of the fleet (2-2.3m TEU). The orderbook is 39-44% of the fleet (13.2-14.9m TEU), with deliveries peaking in 2027-2028. Scrapping is near zero (5k TEU in 2025, an estimated 41k TEU in 2026).
  • Conduct: industry net cash of 26-30bn lets carriers "play chicken" rather than scrap (Bernstein, Jefferies, DNB Carnegie). MSC (21.5% share, orderbook of 2.2-2.9m TEU) and CMA CGM (fleet +34% gross to 2028) are expanding into the wave. Maersk itself has ordered about 500k TEU (26 large LNG dual-fuel ships for 2029-2030 delivery), contradicting its stated 4.1-4.3m TEU fleet target. Even the self-declared disciplined carrier is not restraining supply.
  • The last 12-18 months against the next 6: through 2025 rates fell (Maersk's average loaded rate went from 2,427 in Q1 2025 to 2,081 in Q1 2026, −14%) as 2.2m TEU of new capacity arrived. The 2026 Hormuz and congestion shock reversed this temporarily.

What it means for the model: assume price competition returns in 2027 as the release of Red Sea capacity coincides with 7%+ fleet growth. The shape of the rate decline should look more like 2023 (fast) than the gradual erosion of 2025, because contracts are shorter (quarterly and monthly terms are replacing annual ones) and 56% of Maersk's Ocean volume is spot-exposed.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

D4013th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Input costs, input availability and supplier concentration: headwind or tailwind

Judgement: Input costs are a material headwind that is currently passed through but will bite when the market loosens. The costs are bunker fuel (+44% year on year in Q2 2026, to 777 a tonne; Brent was 111 in the week of 23 October 2026, above the Q2 average of 96), carbon costs, high time-charter rates and congestion-related costs. Supplier concentration (Chinese yards take 65-72% of orders, and yard books are full to 2029; a handful of engine licensors) raises newbuild prices but does not threaten availability. Net effect: neutral in 2026, because costs are recovered; a headwind of perhaps 0.5-1bn in 2027 if rates fall faster than surcharges can be held (our estimate).

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Bunker: about 19-21% of Ocean operating costs; Q2 2026 bunker cost 2.1bn (+36%). Maersk does not hedge bunker with derivatives (Hapag-Lloyd can hedge up to 80% but currently has no hedges). Formula-based bunker adjustment covers the 44% of volumes on contract; spot surcharges, revised every 14 days, cover the rest. The disclosed sensitivity is 100 a tonne = 0.2-0.3bn of EBIT, net of recovery. Morgan Stanley doubts full recovery can be sustained once supply growth outpaces demand.
  • Cape diversion costs: about 500m a month of added network cost, recovered by surcharges of about 500m a month. When the Red Sea normalises, both sides fall away; the earnings effect comes through the base rate, not the surcharge.
  • Charters: the charter market is tight (lessor utilisation 98%). Maersk extended three 9,500 TEU charters at 41,000 a day to 2031. With 43% of the fleet leased, charter costs stay sticky for years even if rates fall.
  • Shipyards: global yards are booked to 2029; newbuild prices are 5-10% off the 2024 peak but about 46% above pre-COVID. Green methanol is scarce; Maersk's offtakes cover over half of its 2027 dual-fuel needs, including 500k tonnes a year from Goldwind.

What it means for the model: hold non-bunker Ocean cost per FFE at about the H1 2026 level, less the Cape-diversion component as Suez reopens. Model bunker at the forward curve, and assume pass-through of 70-90% in 2027 falling to perhaps 60-70% in a loose 2028 market (our assumption, to be tested in the second set).

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3516th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Macro forces acting on the company, its sector and its industry, and where they bite hardest

Judgement: Macro bites hardest at company level through oil (bunker costs, and demand destruction if oil stays high) and the US consumer. At industry level it bites through the discount rate the market applies to a cyclical at its cyclical peak. Macro is nonetheless second-order to the supply cycle: a strong 2027 macro year would slow the rate decline, not stop it.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Environment (Fitness for Fighting digest, 23 October 2026): risk-off confirmed and broadening; US 10-year yield near 5.10% with a December hike more than 90% priced; Brent at 111; European defensives leading cyclicals on every window; the Fragility tilt at Double Underweight, and the Momentum tilt moved to Underweight this week. Maersk is a high-momentum (relative strength in the 97th percentile), operationally complex cyclical at the dearest point of its ten-year P/E range. It sits on the wrong side of both tilts.
  • Demand: US growth tracking 3.3% and unemployment 4.2%, but consumer surveys falling (Michigan 46.3, Conference Board 81.9); European confidence weak; Chinese PPI at 5% against CPI under 1%. Growth is driven by AI-led capex rather than goods consumption (Morgan Stanley), which limits carriers' scope to pass on costs.
  • Currency: Maersk reports in USD; on average 50% of net non-USD flows over the next 12 months are hedged; a 10% USD move is worth about 0.2-0.3bn of EBIT. The DKK share price falls for a given USD value if the dollar weakens (EUR/USD forecasts range from 1.14 to 1.23).
  • Interest rates: Maersk holds net cash, so higher rates raise interest income. The material effect is on valuation.

What it means for the model: keep macro inside the volume range (2-4%) and the bunker line, and carry FX translation as a small item. The valuation discussion in the fourth set must recognise that the current environment penalises exactly this profile.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Three further dynamics matter. First, contract durations are shortening (annual contracts are giving way to quarterly and monthly terms), which accelerates both the up-swing and the down-swing in Maersk's realised rates. Second, the forwarder channel's hostility to integrated carriers is a slow-burning negative for Ocean volumes. Third, the industry's cash pile (26-30bn of net cash) removes the financial distress that ended previous price wars. On the positive side, chronic under-investment in port and landside infrastructure (Drewry: a world "critically undersupplied by terminal capacity") supports terminal returns and periodically absorbs vessel capacity.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Contracts: the contract share is 44% at Maersk. Procurement managers report three-to-six-month terms, and on some lanes the share of cargo on spot rose from 40% to 80%. Customers held to agreed 2025 rates without reopening contracts, a sign that the contract book holds when the market rises.
  • Forwarders supply up to 50% of ocean volumes. Experts report diversion to MSC and CMA CGM, and some shippers cutting Maersk allocations by up to 30% over reliability problems during routing reconfiguration; others report a flight to quality toward carriers that own terminals.
  • Cash: Bernstein says carriers can survive multi-year losses; capacity discipline has historically come only with widespread loss-making.

What it means for the model: realised-rate declines in 2027 transmit within one to two quarters of spot. Do not assume a contract-lag cushion of more than about one quarter, and assume no supply discipline before losses are widespread.

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

F5015th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The threat from new entrants is low in Ocean and moderate in Logistics.

  • Standard entrants in deep-sea container shipping: very low threat (billions of capital needed, decades to build networks).
  • Adjacent entrants: Chinese leasing houses (about 9% of the merchant fleet) add capacity indirectly, a moderate threat through supply. Integrated carriers moving into logistics (CMA CGM/CEVA) are a moderate threat to Logistics & Services. Amazon's Supply Chain Services for non-Amazon merchants is a moderate threat in contract logistics and e-commerce fulfilment.
  • Orthogonal or innovative entrants: digital and AI coordination platforms and visibility aggregators (Project44-type) commoditise the carrier interface, a low-to-moderate threat that slowly erodes the "digital" differentiation.

What it means for the model: no entrant effect on Ocean volumes; a modest cap on Logistics & Services margins.

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F3518th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How suppliers are behaving: a support or a source of risk

Judgement: Suppliers are a source of cost risk, not support. Shipyards (China at 65-72% of new orders; books full to 2029; newbuild prices about 46% above pre-COVID, though down 5-10% from the 2024 peak) hold the power over Maersk's fleet renewal cost. Charter owners lock in high rates for long periods (9,500 TEU ships at 41,000 a day to 2031). Bunker suppliers price off oil, which reached 111 in October 2026. Green-fuel supply is scarce (methanol is expensive and short; Maersk has hedged with offtakes and LNG optionality). Engine makers (Wärtsilä) have pricing power. Port labour is a periodic risk (a 24-hour international port strike in early 2026; the earlier US dockworkers' dispute over automation).

What it means for the model: charter cost per slot stays high into 2028 even as rates fall (a margin squeeze); capex per TEU of newbuild is high; bunker is driven by oil with partial pass-through.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0148C-0330C-0445C-0842C-3235C-3745C-4345
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F4816th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading End-market demand: how much it is changing, structurally or cyclically, and with what visibility

Judgement: Container demand is not the swing factor in this case. It is running cyclically above trend in 2026 (global volumes about +5% in H1 2026, against a 2019-2025 trend of about 2.1% a year), lifted by restocking and tariff front-loading, and it is likely to revert to 2-3% growth over the next 12-18 months. Visibility is poor beyond six months. The structural growth rate is lower than in history: the container-trade-to-GDP multiplier has fallen from about 2x before 2008 to about 1-1.2x now. Demand is the minor partner to supply. A demand air pocket in 2027 would compound the downturn, not cause it.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Strength now: Maersk loaded volumes +9.3% in Q1 2026 and +4.1% in Q2 2026; Asia-Europe +10%, intra-Asia +11% and Transpacific +4% (July 2026); US imports +7% in August; European importers began restocking in mid-2026; US retail inventory-to-sales ratios near their lowest since 2023; Chinese exports of electrical machinery near 90bn a month, +50% since early 2026 (AI and electrification goods).
  • Fragility: H1 2026 demand was partly pulled forward ahead of tariff deadlines (Bernstein). Morgan Stanley warns of a late-2026 air pocket, and notes that the seasonal peak now comes in June, so the post-peak fade is sharper. US consumer confidence is weak (University of Michigan 46.3). The US average tariff is 22.8% against 2.4% in 2024. The IMF cut its 2026 world growth forecast to 3.1%. Consumption has shifted toward services, which Jefferies calls permanent.
  • Structural rate: DNB Carnegie projects a 1.2x trade-to-GDP multiplier through 2029 (1.4x after the financial crisis, 2.3x in 2002-2008); Bernstein puts the post-crisis multiplier at about 1x. Sourcing diversification away from China (to Vietnam and India) slightly lengthens routes, a small tonne-mile positive.
  • Visibility: under six months for volumes. The only multi-year-visible force in this industry is supply (the orderbook), not demand.

What it means for the model: use global container demand growth of 2-4% (Maersk's own 2026 assumption range) for 2027-2029, and Maersk volume growth roughly in line with the market (management's stated Ocean aim). Treat a 2027 demand dip after the 2026 front-loading as a downside scenario rather than the base case. Do not let volume carry the forecast: it is worth a fraction of the rate sensitivity.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F3019th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Disruption forces: whether they are strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction they act on Maersk

Judgement: The disruption force that matters is the geopolitical and climate disruption of shipping routes (Red Sea, Strait of Hormuz, Panama Canal drought, typhoons, strikes). It acts in Maersk's favour, because it absorbs capacity and lifts rates. It is now weakening on its largest leg (the Red Sea) while strengthening on smaller, episodic legs (Hormuz, port congestion). The bull argument that "disruption is the new normal" is partly right: the base rate of capacity-absorbing events has risen since 2020. It is not strong enough to absorb a fleet expansion of 20% or more over 2026-2028. The disruption of Maersk's own model (integrators turning forwarders into rivals; technology platforms such as Amazon Supply Chain Services) is slow and second-order.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Weakening: Suez containership transits are at 54% of pre-disruption levels and rising. Maersk was moving "one-third of normal volumes" through the canal at Q2, then moved four more Gemini services in September. The Suez Canal Authority expects a full Maersk return by March 2027; Maersk has not confirmed this, citing security.
  • Strengthening: Strait of Hormuz restrictions (Middle East imports −40% in Q2), Houthi blockades of Saudi-linked vessels, typhoon-driven port closures, Panama draft restrictions returning in 2026, delays of up to 12 days at Shanghai and Ningbo. These absorb capacity episodically rather than continuously.
  • Sizing: the Red Sea detour absorbed about 7-11% of capacity at its peak (sources range from 6% to 12%); congestion absorbs about 8.5%. Against that, the fleet grows about 4.6% in 2026, 7.4% in 2027 and 13.2% in 2028 (Bernstein). Even if congestion persists, the Red Sea release plus the 2027-2028 deliveries exceed any plausible absorption.
  • The bears' timing record: they were early in 2024 (the Red Sea), in 2025 (tariff front-loading and resilient non-US demand; short interest peaked at 33% of free float in August 2025 and was squeezed) and in 2026 (Hormuz, congestion). That record argues for humility on timing, not on direction.

What it means for the model: build the base case on a gradual Red Sea normalisation through H1 2027 with episodic congestion. Carry an explicit upside scenario in which a renewed Red Sea closure or a Hormuz escalation re-absorbs 5-10% of capacity (rates stay near 2026 levels into 2027), and a downside scenario in which full Suez normalisation coincides with the 2027 delivery wave.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F4515th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Political or regulatory change, and how it bears on Maersk

Judgement: Political and regulatory change is significant and mixed. Trade policy (US tariffs, protectionism) is a moderate demand headwind that has so far produced front-loading rather than collapse. Carbon regulation (EU ETS at 100% of verified emissions in 2026, FuelEU Maritime, the UK ETS from July 2026, the deferred IMO net-zero framework) is a rising cost: it is passed on in tight markets, will be harder to pass on in loose ones, and over time favours carriers with dual-fuel fleets such as Maersk. Geopolitics is, for now, the single largest support to earnings.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Trade: US average tariff 22.8% (2.4% in 2024), Chinese retaliation 34%; the US Section 122 flat 10% tariff expired in July 2026; Managed by Maersk revenue fell 8.9% on China-US tariffs; China's real manufacturing exports still grew 4-5%.
  • Carbon: EU ETS costs rose 96% in 2025 (76m in Q3 2025 alone). Morgan Stanley estimates about 491m a year of EU ETS cost by 2027. The FuelEU intensity cut is 2% in 2025, rising to 6% by 2030. The IMO deferred its net-zero vote by a year, to October 2026; Clerc calls this a "non-event" for Maersk's capex because the fleet renewal is dual-fuel anyway. The delay helps less-green competitors in the near term.
  • Competition law: Gemini and the other alliances operate under antitrust scrutiny in several jurisdictions; Maersk's 2025 prospectus names regulatory challenge as a risk to cooperative agreements.
  • Labour and politics: the US East and Gulf Coast labour deal (a six-year master contract) removed strike risk; Antwerp staff struck over the end of remote working (effective January 2027); Danish exit-tax rules hinder senior hiring.

What it means for the model: model EU ETS/FuelEU as a gross cost of roughly 0.4-0.5bn a year and rising, recovered through surcharges in 2026 and only partly recovered in 2027-2028. Treat tariffs as a volume risk embedded in the 2-4% demand range. No regulatory change alters the structural supply picture.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F4217th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent an industry or sector driver is accelerating the company's demand

Judgement: No industry driver is accelerating Maersk's demand structurally. The forces that lifted 2026 (tonne-mile inflation from detours, congestion, front-loading, restocking) are supply-side or timing effects, not new demand, and they are reversing. The genuinely structural tailwinds are small: sourcing diversification (China-plus-one) lengthening routes, electrification and AI-related goods, and steady terminal throughput growth that benefits APM Terminals.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Head-haul volumes on Maersk's core routes grew an estimated 7-8%. Since 2019 terminal capacity at the top ten hubs has grown 21% against trade growth of 28%, which supports Terminals pricing (revenue per move +7.1% to 385 in Q2 2026).
  • Chinese electrical machinery exports +50% in 2026; high-value electronics lift premium air and ocean demand (Logistics & Services Air volumes +20% in Q1 2026).
  • Against: a trade-to-GDP multiplier of about 1-1.2x, the shift to services, tariffs.

What it means for the model: Terminals volumes about 4% a year, with revenue per move growing low-to-mid single digits; Ocean volumes in line with the market; no structural demand uplift.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F3518th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How customer demand is behaving, how far it supports the thesis, and how fragile that support is

Judgement: Demand is behaving well (H1 2026 global volumes about +5%; Maersk Q1 +9.3%, Q2 +4.1%; Asia-Europe +10%; restocking in Europe and the US) but does not support a positive thesis, because supply growth of 7-13% a year in 2027-2028 overwhelms 2-4% demand growth. The support is fragile: part of 2026 was pulled forward by tariffs, the seasonal peak now comes earlier (June) with a sharper fade, consumer confidence is weak, and growth is capex-led rather than goods-led. Large beneficial cargo owners prioritise space and reliability in tight markets, but in loose markets most standard customers buy on price.

What it means for the model: volumes at market growth (2-4%); no premium pricing from customer demand in 2027-2028.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

D4514th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: On balance it slightly hurts today and slightly helps later, and it is highly fragile because geopolitics dominates. Carbon regulation (EU ETS phased in to 100%; FuelEU; the UK ETS; the deferred IMO framework) raises costs but rewards Maersk's dual-fuel fleet as the penalties tighten. Trade policy (US tariffs at 22.8% on average) caps demand. Competition authorities scrutinise the alliances, Gemini included. Governments (Egypt's Suez Canal Authority, Red Sea security, Hormuz) control the largest swing factor. Danish exit-tax rules hinder senior hiring.

What it means for the model: a rising carbon cost line, recovered in tight markets and partly absorbed in loose ones; no regulatory upside within 18 months; a long-dated tailwind for the green fleet after 2029.

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F4515th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: The addressable markets are large and grow slowly. Ocean: global container volumes growing 2-4% a year, with Maersk holding about 14% of capacity, so its growth is capped near the market's. Logistics: global forwarding, contract logistics and landside markets run to hundreds of billions; Logistics & Services (about 16bn) holds a small share, so the market is not the constraint (execution and the forwarder conflict are). Terminals: global throughput grows roughly in line with GDP (Bernstein expects about 4% a year for APM Terminals), plus pricing. Realistic mid-term organic growth is about 3-4% for group volumes and about 4-6% for revenue excluding freight-rate effects. Freight rates dominate reported revenue growth either way.

What it means for the model: revenue in 2027-2029 is driven by rates (down), with ex-rate volume growth of about 3%; Logistics & Services revenue growth of high single digits if Solutions executes, about 5% if not; Terminals revenue growth of about 6-8% (volume plus price).

Overall external picture

C-0625C-1022
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F2519th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Where Maersk's own revenue and demand cycle stands, and to what extent it is turning

Judgement: Maersk's revenue cycle is at, or within one quarter of, its peak. Revenue growth of +20% in Q2 2026 (from −2.6% in Q1) and consensus FY2026 revenue of 63.5bn (+18%, above 2021's 61.8bn) mark the top. The turn is already visible in the lead indicators: Asia-Europe spot rates rolled over in September-October as Suez capacity returned, and the SCFI peak-season fade began in July, while US-bound lanes stay firm. Expect the first year-on-year revenue decline in Q4 2026 or Q1 2027, and a revenue trough in 2028.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Quarterly revenue: 13.3bn (Q1 2025), 13.1bn, 14.2bn, 13.3bn, 13.0bn (Q1 2026), 15.8bn (Q2 2026). Consensus implies about 34.8bn for H2 2026, about 17.4bn a quarter: a further step up from Q2.
  • Rates: the average loaded rate went 2,427 (Q1 2025) → 2,259 (Q2 2025) → 2,081 (Q1 2026) → 2,746 (Q2 2026). The SCFI rose 55% in Q2 over Q1. Morgan Stanley says Asia-Europe spot has rolled over while US-bound lanes remain firm.
  • Consensus already embeds the turn: revenue −7.5% in FY2027 and −2.6% in FY2028 (brokers' basis); EBITDA −38% and −14%.
  • The second-half 2026 numbers implied by FY2026 consensus are very demanding (see C-68). That is where the turn will first show against expectations.

What it means for the model: peak quarterly revenue in Q3 2026; sequential decline from Q4 2026. Ocean revenue should fall faster than group revenue, because Cape-related surcharges (about 6bn a year) disappear on top of base-rate erosion. Terminals and Logistics & Services revenue grow mid-single digits through the cycle.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F2219th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, then all of the above together: headwinds against tailwinds and the overall external picture

Judgement: The external picture is a strong, temporary tailwind (capacity absorbed by disruption) giving way to a strong, structural, highly visible headwind (the largest supply wave in container-shipping history). The tailwind is fading now; the headwind arrives in force through 2027-2028. On a six-month forward view the balance has turned negative; on an 18-month view it is clearly negative. Only an unforecastable new disruption changes this. The cone of outcomes is very wide on timing and narrow on direction.

Driver by driver (the dashboard's key drivers for this cohort: freight rates, fleet supply and orderbook, route disruption):

  • Route disruption (a tailwind, weakening): the single largest swing factor. Suez transits are at 54% and rising, and six Gemini services have been restored. The tailwind is strong but likely to halve over six to nine months. It is fragile in both directions: a new attack or a Hormuz escalation would bring it back within weeks.
  • Fleet supply and orderbook (a headwind, strengthening): an orderbook of 39-44% of the fleet; deliveries adding 7.4% in 2027 and 13.2% in 2028; near-zero scrapping; Maersk itself adding about 10% to its fleet in 2029-2030. The headwind is very strong and contractually visible two or more years out.
  • Freight rates (the output): at a cyclical high (Q2 2026 loaded rate 2,746; Q3 is likely higher), with the first rollover on Asia-Europe. Direction for 2027: down. Depth: uncertain, with a return to the 2016 lows (DNB Carnegie's expectation) as the bear extreme and a moderate, 2019-like trough as the base case.
  • Demand (neutral to headwind): 2-4% growth, front-loading risk, a weak consumer.
  • Input costs (a headwind, currently recovered): oil at 111, rising carbon costs, sticky charter costs.
  • Regulation: a mild headwind now, a mild tailwind for green fleets later.
  • Landside bottlenecks (a tailwind for Terminals, partial support for rates): the strongest counter-argument to the bear case, and real for APM Terminals' pricing.

Level by level: in the value chain, value is shifting toward terminals (concession scarcity) and shipyards (full books) and away from ocean carriage. In the industry (container shipping), the picture is strongly negative for 2027-2028. In the sector (logistics), it is mixed: forwarders benefit from falling freight costs and cost synergies (DSV), and the Sector digest prefers contracted and asset-light models over Maersk's commodity-priced Ocean business.

What it means for the model: base case of average loaded rates falling materially in 2027 from the 2026 average (itself about 2,600-2,800 if H2 runs above Q2) and bottoming in 2028; Ocean EBIT negative in 2028 in the base case; Terminals and Logistics & Services as the earnings floor (about 2.3-2.6bn of combined EBIT net of unallocated costs).

Internal change forces/risks

–not yet rated

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Maersk's internal change is a better-run Ocean network (Gemini's reliability and unit cost), a deliberately expanding Terminals business, a reorganised Logistics & Services arm with new external leadership, and lighter corporate overhead. It all sits beside a pivot back to fleet growth that contradicts the stated capital discipline, an unproven integrator strategy that continues to cost forwarder goodwill, and visible organisational strain. Guidance has been raised dramatically, but by the market, not by management action. The owner offers stability and no catalyst for strategic change. For the model: modest self-help (a few hundred million of EBIT by 2028), capex near 5bn a year, and capital returns that follow earnings down. Internal change slightly improves Maersk's position relative to peers in the downturn; it does not change the direction of earnings.

Internal risks

Group judgement

Risks

F2418th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is dominated by one high-probability, high-magnitude risk, the unwinding of the capacity absorbed by disruption into the largest supply wave on record, rather than by low-probability tails. The reported growth engine is still accelerating while its input force is reversing, so the deterioration is ahead, not behind. Sentiment risk is elevated: a crowded, high-momentum cyclical at the top of its valuation range in a risk-off market, where a single ceasefire headline can move the shares 10-20%. A fair trough valuation sits about 35-45% below the current price, against about 20-25% upside if disruption persists. The skew over 12-18 months is clearly negative. The balance sheet removes solvency risk, so the downside is valuation and earnings, not capital.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

D5814th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Standards are clearly improving in Ocean operations and cost discipline, deteriorating in parts of Logistics & Services and in cash conversion, and mixed in governance. The Ocean improvement is real and measurable, and it will lower Maersk's break-even relative to peers in the downturn.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Improving: Gemini schedule reliability of about 90% against an industry average of 50-60% (49.9% in August 2026); Ocean unit cost at fixed bunker −7% year on year in Q1 2026 and −0.8% in Q2 2026; Gemini benefits of 950m, above the top of the 820m-1.1bn range; vessel utilisation of 96%; bunker consumption down 8-9% on a capacity-adjusted basis; 1,000 corporate roles cut (15% of corporate functions; savings reported as 130m or 180m a year); Logistics & Services EBIT margin up year on year for nine consecutive quarters; segment reporting made more transparent.
  • Deteriorating: Solutions margin 1.7% (from 3.8%); acquired businesses (Pilot, LF Logistics) still on stand-alone IT platforms; group cash conversion 69% in H1 2026 against 92% in H1 2025; employee turnover up; strikes at Antwerp.
  • Governance: the vessel useful-life extension (20 to 25 years) lifted 2026 EBIT by about 700m with little explanation; PwC has been auditor for fifteen years; individual board shareholdings are not disclosed. Against that, a fully independent Audit Committee and a strong compliance framework.

What it means for the model: assume Maersk's Ocean cost per FFE stays 3-7% below what it would have been without Gemini (a structural cost advantage over non-Gemini peers), Logistics & Services margins edging up toward 5.5-6% by 2028, and Solutions recovering only slowly.

C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

F3018th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The negative corporate developments are significant and point the same way: weaker capital discipline and organisational strain at a cyclical peak.

Reasoning and evidence:

  1. Capital discipline: ordering about 500k TEU into the industry's largest supply wave, at newbuild prices about 46% above pre-COVID, which contradicts the stated fleet policy.
  2. The integrator strategy's cost: forwarders alienated (they supply up to 50% of ocean volumes); some shippers reported cutting Maersk allocations by up to 30%; Solutions margin down to 1.7%; Fulfilled by Maersk loss-making in 2024.
  3. Market share: global capacity share 13.7% in mid-2026, the lowest in 20 years (19.3% in 2018); MSC at 21.5%; CMA CGM projected by Alphaliner to overtake Maersk by 2027.
  4. Organisation: turnover of 14%; the Antwerp strike over the end of remote working (from January 2027); executive churn; a two-member Executive Board; key-person dependence on Clerc; former executives describe the matrix as "too big, too complex, and too slow" and say the legacy "our word is our bond" ethos is gone.
  5. Earnings quality: the vessel-life change; "underlying" definitions; Q1 2026 net profit missing consensus by 67.5% on minorities and financial items.
  6. Cash: conversion falling, capex and lease payments together about 10bn a year.
  7. Plausible: buyback cuts in 2027-2028; impairments of Ocean assets or logistics goodwill in the downturn; forwarder diversion accelerating once carriers compete for cargo again.

What it means for the model: Maersk volume growth slightly below the market in 2027-2028 (share loss to MSC and CMA CGM continues until Maersk's own newbuilds arrive in 2029-2030); a downside scenario with impairment; capex not falling with earnings.

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F3219th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent the culture is, with alignment across the whole system

Judgement: Low congruence. The system is pulled between an asset-heavy, engineering-led carrier culture and an asset-light, customer-led integrator ambition, and the evidence shows friction rather than alignment.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Former employees and executives describe the matrix as "too big, too complex, and too slow", say the "our word is our bond" ethos is "completely gone", and say acquired family-owned teams struggled with the top-down processes. An MSC general director cites "a humongous number of executive changes" and fading loyalty among heritage staff.
  • Turnover rose to 14% in 2025 (from 11%), with 15,294 leavers; only 40% of employees felt progress was made after past feedback. Antwerp staff struck in October 2026 over the end of remote working. The engagement score of 83 (top 10% on Glint) contradicts the departure behaviour, and the survey method changed, so the comparison is weak.
  • Incentives point in the right direction: the 2025-2027 long-term plan weights 80% on segment ROIC and 20% on non-financial measures (no EPS target); the short-term plan weights EBIT and FCF. But the Logistics & Services margin component scored zero in 2025, a sign the strategic goal is not being achieved.
  • Strategy and capital allocation are not congruent: a stated fleet cap against a 500k TEU order; "terminal constraints blunt vessel growth" against vessel investment; integrator against forwarder customers.

What it means for the model: execution risk in Logistics & Services stays high; Ocean operations, where the culture is strongest, are the reliable part.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

F2518th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: The biggest sudden fundamental risk is not low-probability: a rapid, full Red Sea normalisation is the most likely large shock. The truly low-probability, high-magnitude risks are operational (a cyber attack, a hub failure), commercial (a forwarder boycott, a price war) and accounting (impairments). We also list the main sudden upside risk, because the distribution is two-sided.

Risk by risk (plausibility over 12-18 months and approximate magnitude; our judgements):

  1. Rapid, full Suez normalisation (for example a Red Sea peace deal). Plausibility moderate (it is already under way gradually). Magnitude very large: about 7% of global capacity released within weeks; Asia-Europe rates could fall 30-50%; group EBIT could fall by 3-6bn a year from the 2026 run-rate.
  2. A price war ("shark attack") in 2027-2028 as deliveries peak. Plausibility moderate. Magnitude very large: rates toward the 2016 lows (DNB Carnegie's expectation); Ocean losses of 2-4bn a year; group EBIT negative.
  3. A global demand shock (recession or tariff escalation; in Q2 2020 volumes fell 15-18%). Plausibility low to moderate. Magnitude large, mostly through rates.
  4. A cyber attack (the 2017 NotPetya attack erased tens of thousands of computers and cost 250-300m). Plausibility low. Magnitude moderate in money, large in reputation.
  5. Failure at a Gemini hub (Tanjung Pelepas, Salalah, Tangier Med) cascading through the hub-and-spoke network. Plausibility low. Magnitude moderate: the reliability premium is lost and costs rise.
  6. Forwarder boycott and volume diversion. Plausibility low to moderate. Magnitude: up to 50% of Ocean volume comes through forwarders; a 5-10% volume loss would cost a few hundred million of EBIT plus utilisation damage.
  7. Impairment of Ocean assets or logistics goodwill in the downturn (book values are higher after the life extension; acquisitions of more than 8bn). Plausibility moderate in 2027-2028. Magnitude: non-cash, a large reported hit, a headline risk.
  8. A major vessel casualty, sanctions breach or antitrust action against Gemini. Plausibility low. Magnitude moderate.
  9. Sudden upside: renewed Red Sea closure or Hormuz escalation re-absorbing 5-10% of capacity. Plausibility moderate. Magnitude: FY2027 EBIT stays near 2026 levels (6-8bn).

What it means for the model: carry (1) and (2) as explicit downside scenarios and (9) as the upside scenario; the base case already embeds a gradual version of (1).

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

F358th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The drivers are as concentrated as they can be in a company of this size. One variable, the Ocean base freight rate net of pass-through surcharges, generates the whole of the EBITDA difference between the forecast years and the recent past; every other operating driver either moves with it (pass-through costs, hub income, congestion costs) or nets to roughly zero (cost inflation against self-help, volume against Terminals and Logistics growth). Below EBITDA, three further items (a tax charge that does not fall with profit, interest income that falls with the cash balance, and the end of buyback accretion) are what take the difference from "a lower EBITDA" to "no earnings per share", and each is small but, in combination and in a year of near-zero operating profit, decisive. The rate itself has two inputs, the timing of the Suez return (uncertain, measured in quarters) and the orderbook (certain, measured in years), so the case is concentrated on one variable with one uncertain and one certain cause. Concentration of this degree makes the direction of the forecast highly predictable and its level poorly predictable; it also makes the case cheap to monitor, because the one variable is published weekly.

The arithmetic of concentration (from the second set, cross-checked here):

  • FY2027-FY2030 average EBITDA of about 7.3bn against FY2023-FY2025 of 10.4bn (brokers' basis): a difference of about 3.1bn a year. FY2026-to-FY2028 EBITDA falls by 6.0bn; the bridge is Ocean base-rate erosion about -6.3bn, lost hub and other Ocean revenue net of cost -0.7bn, cost inflation -0.8bn, against congestion-cost relief +0.7bn, self-help +0.7bn, volume +0.15bn and Terminals and Logistics growth +0.25bn. The items other than the rate sum to about -0.3bn: they cancel.
  • 100 per FFE of base rate is about 1.36bn of EBITDA and EBIT in FY2027, almost untaxed, so about 95 of EPS. Against a FY2027 base-case EPS of 43 and FY2028 of -47, the rate alone spans the sign of earnings within a 150-200 per FFE band, which is less than the quarter-on-quarter movement of Maersk's realised rate in any of the last six quarters.
  • Below EBITDA, the FY2028 per-share bridge is: EBIT including joint ventures +12 (of which the vessel-life benefit +49), net interest and other -24, tax -28, minorities -7, giving -47. Three items that sum to about -60 a share decide the sign. Each is now independently confirmed by the AlphaSense reports: the tax charge was 1.1bn on a 1.9bn net loss in 2016, 377m on 657m of pre-tax profit in 2019, 78m on 51m in Q4 2025 and 192m on 292m in Q1 2026, and DNB Carnegie models it flat at 480m through 2028; net financial items turn to a net expense in DNB Carnegie's 2026 numbers; Bernstein's company-basis free cash flow of about 200m for 2027 leaves no room for a buyback.
  • The precedents concentrate the same way. The AlphaSense dispersion report finds that in 2015-2016, 2019 and 2022-2023 "freight rates explained most misses" against forecasts made 12-18 months earlier; demand shocks (2019 front-loading, 2023 destocking) acted through rates, not around them.

Where diffusion exists, and why it does not help. The group has three segments, 130 countries and 100,000 customers, but diversification only diversifies the floor: non-Ocean EBITDA of about 4bn is stable, and the swing above it (Ocean EBITDA of 2.3-8.6bn across the forecast years, and 3.7-13bn of group EBITDA across the FY2027 cone) is single-variable. Hapag-Lloyd, with 50-65% of volume on contract, has a slower transmission of the same variable, not a different one; the forwarders (DSV, Kuehne+Nagel) have the opposite sign, which is why Bernstein's pair trade exists.

Concentration cuts both ways, and the Consumer should use both edges. Negatively: a single exogenous, volatile, daily-priced driver means that no amount of company analysis improves the forecast's level; the second set's probability framing (50% base, 30% deeper, 20% disruption-extended) is as good as the forecast gets. Positively: the memo's risk topic (#9) can be written as one driver, two causes and one transmission constant, and the monitoring plan (D-05) needs only the weekly spot indices, the Suez transit count and two dated contract seasons.

Two second-order drivers the Consumer should not promote to first order. Bunker: 100 a tonne is 0.2-0.3bn of EBIT net of recovery, a tenth of the rate's weight, and the pass-through mechanism (the Fossil Fuel Fee on 44-50% of volume, fortnightly spot surcharges on the rest) has held in 2026. Currency: a 10% USD move is about 0.3bn of EBIT; it moves the DKK price of the share more than the USD earnings.

Internal corporate change forces

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

F5217th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Changes among the chief executive, other key leaders and key actors, and what they signal

Judgement: The top is stable and the second tier has been substantially refreshed. The CEO (Vincent Clerc, Group CEO since January 2023, with 27 years at Maersk), the Chair (Robert Mærsk Uggla, also CEO of A.P. Møller Holding) and the controlling owner are all unchanged. Changed in 2026: the CFO, the Head of Investor Relations, the Global Head of Solutions and the Chief People Officer. Together the changes signal three things. First, the board and CEO are doubling down on the integrated-logistics strategy and buying in contract-logistics expertise to fix it, rather than retreating to a pure-play carrier. Second, they want sharper financial discipline. Third, the organisation is under strain. For the model this is modestly positive for Logistics & Services execution from 2027, neutral for Ocean, and slightly negative for organisational stability.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • CFO: Robert Erni joined in February 2026, replacing Patrick Jany after six years. Erni brings 30-plus years in asset-light logistics: about 20 years at Kuehne+Nagel, then Group CFO of Panalpina, then CFO of Dachser. Morgan Stanley flags a possible clash between his asset-light instincts and the asset-heavy fleet and terminal capex.
  • Solutions: Bruno Plantaz (ex Kuehne+Nagel and CEVA) starts on 1 November 2026, replacing Narin Phol, who left at the end of September after building the early Logistics & Services expansion. The Solutions EBIT margin fell to 1.7% in Q2 2026 from 3.8%, so this is a fix-it hire.
  • People: the Chief People Officer, Susana Elvira (24 years), leaves; Roberta Duarte, who spent a decade at Maersk until 2024, returns on 1 November 2026. Investor Relations: Martin Dunwoodie replaced Stefan Gruber before the March 2026 AGM.
  • Bench depletion: Henriette Hallberg Thygesen (30-year veteran) left in 2024 and the Head of Europe left in 2025. The formal Executive Board has only two members (CEO and CFO). There is no obvious internal CEO successor. A general director at MSC describes a "humongous number of executive changes". Employee turnover rose to 14% in 2025 from 11% (15,294 leavers).
  • No activist or ownership change. The board was re-elected in full at the March 2026 AGM.

What it means for the model: do not build any step-change in Ocean strategy into 2027-2028. Allow a gradual recovery of Solutions margins from 2027 under new leadership (low confidence), and a CFO-driven tightening of cost and capex discipline.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F4818th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Strategy has changed in five ways in the last 18 months: Logistics & Services was reorganised into Forwarding, Solutions and Landside (effective April 2026, each a separate cash-generating unit); large logistics acquisitions have been paused since January 2023 in favour of organic margin improvement; Ocean has become hub-and-spoke under Gemini (from February 2025) with a "lowest possible unit cost" priority; Terminals capital is expanding; and corporate overhead was cut (1,000 roles). A sixth change is under-articulated and the most consequential: a return to fleet growth. Maersk has ordered about 26 large LNG dual-fuel vessels (about 484-500k TEU, roughly 10% of its fleet) for 2029-2030, which contradicts its long-standing target of a stable 4.1-4.3m TEU fleet (the operated fleet was already 4.6m TEU in late 2025). The Logistics & Services and Terminals changes are well articulated and well resourced. The fleet pivot is not explained. Management calls it "opportunistic green replacement"; Bernstein calls it illogical given management's own argument that terminal constraints blunt the usefulness of vessel growth. Net: a mix of sound self-help and a regression in capital discipline.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Resourcing is not a constraint: 18.4bn of cash and deposits at Q1 2026, BBB+/Baa1 ratings, capex guided at 10-11bn for 2026-2027 combined, capital commitments of 14.6bn at Q2 2026.
  • Terminals: a 3bn programme to raise capacity by 30%; a 50-year concession at Da Nang; a 37.5% stake in Jeddah's South Container Terminal; automation at Bremerhaven (€1bn) and Lázaro Cárdenas; the Panama Canal Railway (77m of revenue and 36m of EBITDA in 2024).
  • Logistics & Services: Clerc's two 2026 priorities are to accelerate margin improvement and to improve organic growth. The 6% EBIT margin target is unchanged and still unmet (5.1% in Q2 2026, 4.8% for 2025).
  • One Earnings Review reports a "March 2025 Capital Markets Day" resetting targets to organic growth of 1.5x global GDP and a 35% air-and-sea conversion ratio. Those metrics look like DSV's, and the attribution may be wrong; verify before using them.

What it means for the model: capex stays near 5bn a year through 2027-2028 and probably beyond (newbuild instalments for 2029-2030 deliveries). Terminals capacity grows. There is no logistics M&A in the base case. Maersk's own fleet grows about 10% in 2029-2030, so its Ocean market share bottoms and then recovers after 2029.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

D5811th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent financial guidance has been raised, or mid-term targets set or raised

Judgement: FY2026 guidance has been raised twice within six weeks, and a third raise on 5 November 2026 is likely. Each raise was a mark-to-market of spot freight rates, not the product of self-help, and no mid-term target has been set or raised. The raises are large, but low in quality and non-recurring.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Underlying EBITDA guidance: 4.5-7.0bn (February, kept in May 2026) → 8-10bn (29 June 2026, an ad hoc release) → 10.5-12.5bn (13 August 2026). The midpoint doubled, from 5.75bn to 11.5bn.
  • Underlying EBIT guidance: a loss (February 2026 descriptions range from "−1.5bn to −1.0bn" to "−1.5bn to +1bn") → 2-4bn → 4.5-6.5bn. Free cash flow guidance: from about −1.5bn to above zero.
  • FactSet consensus (25 October 2026) has FY2026 EBITDA at 14.0bn and EBIT at 8.1bn, above the top of the range. Bernstein's 25 September call feedback said a further upgrade "looks likely".
  • Standing mid-term targets: ROIC above 7.5% through the cycle (missed: 5.7% in 2025); Logistics & Services EBIT margin of 6% (unmet); Terminals ROIC above 9% (met: 16.1% in 2025). None were raised.
  • The Researcher (22 October 2026) judged that the shipping owners' guidance upgrades "measure spot rates and asset sales rather than operating improvement".

What it means for the model: treat FY2026 guidance as a floor (a Q3 upgrade is probable) but not as evidence of a higher base for 2027. There is no mid-term target to anchor 2027-2029; the model has to be driven by rates and supply.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

F4515th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent the company is reducing its perimeter or composition

Judgement: Minor current activity. The big simplifications are history (Maersk Oil sold 2017, Maersk Drilling demerged 2019, Svitzer demerged April 2024 and later bought out by A.P. Møller Holding's vehicle). In 2026 Maersk sold Maersk Training and Maersk H2S Safety Services (August 2026, immaterial) and paused Maersk Growth's venture investing. C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

D589th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Expansion is modest, adjacent and mostly sensible, concentrated in Terminals (high-return gateway assets) and in specific logistics capacity. There are no large new geographies or product lines.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Terminals: a 50-year concession at Da Nang (a green "smart terminal"), the Jeddah stake, the Panama Canal Railway, automation at Bremerhaven and Lázaro Cárdenas. These earn about 16% ROIC against a 9% hurdle and are the best use of Maersk's capital.
  • Logistics: a 57,300 m² fulfilment centre at Hopedale, Massachusetts (100m), a Singapore distribution centre, the PUMA North American distribution network (three automated warehouses) and a five-year Columbia Sportswear UK contract. These are sensible, but they raise Solutions' start-up and "whitespace" costs in the short term.
  • Other: India-made containers (DCM Shriram); talks with Indian yards about 3,000 TEU vessels; the Altana AI "product passport" partnership; the Trade & Tariff Studio for customs.

What it means for the model: Terminals EBITDA growth of mid-to-high single digits a year from capacity and pricing; Solutions revenue growth with a margin lag; no new segment.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

C+555th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent the company is raising prices in real terms, and whether the rises are real, nominal, or latent

Judgement: Two different stories. In Ocean, prices are rising in real terms now (loaded rates +22% year on year in Q2 2026 against cost inflation of roughly 9-10% from bunker), but the rise is the market's, not Maersk's. It is cyclical, and it reverses as soon as capacity loosens (rates were −14% year on year in Q1 2026). The surcharges (emergency bunker, war risk, the 200-per-TEU fuel surcharge) are nominal cost recovery. In Terminals, real price increases are genuine and durable: revenue per move +6.8% in 2025 on an FX-adjusted basis and +7.1% in Q2 2026 (to 385), backed by scarce gateway capacity. In Logistics & Services, pricing is roughly cost-plus.

Reasoning and evidence: expert interviews claim a 5-10% Maersk premium for reliability, but another expert says that "approximately 95% of the time, standard customers simply demand the cheapest freight rate", and Maersk priced spot aggressively in early 2026 to fill Gemini. Customers did hold to agreed 2025 contract rates.

What it means for the model: model Ocean price as a market variable with at most a small premium; model Terminals with price growth of mid-single digits above inflation; do not capitalise 2026 Ocean pricing as pricing power.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

D5010th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent the company is stepping up M&A, and how sensible, disciplined and well integrated it is

Judgement: Maersk is not stepping up M&A, and the current discipline is a positive. Large logistics acquisitions have been paused since January 2023; only small, sensible infrastructure deals (Panama Canal Railway, Jeddah) have been made since; Ocean M&A is ruled out except opportunistically "to lower our break-even cost". The past record is poor and still weighs on returns: more than 8bn spent since 2019 (LF Logistics 3.6bn, Pilot Freight 1.6bn, Senator 644m, Performance Team 545m), mostly bought pro-cyclically at peak multiples, left on separate IT platforms, and earning below the cost of capital (Morgan Stanley: Logistics & Services needs to double EBIT to cover its capital cost).

Reasoning and evidence: rivals are active: CMA CGM bought FedEx Supply Chain for 1.4bn in 2026, and Hapag-Lloyd is buying ZIM for 4.2bn. A downturn could tempt Maersk back into logistics M&A at lower prices; the new CFO's background makes disciplined bolt-ons more plausible than transformational deals.

What it means for the model: no M&A in the base case. Watch for goodwill impairment risk on the acquired logistics businesses if the downturn hits volumes.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

D5511th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent the company is making more use of its balance sheet, or stepping up shareholder returns

Judgement: The balance sheet is being used more for capex (fleet renewal, terminals) than for returns. Shareholder returns were stepped down in 2026, not up: the buyback is 1bn over twelve months from February 2026 (DKK 6.3bn), against 2bn in 2025 and 3bn a year before it was suspended in February 2024. The dividend follows a 30-50% payout of underlying net profit (DKK 480 for 2025, a 40% payout). The 2026 earnings windfall (consensus net profit about 7.4bn) will lift the FY2026 dividend (consensus DPS 186, about 2.7bn, paid in 2027) and gives room for a larger buyback or a special distribution in February 2027. Management has not mooted either.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Precedent: the combined ordinary and extraordinary dividend of DKK 4,300 a share (10.9bn) in 2023; the share count fell 27% from 2019 to 2025 (20.1m to 14.7m).
  • Net cash excluding leases was 13.7bn at Q2 2026 (1.5bn including 12.2bn of lease liabilities). Capex is guided at 10-11bn for 2026-2027.
  • Morgan Stanley expects negative free cash flow from 2027 to restrict future buybacks. In February 2024 Clerc suspended the buyback within months when the outlook darkened.

What it means for the model: FY2026 dividend at about 40% of underlying net profit; the buyback renewed at about 1bn in February 2027 (perhaps more if the Q4 2026 cash position is strong), then cut or suspended for 2028 if company-defined free cash flow turns negative; payouts in 2027-2028 tracking the much lower earnings (consensus DPS 52 for FY2027 and 25 for FY2028).

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The most material positive is that Maersk enters the downturn with a lower relative cost position (Gemini) and a larger, stronger Terminals business than in any previous downturn. Both should let it break even at lower rates than its peers. The plausible positives are capacity and cost actions as rates fall, a stronger Gemini partner once Hapag-Lloyd completes the ZIM deal, and new logistics leaders lifting Solutions.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Present: Gemini's 950m of benefits; 26% year-on-year Ocean volume growth into Maersk-owned terminals in Q1 2026 (hub-and-spoke funnelling); Terminals ROIC of 16%; a dual-fuel fleet positioned for EU ETS and FuelEU (the cost advantage grows with carbon prices); net cash.
  • Plausible: the historic downturn playbook (blank sailings and capacity swaps withdrawing up to 20% of network capacity, slow steaming that once saved more than 300m of bunker, redelivering charters, cost programmes); deferral or resale of newbuild slots in a full-yard market; a special distribution in February 2027.
  • An accounting positive with no economic content: the vessel-life change keeps reported EBIT about 700m a year higher.

What it means for the model: give Maersk a modest cost-per-FFE advantage over peers in the downturn; include a self-help cost line from H2 2027 in the base case; do not count the depreciation change as an economic improvement.

C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

F4219th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Little scope. A.P. Møller Holding owns 41.5% of the capital and holds 51.5% of the votes, and with the A.P. Møller Foundation's 14.5% the family controls about 66% of the votes. The Chair is A.P. Møller Holding's CEO and backs Clerc and the integrator strategy explicitly. Nothing in the files indicates a planned change. Activism is structurally impossible: shareholder proposals (Kritiske Aktionærer on military cargo; Akademikerpension on sustainability-linked pay) were rejected.

Reasoning and evidence: the press reported in March 2025 that the Chair was "under pressure: not meeting strategic goals", but the board was re-elected in full in March 2026. The owner has a record of decisive portfolio moves (oil, drilling, towage) on its own timetable; it uses its own vehicle (A.P. Møller Holding Invest bought out Svitzer) for assets it wants to keep. It participates pro rata in buybacks.

What it means for the model: assume strategic continuity through 2029. The owner's long horizon also means it will not cut capex or abandon the integrator strategy to protect near-term earnings.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

F4518th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The positive corporate changes are real but narrow, and small relative to the external cycle. They are well resourced financially. They are concentrated in Ocean cost and network efficiency (Gemini), in Terminals expansion, and in the reorganisation and re-staffing of Logistics & Services. They are offset by a regression in capital discipline (the fleet order) and by organisational strain. Measured in money, the remaining self-help pool from here is perhaps 0.3-0.6bn of incremental annual EBIT by 2028 (Logistics & Services margin uplift of about 0.15-0.3bn, overhead savings of about 0.13-0.18bn, Terminals growth, further Gemini efficiencies; our estimate). The Ocean rate sensitivity is 1.3bn of EBIT per 100 per FFE. Corporate change cannot offset the cycle; it can only lower the trough by a few hundred million.

What it means for the model: layer a modest self-help line onto the rate-driven Ocean forecast; do not let management's change programme drive the earnings path.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

F3019th of 19

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's heading What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: dated items within 6 months, in months 6-18 and in months 18-36, how novel each is, and how well founded

Judgement: The staircase of novel positives is short and mostly behind the stock. The 2026 guidance upgrades were the novel positives, and the next one (5 November) is largely expected. Over the next 18 months the dated events are predominantly negative (Suez normalisation, 2027 contract season, FY2027 guidance, the delivery wave), interrupted by small, well-founded positives in Terminals and Logistics & Services and a possible larger cash return in February 2027. The positive staircase rebuilds only from about mid-2028, when the market can look through the trough toward the post-2029 decline in deliveries.

Next 6 months (to April 2027):

  • 5 November 2026, Q3 results: probably a strong quarter and a further FY2026 raise. Novelty low to moderate (consensus is already above guidance); well founded. The risk is a "sell the news" reaction.
  • 1 November 2026: the new Head of Solutions and CPO start. Low novelty.
  • Q4 2026: the Hapag-Lloyd-ZIM deal could close (expected Q4, subject to Israeli consent). It strengthens Gemini's scale but adds integration noise. Neutral.
  • Late 2026 to March 2027: further Gemini services return to Suez; the Suez Canal Authority expects a full Maersk return by March 2027. Novel, and negative for rates.
  • December 2026 to May 2027: annual contract negotiations (Asia-Europe, then transpacific) in a falling spot market. Negative.
  • Early February 2027, FY2026 results: FY2027 guidance (likely below consensus; negative). The FY2026 dividend at a 30-50% payout of about 7bn of underlying net profit (consensus DPS 186, about a 5.4% yield) and the renewal of the buyback (the 1bn programme ends around February 2027). A special distribution or an enlarged buyback would be a novel positive, well founded on the cash balance (net cash excluding leases of 13.7bn), but management has not mooted it.
  • October 2026: the deferred IMO net-zero vote; January 2027: the end of remote working (labour friction).

Months 6-18 (April 2027 to April 2028):

  • The 2027 delivery wave (7.4% fleet growth on Bernstein's figures; Morgan Stanley's 9.5% effective supply growth). Negative.
  • Logistics & Services margin progression toward 6% and Solutions recovery under new leadership. Novel if delivered, moderately well founded.
  • Terminals capacity additions (Bremerhaven, Lázaro Cárdenas, Da Nang works). Positive and incremental.
  • Probable self-help announcements (blank sailings, charter redeliveries, cost programme) in H2 2027. Positive for margins, but they confirm the downturn.
  • The March 2027 AGM.

Months 18-36 (April 2028 to October 2029):

  • The 2028 delivery peak (13.2%) and the probable earnings trough. Negative, but the stock should start to anticipate the recovery.
  • Deliveries decline from 2029; scrapping may accelerate under carbon rules (a positive if it comes).
  • Maersk's own dual-fuel newbuilds arrive in 2029-2030, positioned for tightening FuelEU (6% by 2030) and EU ETS. A positive in green positioning and share recovery.
  • Possible new mid-term targets or a Capital Markets Day under the new CFO. Speculative.

What it means for the model: few positive inflections within 18 months; the narrative is set by the supply cycle.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

F2819th of 19

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

F2819th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Container shipping is a concentrated industry that behaves like a fragmented one when capacity is loose. Exit is blocked (cash-rich, state- and family-owned carriers; long-lived assets), incumbent expansion is easy (an orderbook of about 40% of the fleet), and the largest competitor is volume-first and opportunistic. Value in the chain accrues to shipyards, terminal owners and asset-light forwarders, not to ocean carriers. Maersk's own position is split. In Terminals it owns a scarce, high-return asset with real pricing power. In Ocean it is a price-taker that has lost about six points of share, faces channel conflict with forwarders, and will drop to number three by 2027. History says carriers underperform from here (a record orderbook, a re-rating already done, routes normalising) and that Maersk fits the late-cycle peak pattern. For the model: rates are set by the supply-demand balance and the marginal carrier's break-even, not by industry structure; Maersk's relative resilience comes from Terminals and cost, not from share or price.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3235C-3338C-3430C-3745C-3835C-3935
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?F3518th of 19full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F3819th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How far the route to market and the intermediaries help or hinder the company, and how fragile that is

Judgement: The route to market is a moderate and fragile hindrance. Freight forwarders supply up to 50% of ocean volumes, and Maersk's integrator strategy has turned them into competitors. Forwarders have alternatives (MSC, CMA CGM, which serves forwarders through CEVA at arm's length, and Hapag-Lloyd, which explicitly collaborates with forwarders). Maersk ended direct contracts with some forwarders, pushing them to spot-only bookings. In a tight 2026 market this cost little; in a loose 2027-2028 market forwarders will place cargo with the carrier that treats them best.

Reasoning and evidence: Bernstein (Antoine Madre): "forwarders will avoid booking with Maersk Ocean due to its aggressive integrator strategy". Q1 2026 volume growth of +9.3% (against Hapag-Lloyd's −0.7%) shows no current damage, but it reflects aggressive spot pricing to fill Gemini. Maersk's own direct digital channels (spot booking, tracking, customs tools) are a strength with direct shippers.

What it means for the model: a small volume penalty in the downturn (0-1 point a year below market) is reasonable, together with a modest rate concession to keep forwarder volumes.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F3019th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Competitor behaviour hurts Maersk. MSC and CMA CGM are expanding capacity aggressively and buying logistics and terminal assets (CMA CGM: FedEx Supply Chain for 1.4bn, terminal stakes in the US, Europe and China, and Khalifa Port expanding 50% to 2.7m TEU). COSCO has ordered 42 methanol and 12 LNG dual-fuel ships. Hapag-Lloyd, Maersk's Gemini partner, is buying ZIM (4.2bn, more than 400 vessels and over 3m TEU combined, synergies of 300-500m, Israeli consent expected late 2026). That makes the partner larger, but it adds a high-cost, charter-heavy fleet and integration risk inside Gemini. Maersk's position is robust in Terminals and in cost, and fragile in Ocean share and pricing.

What it means for the model: competitor capacity drives the 2027-2028 rate path; include Gemini-related integration noise from 2027 if the ZIM deal closes.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?D4514th of 19full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F3519th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The disruption forces act mainly on supply (route disruption absorbing vessel capacity; weather and congestion), on suppliers (yards full to 2029; scarce green fuel; tight charter and dry-dock markets) and on regulators (delayed global carbon rules). They act less on demand (tariff timing effects) and on channels (integrators against forwarders). Today they favour Maersk; as the route disruption unwinds, Maersk's Ocean position becomes fragile while its Terminals position stays robust.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Supply: Red Sea, Hormuz and typhoons absorbed 7-12% of capacity; returning now (a tailwind fading).
  • Demand: front-loading and an air-pocket risk (neutral to negative).
  • Competitors: also benefit from disruption, but carbon rules penalise older fleets more than Maersk's dual-fuel ships (mildly positive).
  • Suppliers: yards and charter owners hold pricing power (negative for cost); yards' fullness caps new orders until 2029-2030 (a positive only beyond the model horizon).
  • Terminals and landside: chronic under-capacity and congestion favour gateway owners such as APM Terminals (positive); automation labour disputes such as the US dockworkers' (a risk).
  • Channels: integrators against forwarders (a negative for Maersk Ocean volumes).

What it means for the model: two different value-chain positions: Ocean is a price-taker exposed to every supply shock, while Terminals holds a scarce asset.
