3b

Industry structure

10 answers
Judgement28C-2445C-2550C-2638C-2740C-2825C-2925C-3122C-3550C-3660
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)F2819th of 19full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

F4515th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Where the company ranks by market share or size, and how far it is from number one or two

Judgement: In container shipping, Maersk is number two by capacity (about 13.6-14%, about 4.6-4.7m TEU and about 700 ships), about 8 percentage points (2.6m TEU) behind MSC (21.5%, 7.3m TEU), and only about 1 point ahead of CMA CGM (12.7%), which Alphaliner projects will overtake it by 2027. The gap to number one is widening: MSC's orderbook is 2.2-2.9m TEU. In terminals, APM Terminals is a top-tier global operator (53 facilities in 29 countries). In logistics, Maersk is a mid-tier player (Logistics & Services revenue about 16bn) against DSV (about DKK 247bn, about USD 37bn) and Kuehne+Nagel (about 27.5bn).

Reasoning and evidence: Maersk's capacity share has fallen from 19.3% in 2018 (20% in other sources) to 13.7% in mid-2026, the lowest in 20 years, because management capped the fleet at 4.1-4.3m TEU while MSC grew its fleet by 32% and overtook Maersk in 2022. Clerc's stated view is that there is "no correlation" between fleet size and margin. The current top five are MSC 21.5%, Maersk 13.6%, CMA CGM 12.7%, COSCO 11%, and Hapag-Lloyd 7.2% (about 9-10% with ZIM).

What it means for the model: Maersk's Ocean volume growth tracks or slightly lags the market until its 2029-2030 newbuilds arrive. Its rank will slip to number three in 2027.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

F5015th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How concentrated the market is, and how steep the share distribution

Judgement: The market is concentrated (the top five hold 65.4% of capacity, far above the 30% threshold; the top ten 84.7-86.3%), but the distribution is not steep. The top five hold about 3.1-3.4x the share of numbers six to ten (65.4% against about 19-21%), below the 5x marker. And within the top five, shares are close (21.5, 13.6, 12.7, 11, 7.2). There is no dominant price leader except MSC, which is volume-first. Concentration in this industry has not produced price discipline when capacity is loose.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Concentration is higher at the alliance level: three alliances control 62.2% of total fleet capacity and 69.7% of East-West mainlane capacity (Ocean Alliance 31.2%, Gemini 21.3%, Premier about 17.2%; MSC operates alone). Route-level Herfindahl-Hirschman indices reach 5,100.
  • Concentration rose from 65% (top ten, 2015) to 84.7% through the 2016-2018 merger wave, and Hapag-Lloyd's ZIM deal continues it.
  • The state-owned (COSCO) and family-owned (MSC, CMA CGM) structures of the largest rivals mean further consolidation is partly blocked, and that these rivals are not run for listed-equity returns.

What it means for the model: do not assume oligopoly pricing. Concentration helps only in coordinating blank sailings once losses are widespread.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F3817th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Market shares are unstable and predictable at the same time: they move with each carrier's orderbook. Over the last 8-10 years Maersk lost about 6 points (19.3% to 13.7%) while MSC gained to 21.5% and CMA CGM grew. New deep-sea entrants have taken a negligible share over 3-5 years; the capital barrier is prohibitive, and the COVID-era niche entrants were marginal. The real "entry" is expansion by incumbents through newbuilds, and it is large.

Reasoning and evidence: the orderbook distribution tells you the next shifts. MSC (2.2-2.9m TEU on order) and CMA CGM (+34% gross to 2028) will gain share through 2028; Hapag-Lloyd's own orderbook is about 14% of its fleet (20% in another figure), and Maersk's newbuilds arrive in 2029-2030. Chinese bank-backed leasing houses now own about 9% of the global merchant fleet: a financial owner class that adds capacity without operating it.

What it means for the model: Maersk volume growth 0-1 point below the market in 2027-2028, back in line or above it in 2029-2030 as its own ships arrive.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F4017th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent share consolidates toward the largest and the most advantaged competitors, and whether Maersk is among them

Judgement: Share has consolidated strongly toward the largest carriers (the top ten went from 65% in 2015 to about 85% now), and within them toward the most aggressive expanders (MSC, CMA CGM), not the most profitable. Maersk is among the largest, but by choice it is not among the share-gainers. It is advantaged on unit cost and reliability through Gemini, and on its terminal network, but not on scale economies, where MSC is larger. Its 2026 fleet order shows that management now accepts share loss has gone far enough.

Reasoning and evidence: in this industry the "advantaged" carrier in a downturn is the one with the lowest cash cost per slot and the deepest pockets: MSC (private, the largest fleet), COSCO (state-backed, domestic cargo), CMA CGM (family, diversified into logistics and media). Maersk's advantages (net cash, Gemini cost, terminal earnings) make it a survivor, not a share-taker. ZIM is the clear weak carrier: net debt of 3.5-3.9bn and a cost base about 300 per TEU above Hapag-Lloyd's.

What it means for the model: no share gains for Maersk in the downturn. Its relative earnings resilience comes from Terminals and cost, not from taking volume.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

F2519th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Competitors' attitude to competition: margin first or volume first, benign or loss-tolerant, and whether industry prices fall when volume falls

Judgement: The decisive competitors put volume and share first, are willing to sustain losses (the industry holds 26-30bn of net cash), and are not benign. Industry prices fall sharply whenever capacity runs ahead of demand, even with volume still growing (2016; 2023, when Maersk's EBITDA fell to 839m in Q4; 2025, when rates fell 14% year on year). Maersk and Hapag-Lloyd are the margin-first exceptions; MSC, CMA CGM and the state-backed carriers set the price in a loose market.

Reasoning and evidence: Jefferies says lines will "play chicken" with excess capacity rather than scrap modern vessels; DNB Carnegie says a cash-rich industry will prolong the downturn as lines defend share; Bernstein's view is that capacity discipline comes only from widespread losses. Orders grew 12% in Q1 2026 despite tariff threats, and the orderbook added about 1.6m TEU in Q3 2026 alone. Alliances (Ocean Alliance extended to 2032; Gemini) allow blank-sailing coordination, but only once losses force it.

What it means for the model: in 2027-2028, model rates falling until the weakest large carriers are loss-making at the operating level, i.e. below break-even for an average carrier, not merely below Maersk's 2026 level.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F2519th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable that attitude is, how it has changed over the last 18 months to three years, and whether a competitor would launch a "shark attack" on industry pricing

Judgement: The attitude has become less stable over the last 18-36 months. The 2M alliance dissolved (February 2025); MSC went stand-alone with aggressive pricing; THE Alliance became the Premier Alliance; carriers diverge sharply on Suez routing (CMA CGM returned first, the Premier Alliance and Evergreen still avoid the canal); and record ordering has continued despite everyone forecasting oversupply. MSC is the archetypal opportunistic, situation-dependent aggressor. A shark attack on pricing is plausible in 2027 as deliveries peak and the Red Sea route reopens. Its trigger would be a carrier seeking to fill new tonnage, not a perception of Maersk weakness (Maersk is financially strong).

Reasoning and evidence: Hapag-Lloyd's digest notes "smaller regional carriers undercutting major alliances with highly discounted transaction-specific spot rates" and MSC's "highly aggressive freight pricing". Maersk itself priced spot aggressively in early 2026 to secure Gemini utilisation. With contracts shortening, a price move transmits within weeks.

What it means for the model: include a downside scenario with a 2016-style price war in 2027-2028 (DNB Carnegie's expectation that rates converge toward the 2016 lows), with a meaningful probability attached.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

F2219th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The industry structure is concentrated but fundamentally unsupportive, because exit is blocked and incumbent expansion is easy. Competitive intensity equals entry minus exit (a Wisdom Library model). Here "entry" (incumbent fleet growth) runs at 4.6%, 7.4% and 13.2% a year for 2026-2028, while "exit" (scrapping) runs near 0.1%. Exit barriers are high: long-lived assets (now 25-year accounting lives), cash buffers, state and family ownership. The only support for pricing comes from outside the structure (disruption absorbing capacity), and that support is extremely fragile: it can disappear within weeks of a Red Sea settlement.

Reasoning and evidence: the value-chain margin map shows where profit accrues: shipyards (books full to 2029, about 34% gross margins at Yangzijiang), terminals (33% EBIT margin), asset-light forwarders (DSV 15.4% ROIC). Ocean carriage is the cyclical, capital-heavy stage with the lowest through-cycle returns (Maersk ROIC 5.7%, Hapag-Lloyd 4.6% in 2025).

What it means for the model: do not assume a structurally higher rate floor than in the last cycle. Assume the 2028 trough is set by cost (break-even of the marginal carrier), not by industry structure.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?F5015th of 19full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

B605th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Substitutes are a low and stable threat. China-Europe rail and the Trans-Caspian corridor gain share during sea disruptions but remain small and capacity-constrained. Air freight takes only the highest-value goods (and Maersk captures part of it through Logistics & Services Air, +20% in Q1 2026). Nearshoring and reshoring are slow, and tariffs have so far diverted sourcing to other Asian countries rather than home. In practice the main "substitute" is a shipper reducing safety stock or localising standardised inputs, which some consumer goods firms are doing at the margin.

What it means for the model: no substitution effect beyond the 2-4% demand range.

Memo topic 3, : the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

F4516th of 198 answers
Judgement45C-4145C-4255C-4345C-4460C-4540C-4640C-4738
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

F4516th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Maersk's advantages are real in two places and weak elsewhere. APM Terminals is a genuine, widening moat with pricing power and returns well above the cost of capital, and it will hold its value through the shipping cycle. Gemini gives Maersk a measurable cost and reliability edge in Ocean, but the edge is shared with Hapag-Lloyd, will be competed away to customers as capacity loosens, and lowers the depth of Maersk's trough rather than raising its peak. The balance sheet gives staying power. Ocean scale, brand premium and the integrated-logistics model do not constitute moats; the last may be a net negative through channel conflict. For the model this means Ocean is a price-taker with a modestly better cost line than peers, Terminals is a compounding infrastructure asset, and Logistics & Services is a low-margin service business with modest upside. Competitive advantage does not protect the group from the cycle; it determines who survives it best, and Maersk is among the survivors.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

F4516th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Maersk has one genuine moat (APM Terminals), one real but shared and replicable cost advantage (the Gemini network), one structural financial advantage (its balance sheet, i.e. its capacity to suffer), and several weak or negative "advantages" (Ocean scale, brand premium, integrated logistics). The moat sits in a business that is about 10% of revenue but a much larger share of trough earnings (about 2bn of EBITDA against consensus group EBITDA of 7.5bn in FY2028).

Advantage by advantage:

  • Terminals (regulatory and production-based: long concessions, gateway control, scale): strong and widening. EBIT margin 33.2% and ROIC 15.7% in Q1 2026 (16.1% in 2025) against a 9% hurdle; revenue per move +7.1% in Q2 2026; utilisation 88-89%; capacity +30% through a 3bn programme; new concessions (Da Nang, Jeddah). 63% of revenue comes from third-party carriers, which diversifies it but leaves it exposed if rival carriers build their own terminal networks (Hapag-Lloyd's terminals arm targets 30+ terminals by 2030; CMA CGM is expanding).
  • Gemini network (production and cost, plus reliability for customers): moderate, widened in 2025-2026, likely to plateau. About 90% schedule reliability against 50-60% for the industry; Ocean unit cost at fixed bunker −7%; 950m of benefits; 40% fewer port calls; volume funnelled into Maersk's own hubs. But it is shared with Hapag-Lloyd, it can be copied by any alliance over time, and it has single-point-of-failure risk at the hubs (Tanjung Pelepas, Salalah, Tangier Med).
  • Balance sheet (financial capacity to suffer): strong. Net cash excluding leases of 13.7bn, investment-grade ratings. It lets Maersk keep investing and distributing through a downturn that will hurt ZIM-like carriers, but it is matched by the industry's own cash (26-30bn), so it is not unique.
  • Brand and service premium (customer-related): weak to moderate, narrowing in downturns. Experts cite a 5-10% premium only when reliability clearly outperforms; most standard customers buy the cheapest rate.
  • Ocean scale: weak and narrowing. Share fell from about 19-20% to 13.6%; MSC is larger; in an unbounded global market, scale economies flatten beyond minimum efficient scale (Wisdom Library: "scale needs a bounded market").
  • Integrated logistics (bundling): weak, possibly negative. It produces channel conflict with forwarders, sub-WACC returns and unintegrated IT. The bundling linkage claimed (end-to-end supply chains) fails the "linked businesses" test, because a well-funded rival can attack each piece on its own (DSV in forwarding, specialists in contract logistics).
  • Knowledge and digital (tracking, customs tools, Trade & Tariff Studio): moderate, an operating basic rather than a moat.

What it means for the model: value and model Terminals as the durable profit pool (EBITDA of about 2.0-2.1bn growing about 3-6% a year); give Ocean a small cost advantage over non-Gemini peers but no pricing advantage; treat Logistics & Services as a low-margin service business.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

C-559th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: By revenue Maersk is the largest listed company in its peer set (53.99bn in 2025, against DSV about 37bn, COSCO about 32bn, Kuehne+Nagel about 27.5bn, Hapag-Lloyd about 21bn). It operates about 700 ships (4.6-4.7m TEU), about 60 terminals, about 100,000 employees, and serves 100,000+ customers in 130 countries; its top 200 customers are about 46% of group revenue. Scale confers network breadth (500+ ports), the ability to build Gemini on its own terms, a large owned terminal portfolio, procurement and digital spend, and balance-sheet staying power. It does not confer price leadership in Ocean (MSC is about 55% larger by capacity), and it adds complexity: former executives call the matrix "too big, too complex, and too slow".

What it means for the model: scale supports Maersk's survival and its cost position, not its margins. No scale-driven margin expansion should be modelled.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D6011th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Maersk is demonstrably not inferior, and it leads on schedule reliability and digital visibility, the attributes beneficial cargo owners value in disrupted markets. Gemini delivered about 90% reliability against an industry average of 50-60% and an alliance average of about 62%; experts rank Maersk the number-one carrier for on-time arrivals; Sea-Intelligence ranked Gemini partner Hapag-Lloyd the most reliable carrier in March 2026. Its landbridge solutions kept Gulf customers supplied during the Hormuz disruption. There are some contrary data points: containers dropped at intermediate ports (March 2026), shippers slow to adapt to the new Gemini routes, some customers cutting allocations by up to 30%, inconsistent personal customer support, and mid-market clients rejecting complex end-to-end packages.

What it means for the model: customer utility supports Maersk's volume share among large beneficial cargo owners and should keep volume growth near the market's. It does not give pricing power in an oversupplied market, because the utility edge (reliability) loses value when capacity is abundant and every carrier has space.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

D4013th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent the company has and uses demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative customer utility, shown by evidence of application

Judgement: In Ocean, Maersk has no demonstrable pricing power independent of the market. Its realised rates track market indices up and down (−14% year on year in Q1 2026 when the market was loose; +22% in Q2 when it was tight), and it priced spot aggressively in early 2026 to fill Gemini. The applied evidence of "power" (recovering about 500m a month of Cape costs through surcharges; emergency bunker surcharges; customers keeping to 2025 contract rates) works only while capacity is tight. The one sign of relative strength is that Q1 2026 loaded rates fell 14.3% year on year against a 32% fall in the Drewry World Container Index, a cushion provided by contract mix and Gemini service. In Terminals, Maersk has demonstrable pricing power: revenue per move +6.8% in 2025 (FX-adjusted) and +7.1% in Q2 2026, at record margins, with volumes still growing (+4.3% in Q1 2026).

What it means for the model: Ocean realised rates should fall somewhat less than spot indices in a downturn (contract mix of about 44%, a short lag), but in the same direction and of similar order. Terminals price growth of about 3-6% a year continues.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F4016th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent the company shares its scale economies with customers

Judgement: Not deliberately. Maersk's messaging and strategy emphasise reliability, end-to-end service and decarbonisation, not passing cost savings on to customers. Gemini's savings (950m) were retained in the tight 2026 market. In practice they will be competed away to customers when the market loosens, which is sharing by force rather than by design. Clerc's stated Ocean priorities ("reliable customer service, the lowest possible unit cost, growing volumes in line with the market") are a cost-leadership stance, not a Costco-style share-the-savings model.

What it means for the model: assume Gemini cost savings protect Maersk's margin relative to peers but do not expand its absolute margins in 2027-2028; most of the cost gain passes to customers through lower rates.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

F3818th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: The mechanical forces are mostly against Maersk in Ocean and for it in Terminals. In Ocean, each period of the coming cycle erodes position (competitors' newbuilds take share; scrapping is too low to rebalance). Gemini's savings were a step change, not a compounding flywheel, so standing still requires running (the "Red Queen" effect). In Terminals, gravity helps: since 2019 terminal capacity at the top ten hubs has grown 21% against trade growth of 28%, concessions run for decades, and hub-and-spoke funnelling adds volume to Maersk-owned hubs (+26% year on year Ocean volume into own terminals in Q1 2026). In Logistics & Services there is no gravity; progress needs heroic execution, which the record (repeated misses of the 10% growth and 6% margin targets) says is hard.

What it means for the model: compound Terminals; hold Ocean relative cost flat after the Gemini step; do not build progressive Logistics & Services margin gains beyond about 6%.

4b

Management quality

F4716th of 1912 answers
Judgement47C-5548C-5640C-5750C-57b50C-5865C-5932C-6055C-6150C-6255C-6355C-6442
Group judgement

Company - Management

F4716th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is strong at running the network and weak at deploying capital. Clerc's team delivered the industry's standout operational change (Gemini), handles disruption well, guides conservatively and communicates the earnings mechanics clearly. The same leadership created a costly integrator strategy, spent more than 8bn on logistics acquisitions at peak prices, lost about six points of Ocean share, used an accounting change to lift reported EBIT, and has now ordered 500k TEU into the industry's largest supply wave without a convincing rationale. The new logistics-heavy hires (CFO Erni, Solutions head Plantaz) improve the odds in Logistics & Services, but the culture is strained and the bench is thin. The family owner provides stability and generous distributions, not discipline. For the model: trust the cost and operations lines and the conservatism of formal guidance; discount strategic targets; assume capex stays high and distributions follow earnings down.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

F4814th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How big the company's ambition or vision is, particularly against peers

Judgement: The ambition is large in vision and modest in execution intensity. The vision is to be the global end-to-end container logistics integrator (since 2016), to reach net zero by 2040 (a decade ahead of the industry), and to set a new reliability standard in Ocean. But the Ocean growth ambition was deliberately shrunk for years (a fleet cap of 4.1-4.3m TEU; share falling from about 19-20% to 13.6%) and is only now reversing with the fleet order. Against peers: MSC and CMA CGM are more ambitious on scale and share; DSV is more ambitious and more credible in logistics (DKK 9bn of Schenker synergies plus DKK 9bn of AI and network savings by 2030); Hapag-Lloyd's Strategy 2030 has more explicit, measurable targets (20% unit-cost reduction, 30+ terminals, NPS above 50). Maersk ranks in the middle: big words, a mixed record, few hard targets.

What it means for the model: management will keep investing for the vision (terminals, logistics capability, green fleet) through the downturn; there is no aggressive growth push in Ocean before 2029.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F4018th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart the strategy is: right and bold priorities, widening the moat, growth as a priority, and adaptability to a range of environments

Judgement: The strategy is mixed. Two parts are right and bold (Terminals and Gemini), one is questionable and costly (the integrator model), and the newest move (fleet growth) is poorly timed and poorly explained. Only the Terminals priority clearly widens a moat over the mid term.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Right and bold: Gemini (leaving the 2M alliance, a hub-and-spoke design, 90% reliability, 950m of benefits) is the most significant operational achievement in the industry in years. The Terminals expansion targets the highest-return part of the value chain.
  • Questionable: the integrator strategy has consumed more than 8bn of capital at sub-WACC returns, turned forwarders (up to 50% of Ocean volume) into competitors, and missed its targets for a decade. Its claimed benefit, that diversification cushions Ocean, is real but small (about 4bn of non-Ocean EBITDA against an Ocean swing of 5-10bn). CMA CGM (CEVA run at arm's length) and Hapag-Lloyd (a "pure play plus" model that partners with forwarders) chose differently and avoided the channel conflict.
  • Fleet pivot: if the 26 dual-fuel ships were ordered to arrive after the trough (2029-2030) and replace aging tonnage at falling yard prices, it could prove counter-cyclically smart. Ordered in 2026, near peak newbuild prices (about 46% above pre-COVID) and without a clear rationale, it looks pro-cyclical. Bernstein: management "often claims terminal constraints blunt the usability of global vessel growth", so "investing heavily in vessels seems illogical".
  • Adaptability: high in operations (Hormuz landbridges, Cape routing, surcharge mechanisms); low in strategic posture (the integrator model is fixed).

What it means for the model: strategic choices raise capex and keep Logistics & Services margins below best-in-class through 2028; they do not change the Ocean cycle.

C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

F5017th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Vincent Clerc is a strong operator and a weak capital allocator: an enabler in network and operations, not in capital deployment. Judged by actions, his record splits cleanly.

Positive actions: exiting the 2M alliance and building Gemini with Hapag-Lloyd quickly (launched February 2025; about 90% reliability in year one; 950m of benefits against an 820m-1.1bn target); suspending the 3bn-a-year buyback at once in February 2024 when Red Sea uncertainty rose, then reinstating 2bn in February 2025 (at roughly half today's price, in hindsight well timed); the 2023 restructuring (headcount from 110,000 to 100,000; capacity withdrawn); the Svitzer demerger; operational agility through Red Sea, Hormuz and congestion; consistent, conservative guidance.

Negative actions: as CEO of Ocean & Logistics (2019-2022) he drove 7.7-8bn of pro-cyclical logistics acquisitions (LF Logistics at 3.6bn, Pilot at 1.6bn) that remain unintegrated and below the cost of capital; market share fell from about 20% to 13.6% under his divisional and group leadership; the January 2026 vessel-life change lifted reported EBIT by about 700m; he dismisses the market-share loss ("no correlation" between fleet size and margin) and then ordered 500k TEU; he has made no open-market share purchases (his equity build-up is all incentive vesting).

Peer comparison: Rolf Habben Jansen at Hapag-Lloyd (an external hire in 2014) has a cleaner capital-allocation record (CSAV and UASC synergies delivered ahead of schedule). Jens Lund at DSV has the stronger M&A integration record.

What it means for the model: trust management on operations, cost and conservative guidance; be sceptical of capital-allocation choices (capex, M&A, timing of returns).

C-57b

The Investment Analyst's heading How high a priority hiring well is for the chief executive, and how great the leadership team he has built

Against this, the bench has thinned: the departures of Henriette Hallberg Thygesen (2024), the Head of Europe (2025), Narin Phol and Susana Elvira (2026); a two-person Executive Board; no obvious internal CEO successor; Danish exit-tax rules impeding senior international hires; MSC's 2020 poaching of COO Søren Toft as a precedent. The team is good and getting better in logistics finance and operations, but thin and in flux.

What it means for the model: better odds that Logistics & Services and Solutions margins improve from 2027; elevated key-person risk on Clerc.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

B655th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Well resourced and invested where it matters for resilience. Cash and deposits were 18.4bn at Q1 2026; ratings are investment grade; capex runs at 10-11bn over 2026-2027 (fleet renewal, dual-fuel, terminal automation and capacity); green-fuel offtakes are secured (over half of 2027 dual-fuel needs, 500k tonnes a year from Goldwind); 150 vessels were retrofitted for efficiency in 2025, with 200 chartered vessels in a retrofit programme; digital tools (Trade & Tariff Studio, Altana product passports). The under-resourced areas are the integration of acquired logistics platforms (4PL and supply-chain software delayed; Pilot and LF on stand-alone systems) and warehouse utilisation in Solutions. The business is built for resilience, but resilience here means survival, not earnings stability.

What it means for the model: no under-investment risk; capex stays high (about 5bn a year), and the cost of resilience is low free cash flow.

C-60

How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

Judgement: Clear and helpful on the numbers and the transmission mechanism, less so on strategy. C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

C-62

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

C-559th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: On balance Maersk is moderately shareholder-friendly, because the controlling family's interests (long-term value and cash distributions) mostly coincide with minority interests. Governance scaffolding is solid in compliance and audit, and weak in independence and transparency. The family puts its own strategic vision (the integrator) ahead of near-term shareholder returns, and minorities have no leverage.

Evidence for shareholders first:

  • Very large cash returns at the peak: dividends per share of 397 (2021) and 607 (2022), and the combined extraordinary dividend of DKK 4,300 a share (10.9bn) in 2023. The share count fell 27% between 2019 and 2025.
  • The family participates pro rata in buybacks (no creeping control; Maersk bought 555 shares from the Foundation in the second phase).
  • Portfolio simplification released value (oil sale, drilling demerger, Svitzer).
  • Prudent buyback suspension in 2024; investment-grade balance sheet.
  • Incentives on segment ROIC (capital efficiency) rather than EPS; executives must hold shares worth 200% of salary.
  • A fully independent Audit Committee, a strong compliance framework (70+ professionals, whistleblowing since 2011), no political donations.

Evidence against:

  • A controlled company: A.P. Møller Holding holds 51.5% of votes on 41.5% of capital; the Chair is its CEO; the Nomination and Remuneration Committees have low independence.
  • The more than 8bn of logistics M&A at peak multiples (empire-building rather than returns).
  • The vessel-life change managing reported profit.
  • PwC as auditor for fifteen years; individual board shareholdings not disclosed.
  • No open-market insider buying; relative TSR dropped from the long-term plan.

Why: a multi-generational family owner optimises for the long-term survival and strategic relevance of the group, which aligns with minorities on prudence and distributions but not on capital-allocation discipline in strategic projects.

What it means for the model: dividends follow the 30-50% payout reliably; buybacks are flexible and will be cut in a downturn; expect no shareholder-driven change of strategy.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

F4215th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent the company is an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital

Judgement: Not an enlightened allocator. Maersk is a generous distributor with a mixed timing record and a poor record on large capital deployments.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Distributions: big and real (see C-63). But buybacks were heaviest in the peak-cash years, at high prices, and were suspended in February 2024, when the valuation was low (the B share closed 2024 at DKK 11,905, about half today's price). The 2025 reinstatement at about half today's price was well timed. Repurchase timing follows cash availability, not valuation: it is not counter-cyclical by design.
  • Deployment: logistics M&A of more than 8bn, sub-WACC and unintegrated; a fleet order of about 500k TEU at elevated yard prices into a supply wave; Terminals investment at about 16% ROIC (the one high-return use, and the smallest).
  • Return on new invested capital overall: low; group ROIC of 5.7% in 2025 is below the 7.5% target and the cost of capital.

What it means for the model: assume capital deployment continues at about 5bn a year regardless of the cycle, distributions track earnings with a lag, and no value creation from capital allocation in the base case.

4c

Financial profile

F3218th of 198 answers
Judgement32C-4920C-5030C-5128C-5235C-5330C-5430C-7675
Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

F3218th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Maersk's economic strength is a strong balance sheet wrapped around weak, cyclical earnings power. Returns are below the cost of capital through the cycle (ROIC 5.7% in 2025; consensus implies about 1-3% in 2027-2028); cash conversion is low once lease payments are counted; EPS is a pure function of freight rates; and the earnings base is becoming increasingly accounting-driven (the vessel-life benefit is about 88% of FY2028 consensus EBIT). Operating leverage is extreme (100 per FFE = 1.3bn of EBIT), giving a FY2027 EBIT cone of roughly −3bn to +8bn. Against this stand a net-cash balance sheet with no gearing, a Terminals business earning about 16% ROIC that compounds, and a cost base with 1-2bn of reducible discretionary cost. For the model: rates drive everything; show the depreciation change separately; count leases as an operating cash cost; expect the balance sheet to absorb two years of negative company-basis FCF without strain.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

F2019th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: EPS growth is entirely a function of freight rates and is not durable. On consensus (brokers' basis), EPS goes +170% in FY2026 (181.63 → 490.00) on revenue +18%, then −75% in FY2027 (124.69) on revenue −7.5%, then −50% in FY2028 (62.09) on revenue −2.6%. History is as volatile: FactSet's diluted EPS before unusual items went from 0.78 (2019) to 1,612 (2022) to 233 (2023), 397 (2024) and 148 (2025). Over 2019-2025, revenue grew about 5.6% a year (38.9bn to 54.0bn, including logistics acquisitions), while EPS was dominated by the pandemic cycle. Two non-operating supports matter: the share count fell 27% (2019-2025) and continues to fall about 2% a year; and the vessel-life change adds about 700m of pre-tax profit a year, about USD 48 a share, taxed lightly under tonnage tax.

Key insight: that 48 a share is about 77% of consensus FY2028 EPS (62.09). Excluding the accounting change, consensus FY2028 EPS is about 14. The FY2028 consensus is close to break-even at the economic level.

What it means for the model: build EPS from the rate-driven EBIT path; show the vessel-life depreciation benefit separately; expect EPS to fall about 70-90% from FY2026 to the trough, with a wide cone (FY2028 broker estimates range from −383 to +294).

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F3019th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The scope is limited. Only Terminals can absorb capital at a high return (ROIC about 16% against a 9% hurdle), and its programme is about 3bn over several years, roughly 0.6-1bn a year, under 20% of group capex of about 5bn a year. The rest of the capex goes to fleet renewal (dual-fuel newbuilds ordered near peak prices, earning below the cost of capital through the cycle) and logistics assets with sub-WACC returns. The M&A record is poor. So reinvestment does not compound value; at best it maintains the franchise.

What it means for the model and the fair multiple: the fair valuation lens is book value or invested capital, not earnings growth. The through-cycle fair multiple is below book for Ocean (sell-side terminal EV/invested capital of 0.4-0.7x), with a premium infrastructure multiple only for Terminals. Mid- and long-term potential is a cyclical recovery after 2029, not structural growth.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

F2817th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Returns are low through the cycle and very low at the trough. Group ROIC was 5.7% in 2025 (12.3% in 2024), against a long-term target above 7.5%. On consensus, return on equity is roughly 12% in FY2026, 2-3% in FY2027 and about 1-1.5% in FY2028 (net profit of 7.36bn, 1.63bn and 0.81bn on equity of about 60bn). By segment: Terminals 15.7-16.1% ROIC (excellent), Logistics & Services below its cost of capital since 2021, Ocean deeply cyclical. Post-tax and pre-tax returns are nearly the same because of the tonnage tax. Tangible returns are slightly higher than reported (logistics acquisitions created goodwill; the amount is not in the files). Return on new invested capital in the fleet is low. Peers: DSV 15.4% and Kuehne+Nagel double-digit (asset-light); COSCO 14.1%; Hapag-Lloyd 4.6% (2025).

What it means for the model: ROIC falls to about 1-3% in 2027-2028 on consensus; equity stays about flat (dividends roughly equal to earnings); the ROIC target of more than 7.5% through the cycle will not be met over 2025-2028.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F3518th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Cash conversion looks high on FactSet's definition and is low on the economically correct one. FactSet's free cash flow (operating cash flow less capex, before leases) was 114% of net income in 2024 and 130% in 2025. But Maersk runs about 43% of its fleet on charter, and lease repayments (about 1.18bn in Q1 2026, roughly 4.5-5bn a year) are a real operating cost of the fleet. After leases, the company's own FCF was −874m in Q1 2026 and +549m in Q2 2026, and its FY2026 guidance is only "above zero" on consensus net profit of about 7.4bn. Conversion on the company basis is therefore near zero even in a peak year, because capex (about 5.4bn) plus leases (about 4.7bn) absorbs about 10bn a year. Working capital is structurally negative (payables exceed receivables plus inventories) but swings against Maersk when rates and oil rise (H1 2026 cash conversion 69% against 92%; a −913m working-capital outflow in Q1 2026 from bunker inventory values and receivables) and releases cash when they fall, a counter-cyclical cushion for 2027.

What it means for the model: model operating cash flow at about EBITDA less tax, plus or minus working capital (a release in 2027 as rates and bunker fall); capex of about 5bn; lease repayments of about 4.5-5bn; company-basis FCF negative in FY2027-2028 (Morgan Stanley: negative from 2027; consensus FactSet FCF of 3.0bn for FY2027 and 1.1bn for FY2028 becomes roughly −1.5bn to −2bn and −3.5bn to −4bn respectively after leases; our estimate).

C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

F3018th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Margins are at a cyclical high and about to fall to cycle lows. Gross margin is not a meaningful measure here (FactSet gross margin was 4.9% in 2025). EBITDA margin on the brokers' basis: 18.8% (2023), 21.9% (2024), 17.7% (2025), then consensus 22.0% (FY2026), 14.8% (FY2027), 13.1% (FY2028). FactSet's adjusted EBIT margin: 6.0% (2023), 10.5% (2024), 4.6% (2025), then consensus about 12.8%, 3.5% and 1.4%. The FY2028 consensus EBIT margin is below 2019's 4.0% even with the 700m depreciation benefit. By segment: Terminals about 33% EBIT margin (stable); Logistics & Services 5.1% (nine consecutive quarters of year-on-year improvement; Forwarding 6.4%, Landside 6.3%, Solutions 1.7%); Ocean −2.3% (Q1 2026) to about 8.9% (Q2 2026). Peer comparison for FY2027-2028 consensus: Hapag-Lloyd 1% and −1% EBIT margin, Maersk 3% and 1%. Maersk's margin premium comes from Terminals and Logistics & Services, not from Ocean.

What it means for the model: group EBITDA margin of about 13-15% at the trough, with segment margins: Terminals about 33-35% (EBIT), Logistics & Services about 5-6%, Ocean EBIT margin negative at the trough.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F3016th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How clean the earnings base is: the gap between adjusted and reported, one-offs, capitalised costs, and tax or other leakage

Judgement: The earnings base is only moderately clean, and it gets less clean as operating profit falls. The single largest issue is the vessel useful-life extension (20 to 25 years from 1 January 2026). It lowers depreciation by about 700m a year (about 175m a quarter). It is non-cash and continuing, and it is equal to about 9% of FY2026 consensus EBIT, about 34% of FY2027 and about 88% of FY2028. It also keeps fleet book values higher, which raises impairment risk in a downturn. Other issues:

  • "Underlying" measures exclude gains, impairments and restructuring (77m of restructuring in Q1 2026).
  • The EPS basis gap is large: for FY2025, brokers' EPS was 181.63, reported basic 178.99 and FactSet adjusted diluted 148.17, a 20% spread.
  • Financial items include FX and derivative swings (FX translation +595m in 2025 against −447m in 2024; trading derivative gains of 510m in 2025).
  • Minorities leak earnings in weak quarters (Q1 2026 net profit to shareholders of 53m against pre-tax profit of 292m).
  • IFRS 16 moves about 4.5-5bn a year of charter cost below EBITDA, which flatters EBITDA and EV/EBITDA against cash reality.
  • Tax is low and fairly clean under the tonnage regime (3-15% reported in 2021-2025).
  • No capitalised-cost issue was flagged.

What it means for the model: show EBIT both with and without the depreciation benefit; model minorities explicitly (terminal joint ventures); use company-basis FCF after leases; assume a tax rate of about 10-15%.

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

B754th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Financial leverage is negligible and very comfortable. Maersk had net cash of about 1.0-1.5bn including 12.2bn of lease liabilities at Q2 2026 (13.7bn excluding leases), ratings of BBB+/Baa1, and net debt to EBITDA of about −0.1x. Debt does not amplify EPS; if anything, interest income on about 18.5bn of cash cushions earnings at the trough. Even with company-basis FCF negative in 2027-2028 and continued dividends and buybacks, net cash excluding leases would remain positive through 2028 (our estimate: a decline to roughly 3-8bn). The balance sheet is a buffer, but also a temptation: it funded the pro-cyclical logistics M&A and now funds the fleet order.

What it means for the model: no refinancing or covenant risk; net financial items roughly neutral; the risk to watch is that the cash buffer is spent on capex and distributions at the peak rather than kept for the trough.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

Memo topic 5, : the Rater's score

5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1755C-4540P-0425
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?C+555th of 19full answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces
C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?D4013th of 19full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F2516th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: In Ocean, price is the dominant and negative contributor over the horizon. The average loaded rate adds about +24% to Ocean freight revenue in FY2026 (2,746 against about 2,210 in FY2025), then subtracts about 24% in FY2027 (to 2,075) and 8% in FY2028 (to 1,905), before small positive contributions in FY2029 (+3%) and FY2030 (+5%). The Ocean EBITDA margin follows: 20.4% (FY2026), 11.1%, 7.5%, 8.9% and 11.4%. Maersk has no pricing power of its own in Ocean in a loose market: no reliability premium beyond niche cargo, short contracts that transmit spot within a quarter, and a forwarder channel (up to 50% of volume) that it has turned into a rival. The only real pricing power in the group is in Terminals, where tariff growth of 3-5% a year is protected by indexed concessions, though about one year of it is absorbed in 2027 as congestion-related storage income fades. Logistics & Services pricing is cost-plus and adds nothing to margin.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Contract structure. About 44-45% of Ocean volume is on long-term rate products (45% in Q4 2025; 75% at the Q3 2024 peak, when shippers locked in space), with quarterly and monthly terms replacing annual ones. Hapag-Lloyd contracts 50-65%, which is why its downturn will be slower and shallower; ZIM's 65% Transpacific spot exposure makes it faster. Customer interviews (19 October 2026) say most customers "will easily move volume" and ask why they should support contract rates above spot.
  • No sustained premium. The correlation between schedule reliability and spot rates is -0.81 (Carnegie, 4 November 2024): reliability improves when capacity is loose, which is exactly when nobody pays for it. Gemini's 90% reliability earns a premium of up to 10% only from specialised shippers (a chemical buyer, 21 October 2026); an expert says about 95% of standard customers buy the cheapest rate.
  • The 2027 contract season. DNB Carnegie cites industry expectations of contract declines of up to 30% against 2025 levels. We model the Asia-Europe season (December to February) settling into a falling spot market and the Transpacific season (spring) following, so that the contract book offers no protection after Q2 2027.
  • Price within the quarterly path. Realised loaded rates: Q3 2026 about 3,230 (contract repricing catches up with Q2 spot), Q4 2026 about 2,880, Q1 2027 about 2,400, Q2 2027 about 2,100, Q3 2027 about 1,950, Q4 2027 about 1,860. Q1 2027 group revenue is still about +7% year on year because Q1 2026 was priced at 2,081; the first year-on-year decline comes in Q2 2027 (about -16%) and the steepest in Q3 2027 (about -27%).
  • The fuel-adjusted test of the trough. Our FY2028 rate of 1,905 less bunker recovery of about 490 gives a fuel-adjusted rate of about 1,400 per FFE, below the 2016 trough (1,795 less about 300 of bunker, about 1,500) in nominal terms, even though costs have inflated since. That is deliberate: Q1 2026 already showed base rates near this level (2,081 less 437 of bunker less roughly 300 of Cape surcharges, about 1,350) with Ocean roughly at break-even, in a market that was tighter than 2028 will be. We judge the 2028 trough sits at the cash cost of the marginal carrier: Maersk, with a 3-7% Gemini cost advantage, keeps Ocean EBITDA positive (about 165 per FFE) but Ocean EBIT negative.
  • Carbon and fuel pass-through. Formulaic surcharges (the Fossil Fuel Fee replaced the bunker adjustment factor in Q1 2026) recover EU ETS and fuel costs in tight markets; Morgan Stanley notes a loose market may force absorption. We assume 80-90% recovery in 2027 and about 70% in 2028, a cost of about 0.1-0.2bn a year.
  • Terminals. Revenue per move +7.7% like for like in Q2 2026 (tariffs and storage) against labour inflation of 7.4%; concessions average 22 years and include indexation (ICTSI disclosure). Bernstein warns that storage and detention income inflated by the 4.3m TEU of congestion will normalise. Terminals EBITDA: 2.17bn (FY2026), 2.15bn, 2.25bn, 2.40bn, 2.55bn; margin about 37-39% of segment revenue.
  • Logistics & Services. Forwarders' gross profit per unit shows no correlation with sea freight rates (Bernstein, 13 October 2026). Logistics revenue dips about 3% in FY2027 as freight pass-through falls, while its margin improves.

What it means for the model: treat the Ocean rate as an exogenous market path with a one-quarter lag; take Terminals tariffs at +3-5% a year with a flat FY2027; give Logistics & Services no price contribution to margin.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7520P-0120
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F2019th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Operating leverage is extreme, and it is almost entirely price leverage, not volume leverage. A freight-rate change flows nearly one-for-one to EBIT: 100 per FFE on the average loaded rate is about 1.3bn of EBIT, about 3% of FY2026 consensus Ocean revenue (42.4bn). So a 1% change in the average Ocean rate moves EBIT by about 0.35bn, about 4% of FY2026 consensus EBIT and about 17% of FY2027 consensus EBIT. Morgan Stanley puts a 5% rate change at about 1.7bn of EBITDA. Volume leverage is small: the stated sensitivity is about 10m of EBIT per 100k FFE, because most costs scale with sailings or boxes.

Cost structure behind it (Ocean, Q1 2026 split): terminal handling 39% (variable per box), network costs excluding bunker 27% (vessel, charter and crew: fixed per sailing), bunker 19% (variable per sailing, partly passed through), SG&A 9% (fixed), plus group depreciation of about 6.3-6.5bn a year (fixed). Capacity on the water is fixed in the short term; revenue per box is not.

Range of outcomes: if the FY2027 average loaded rate falls 300-500 per FFE from about 2,600-2,800 in FY2026, group EBIT falls by about 4-6.5bn, from about 7.5-8bn to about 1.5-3.5bn. Consensus FY2027 EBIT of 2.05bn sits inside that range. A 2016-style collapse (another 300-500 lower) would push group EBIT to about −1bn to −3bn, while a renewed disruption that holds 2026 rates would keep FY2027 EBIT at about 6-8bn. The plausible FY2027 cone is therefore about −3bn to +8bn of EBIT, one of the widest relative to market value among European large caps.

What it means for the model: build scenarios on the rate, not on volume. A rate path assumption is the model.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

F2016th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: The operating leverage that matters is price leverage on a cost base that is fixed per sailing, sitting on top of a depreciation charge of 6.25-7.0bn a year. In the downturn it shapes the line items in three ways. First, a large share of the revenue fall is pass-through (surcharges and bunker recovery leaving with their costs), so revenue falls by much more than its profit content: group revenue falls 16.6% in FY2027 while the base-rate erosion that hits profit is about 5bn. Second, that base-rate erosion drops almost one-for-one to EBITDA, so EBITDA falls 40% in FY2027 and EBIT falls 79%. Third, because depreciation is about 12-13% of revenue in FY2027-FY2029 and the group EBITDA margin is only 12-14%, group EBIT sits close to zero for three years, and small changes in the rate decide the sign of earnings. The speed of the decline will resemble 2023; its depth, in fuel-adjusted terms, will resemble 2016; its length (three years near the trough) will be longer than either.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Where the leverage comes from. Ocean's disclosed cost split (Q1 2026) is terminal handling 39% (variable per box), network costs excluding bunker 27% (fixed per sailing), bunker 19% (variable per sailing, recovered through the Fossil Fuel Fee), SG&A 9% (fixed). Most costs scale with the network deployed, not with the price charged. The rate is set by the market. So the Ocean margin is the rate minus a near-fixed unit cost; the AlphaSense report confirms the precedents: in 2016 Maersk Line's unit cost fell 11.5% (mostly bunker) while the loaded rate fell 18.7% to 1,795 per FFE, and the business swung from a 1.3bn profit to a 155m underlying loss; in 2023 Ocean EBIT fell from 29.2bn to 2.2bn, with Q4 2023 at -920m (-12.8% margin).
  • The sensitivity. 100 per FFE on the average loaded rate is about 1.36bn of annual EBITDA and EBIT in FY2027 (13.6m FFE). Under tonnage tax that is almost untaxed, so it is worth about USD 95 per share, more than twice our FY2027 EPS of 43. This is the single most important fact for the predictability work in the third set.
  • The FY2026-to-FY2027 Ocean EBITDA bridge (8.6bn to 3.6bn). The average loaded rate falls 671 per FFE (-24%), about 9.1bn of freight revenue. Of that, about 290 per FFE (about 3.9bn) is Cape-diversion surcharges and bunker recovery that leave with the matching costs (the Cape network cost and lower bunker burn on the Suez route). Other Ocean revenue (hub and related income, much of it congestion-linked) falls about 108 per FFE (about 1.5bn) with roughly 0.7bn of matching cost. The remainder, about 380 per FFE of base-rate erosion (about 5.2bn), drops straight to EBITDA, partly offset by about 0.6bn of congestion-cost relief and self-help, less about 0.4bn of handling, labour and carbon cost inflation. Net: about -5.0bn.
  • What makes the decline fast. Contracts are short (44-45% of volume on contract, on quarterly or monthly terms), so realised rates follow spot within about one quarter. Q1 2026 showed the cushion is partial and brief: realised rates -14% year on year against -32% for the Drewry index, followed by catch-up.
  • Line items against the last four to six years:
Line FY2021 FY2022 FY2023 FY2024 FY2025 FY2026E FY2027E FY2028E FY2029E FY2030E
Revenue (bn) 61.8 81.5 51.1 55.5 54.0 62.8 52.4 51.5 54.2 58.1
Revenue growth +55.5% +31.9% -37.4% +8.7% -2.7% +16.4% -16.6% -1.9% +5.3% +7.1%
EBITDA margin 38.7% 44.9% 18.8% 21.9% 17.7% 19.7% 14.2% 12.3% 13.2% 14.6%
EBIT margin (incl. JVs) 30.7% 37.3% 6.0% 10.5% 4.6% 10.3% 2.6% 0.3% 1.5% 3.4%

(History: FY2023-FY2025 EBITDA margins on the brokers' basis; earlier years FactSet operating; EBIT margins FactSet adjusted.) The FY2028 EBIT margin of 0.3% is below every year in the table and below 2019's 4.0%, even though it contains the 0.7bn vessel-life depreciation benefit.

  • The degree of leverage, year to year. FY2027 against FY2026: revenue -16.6%, EBITDA -40%, EBIT -79%, EPS -88%. For comparison, 2022-to-2023: revenue -37%, EBITDA -75%, EBIT -90%; 2024-to-2025: revenue -2.7%, EBITDA -23% (FactSet operating), EBIT -57%. The ratio of EBITDA change to revenue change looks lower in FY2027 (2.4x) than in 2025 (8.5x) only because so much of the FY2027 revenue fall is pass-through. Do not read the lower ratio as lower risk.
  • Terminals and Logistics & Services carry the opposite, milder leverage. Terminals' revenue per move rose 7.1% in Q2 2026 against cost per move +5%, so leverage is positive while utilisation stays at 88-89%; storage and demurrage income tied to congestion fades in 2027, which is why Terminals EBITDA is flat in FY2027 (2.15bn) before growing 5-7% a year. In Logistics & Services, Solutions' fixed warehouse costs ("whitespace") depressed its margin to 1.7%; as new sites fill, this is positive operating leverage of about 0.1-0.15bn by 2028.
  • The cone. FY2027 average loaded rate of 1,800-2,600 per FFE spans group EBITDA of about 3.7bn to about 13bn and EBIT of about -2.4bn to about +7bn. The lower end is a 2016-type price war with fast Suez normalisation; the upper end is renewed Red Sea or Hormuz disruption holding rates near 2026 levels.

What it means for the model: build Ocean on the rate and unit cost per FFE, not on revenue growth; keep pass-through revenue and cost moving together; show group EBIT and EPS as near-zero quantities in FY2027-FY2029 whose sign is set by about 150-200 per FFE of rate.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7675P-0245
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?B754th of 19full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

F4516th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Maersk enters the downturn with no financial leverage and leaves it with modest leverage. The balance sheet is never at risk, but it stops supporting earnings. Net cash excluding leases peaks at about 15.5bn at end-2026 and falls to about 11.0bn (2027), 7.9bn (2028), 5.5bn (2029) and 4.7bn (2030), because company-basis free cash flow is negative in every year from 2027 while the FY2026 dividend (about 2.15bn, paid in 2027) is large. Including lease liabilities of about 12bn, the group moves from about 3.3bn of net cash at end-2026 to about 1bn of net debt at end-2027 and about 7bn at end-2030 (about 0.8x EBITDA). The consequence for EPS is the part the market has not priced: interest income falls with the cash, so net interest and other financial items move from about +0.05bn in FY2026 to -0.15bn, -0.35bn, -0.50bn and -0.60bn, a drag of 10-42 USD per share in years when operating EPS is close to zero. We disagree with the first set's description of net financial items as "roughly neutral to slightly positive": that is true for FY2026-FY2027 only.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Starting point (Q2 2026): cash and deposits 18.5bn (6.3bn bank balances, 11.7bn short-term deposits); lease liabilities 12.2bn; net cash 13.7bn excluding leases and about 1.0bn including them; BBB+/Baa1. Interest income was 942m in FY2025 and 1.1bn in FY2024. Lease interest runs at 176m a quarter (728m in FY2025) and does not fall in a downturn.
  • The cash path. FY2027: company-basis free cash flow -2.0bn, dividend paid 2.15bn, acquisitions 0.2bn, only the January 2027 tail of the buyback (about 0.1bn). FY2028: free cash flow -2.7bn and a small dividend. FY2029: -2.2bn. FY2030: -0.6bn. DNB Carnegie's own forecast of cash and deposits falling from 22.3bn (2026) to 17.0bn (2028) points the same way, though it is less severe than ours because its free cash flow definition does not deduct leases.
  • Interest income and expense. With average gross cash falling from about 19bn (2026) to about 9.5bn (2030) and deposit yields easing from about 4.3% to about 3.6%, interest income falls from about 0.85bn to about 0.35bn, while lease interest (about 0.7bn) and bond interest (about 0.25bn) stay. Every 1bn of cash consumed costs about 40m of pre-tax income a year.
  • The buyback is the other financial lever, and it stops. We assume no new programme in February 2027 (the current DKK 6.3bn programme ends 29 January 2027). The precedent is February 2024, when Clerc suspended a 3bn-a-year programme as soon as the outlook darkened; the FY2027 guidance in February 2027 will show negative company-basis free cash flow. The share count is then flat at about 14.34m from 2027, against a 27% reduction between 2019 and 2025. Buyback accretion, which added about 2% a year to EPS, ends.
  • Equity and returns. Shareholders' equity of about 61bn at end-2026 falls to about 58.3bn by end-2029 (losses and dividends), so book value per share is about 4,060-4,100 in FY2028-FY2029, slightly below the consensus 4,187. Return on equity: about 9% (FY2026), 1.0% (FY2027), -1.2% (FY2028), -0.4% (FY2029), 1.3% (FY2030).
  • Impairment is the hidden balance-sheet lever. The Ocean cash-generating unit's impairment headroom fell to 1.1bn at end-2025 from 4.1bn a year earlier (2025 annual report), after the vessel-life extension raised carrying values. With our Ocean EBITDA of 2.3-2.9bn in FY2028-FY2029 against 8.6bn in FY2026, an Ocean impairment at the FY2027 annual test (reported February 2028) is more likely than not in our base case. It would be non-cash and excluded from underlying results, and it would lower future depreciation (a 4bn write-down over about 18 remaining years is about 0.2bn a year), slightly lifting underlying EBIT from FY2028. Logistics goodwill from more than 8bn of acquisitions since 2019 is a second, smaller candidate.
  • Peers. Hapag-Lloyd held a 5.9bn liquidity reserve at mid-2026 and is spending 4.2bn in cash on ZIM, which carries net debt of about 3.8bn; Gemini's partner will be more leveraged than Maersk through the trough. Industry net cash of 26-30bn means no wave of distress before 2028, which is why the downturn is long (see P-05).

What it means for the model: model interest income on a falling cash balance rather than as a constant; keep the share count flat from 2027; treat an Ocean impairment as a likely FY2027 reported (not underlying) event; no refinancing risk in any scenario.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7745P-0345
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

F4516th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: A meaningful part of the cost base is reducible over one to two years, but most of the reduction is pass-through (bunker, Cape diversion costs) rather than margin-accretive self-help. Discretionary cuts beyond pass-through are perhaps 1-2bn a year by 2028 (our estimate): material against the FY2028 consensus EBIT of 0.8bn, small against a rate swing of 5bn or more.

Cost line by cost line:

  • Bunker (about 19-21% of Ocean costs; about 7-8bn a year at current prices): reducible through slow steaming (more than 300m saved in past downturns), Gemini (−5% to −9% consumption) and shorter Suez routes. Largely passed through to customers, so cuts here also reduce revenue.
  • Cape diversion costs (about 500m a month): disappear with Suez normalisation, along with the matching surcharges.
  • Network excluding bunker (27%: charters, vessel running costs): blank sailings and capacity withdrawal (up to 20% of network capacity in past downturns) and charter redeliveries (43% of the fleet is leased; short charters can go back within about a year, but some long charters run to 2028-2031 at high rates). About 10-20% reducible in a deep downturn.
  • Terminal handling (39%): variable with volume, and partly paid to Maersk's own terminals (an internal margin).
  • SG&A (9% of Ocean costs plus group overheads): the 1,000-role cut (15% of corporate functions; 130-180m a year); in 2023 headcount was cut from 110,000 to 100,000. More is possible in a downturn.
  • Depreciation: not reducible; the life extension has already been used.
  • Logistics & Services: Solutions start-up and "whitespace" costs; warehouse utilisation.

What it means for the model: a self-help cost line of 0.5-1bn by 2028 in the base case (higher in a deep downturn), separate from bunker and Cape pass-through effects, which net out against revenue.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

F4512th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Two very different pools of cost reduction are coming, and the model must keep them apart. The large pool, about 4bn in FY2027 rising to about 5bn by FY2028 against FY2026, is pass-through: Cape-diversion network costs leaving as Gemini returns to Suez, lower bunker burn on the shorter route, and lower oil. It reaches the cost lines but not profit, because the matching surcharges and fuel recovery leave revenue at the same time. The small pool is genuine self-help: about 0.8bn of EBITDA by FY2028 (Ocean network actions about 0.55bn, corporate overhead about 0.18bn, Solutions warehouse utilisation about 0.1bn), arriving mostly from H2 2027 into 2028. That self-help offsets only about 10-15% of the base-rate erosion (about 5.2bn in FY2027 and a further 2.4bn in FY2028). It lowers the trough; it does not change the sign of Ocean EBIT. The upside risk is that Maersk, which delivered Gemini benefits of 950m against an 820m-1.1bn range, cuts harder once losses are widespread: perhaps a further 0.3-0.5bn by 2029.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Pass-through pool, line by line. Cape routing added about 500m a month of network cost at its peak (DNB Carnegie, Morgan Stanley); with services already partly back on Suez, we assume about 4bn of Cape-related cost in FY2026 falling to about 1.2bn in FY2027 and close to nil in FY2028. Maersk's war-risk surcharges fell 67% in January 2025 and a further 80% in February 2025, which shows surcharge revenue leaves in step with the cost. Bunker: about 610 per FFE in FY2026 (Brent averaging about 95 dollars), about 530 in FY2027 (Brent about 90, about 8% lower consumption per FFE on Suez routings) and about 490 in FY2028 (Brent about 80). Each saving is matched by a lower Fossil Fuel Fee, with recovery of roughly 80-90% in 2027 and 70% in 2028 as the market loosens.
  • Self-help pool and its timing.
  • Corporate overhead: 1,000 of 6,000 corporate roles, 180m a year of run-rate savings, against a 200m restructuring charge in 2026 (Bernstein, 6 February 2026). Net benefit is fully visible only from 2027. Precedent: the 2023 cut from 110,000 to about 100,000 staff delivered about 600m of SG&A savings in 2024, and headcount then crept back with volume, so we treat about half as durable.
  • Ocean network: blank sailings (immediate, but they raise cost per box carried), slow steaming (months), charter redeliveries (only as charters expire; container-vessel lease commitments of 4.29bn with 572m due in 2026 and 301m in 2027, and some charters run to 2028-2031 at about 41,000 dollars a day), idling and scrapping (1-2 years). The sequence engages only when losses are widespread, so we phase about 10 per FFE in FY2027 and about 40-50 per FFE from FY2028 (about 0.55-0.7bn).
  • Solutions: the margin fell to 1.7% in Q2 2026 from 3.8% on new-warehouse whitespace; filling sites lifts the margin toward 3-4% by 2028 (about 0.1bn). Logistics & Services EBIT margin in our base case: 5.1% (FY2026), 5.4%, 5.6%, 5.8%, 5.8%, still below the 6% target it has missed for years.
  • Cost inflation working the other way: labour inflation of 7.4% in Terminals (Q2 2026), handling cost inflation, EU ETS at 100% of verified emissions and FuelEU tightening (Morgan Stanley: about 491m a year of EU ETS cost by 2027), and sticky charter rates. Hapag-Lloyd's management says these regulatory costs "cannot be negotiated away".
  • Peer precedents. Hapag-Lloyd's Ventus programme targets 1.1bn of run-rate savings by 2027, but it includes Gemini synergies Maersk has already banked; Hapag-Lloyd took more than a year to capture 435m of UASC synergies. Kuehne+Nagel expects CHF 150m of overhead savings in 2026. DSV is retiring more than 70 warehouse management systems; Maersk's acquired logistics platforms still run on separate systems, so IT simplification is a later (2028+) and unquantified opportunity.
  • Depreciation is not cuttable, and the vessel-life extension has already been used. Only an impairment lowers it (P-02).

What it means for the model: let pass-through costs fall with the matching revenue; add self-help as a separate, phased line (about 0.15bn in FY2027, 0.8bn by FY2028, steady thereafter); do not let cost actions lift the FY2027-FY2028 trajectory more than a few hundred million.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4738P-0522
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?F3818th of 19full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

F2216th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: The loops explain the shape of our forecast: a fast fall in H1 2027 and a long trough through 2028-2029. In 2027, two balancing loops that ended the 2026 tailwind (the route-disruption loop, as carriers return to Suez to save fuel and time, and the congestion loop, as softer volumes clear port queues) release capacity at the same moment as the reinforcing profit-to-orderbook loop delivers the 2021-2022 and 2024-2026 orders. They compound each other. The balancing loop that ends downturns (losses, then withdrawal, then scrapping) is real but lagged, and the industry's 26-30bn of net cash (the cash-buffer loop) delays its last and decisive step, scrapping, until losses are widespread and persistent. Our reading of the precedents is that withdrawal begins in H2 2027, scrapping peaks in 2028-2029, and the market only tightens in 2030 as deliveries fall. That is why FY2029 stays near the trough in our base case and why FY2030 is the first recovery year. For Maersk specifically, two further loops matter: the integrator-forwarder loop costs it about one point a year of volume growth against the market in a loose market, and the Terminals loop keeps compounding the earnings floor.

Loop by loop (direction; evidence; effect on the line items):

  1. Profit to orderbook (reinforcing, negative, locked in). The orderbook is 13-15m TEU (39-44% of the fleet), up from 12.9m to 14.2m TEU in Q3 2026 alone (Bernstein, 9 October 2026); MSC holds 2.9m TEU, COSCO placed a 7.07bn order in December 2025, Yang Ming ordered 81.9k TEU in September 2026, and Maersk itself ordered about 500k TEU for 2029-2030. Global yard capacity rises 30% to 77m CGT by 2028 (Deutsche Bank), so yards do not cap it. Effect: fleet growth of 7.4% (2027) and 13.2% (2028) on Bernstein's figures, with a tail into 2029; rates down in FY2027-FY2028; capex and depreciation up for Maersk from 2029.
  2. Cash buffer delays scrapping (reinforcing the downturn). Only 5k TEU was scrapped in 2025 and about 41k TEU is expected in 2026 (Deutsche Bank). In the last cycle scrapping went from 197k TEU (2015) to 657k TEU (2016) only after a year of losses; Hapag-Lloyd's Q4 2023 operating loss was its first since Q2 2016, and discipline followed it. Effect: the trough lasts at least two years (FY2028-FY2029).
  3. Losses to withdrawal (balancing, lagged). The sequence is blank sailings (weeks; 11% of Transpacific sailings blanked in October 2025), slow steaming (months; average speeds fell from almost 17 to 15 knots by 2023, about 10% of productive capacity), charter redelivery (as contracts lapse), idling (idle capacity 2.2% now against a 6.0% peak in mid-2020), then scrapping. Effect: rate stabilisation from 2028 and a small recovery from 2029, not a V.
  4. Congestion (balancing, self-reversing). Port queues absorbed about 8.5% of the fleet in October and 12.6% in August 2026 (Jefferies, Freighthub). Morgan Stanley (21 October 2026) notes Transpacific demand softening and queues easing. Effect: a second capacity release in H1 2027 on top of Suez.
  5. Route disruption (balancing for the tailwind). Returning to Suez cuts 12-15 days per voyage and bunker cost, so each carrier that returns gains a cost edge and pressures the others; CMA CGM went first, Gemini has restored six services, while Evergreen, HMM, Yang Ming and ONE still avoid the Red Sea. The split behaviour slows re-entry and some released capacity is absorbed by slower steaming on the shorter route. Effect: Suez normalisation spread over about Q4 2026 to Q2 2027, consistent with the Suez Canal Authority's expectation of a full Maersk return by March 2027.
  6. Contract duration (reinforcing volatility). Volatility shortened contracts, and short contracts transmit spot faster. Effect: realised rates track spot with a one-quarter lag in both directions.
  7. Integrator against forwarder (reinforcing, negative in loose markets). Forwarders supply up to 50% of Ocean volume and are placing cargo with carriers that do not compete with them (Hapag-Lloyd, MSC, CMA CGM); DSV is now the largest forwarder after buying DB Schenker. Effect: Maersk loaded volume +1.8% in FY2027 and +2.2% in FY2028, below market demand growth of about 3%; a modest rate concession to keep forwarder volume.
  8. Terminals hub concentration (reinforcing, positive). Gemini routes volume into Maersk-owned hubs (+26% year on year in Q1 2026). Effect: Terminals EBITDA growth of 5-7% a year from FY2028, after a flat FY2027 as congestion income fades.
  9. Capital returns (not a constraint on ordering). Maersk paid out heavily at past peaks and still ordered 500k TEU; the FY2026 dividend (about 2.15bn) is paid just as free cash flow turns negative. Effect: cash falls faster (P-02); no supply restraint.
  10. Accounting (balancing for reported EBIT). The vessel-life extension raised book values; low earnings raise impairment risk; an impairment lowers later depreciation.

The upside loop the Consumer must keep in view: a renewed Red Sea closure or Hormuz escalation restarts the route and congestion loops within weeks. That is the upper tail of the cone (FY2027 EBITDA near 13bn), and its probability is not small given the history since 2024.

What it means for the model: rates down 24% in FY2027 and 8% in FY2028, then +3% and +5%; volume below market until Maersk's own ships arrive in 2029-2030; no recovery before FY2030.

Maersk's formal guidance is conservative, specific in its mechanics and silent on the variable that matters (the rate path).
