C-68

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

F4516th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: FY2026 guidance is well below consensus. Underlying EBITDA guidance of 10.5-12.5bn (midpoint 11.5bn, set on 13 August 2026) compares with FactSet consensus of 14.0bn (median; mean 13.9bn), 12% above the top of the range and 22% above the midpoint. Underlying EBIT guidance of 4.5-6.5bn compares with consensus of 8.1bn, 25% above the top (allow for some basis difference between "underlying" and the brokers' EBIT). The free cash flow guidance, "above zero" on the company's definition, is consistent with FactSet consensus FCF of 4.75bn on FactSet's definition (before leases). There is no FY2027 guidance yet; it comes with the FY2026 results in early February 2027. FY2027 consensus is EBITDA of 8.7bn, EBIT of 2.05bn and EPS of 124.69.

Why the gap:

  1. Spot rates stayed higher in Q3 than Maersk assumed in August (congestion, typhoons, Hormuz), and brokers have marked to market since (Deutsche Bank noted in August that the 11.5bn midpoint was then ahead of a 9.12bn consensus; consensus has since moved past guidance).
  2. Maersk's habit is to anchor guidance on cautious spot assumptions and raise it later (sandbagging).
  3. A few very bullish brokers lift the average (two FactSet rows show 18.2bn).

What the numbers imply: the guidance midpoint implies H2 2026 EBITDA of about 6.75bn (about 3.4bn a quarter, +13% on Q2's 2.99bn); consensus implies about 9.3bn (about 4.6bn a quarter, +55% on Q2, about the Q3 2024 peak level of 4.8bn). Q3 2026 can plausibly reach that level; Q4 2026, with Suez capacity returning and the Asia-Europe spot rate already rolling over, is less likely to.

Our judgement: a further raise on 5 November 2026 is likely (to about 12.5-14.5bn of EBITDA; our estimate). Consensus at 14.0bn already discounts most of it, so the November event is roughly balanced. The February 2027 FY2027 guidance is likely to sit below FY2027 consensus, given the sandbagging habit and Suez normalisation, which makes it a negative event risk.

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

F4516th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Near-term guidance (FY2026) is conservative. Beyond FY2026 there is no formal earnings guidance, and the standing long-term targets are not conservative once adjusted for the input forces.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • FY2026: guidance is 12-25% below consensus (C-68) despite a strong Q3, so it is conservative.
  • 2026-2027 capex of 10-11bn and a market volume assumption of 2-4% are realistic.
  • The long-term targets (ROIC above 7.5% through the cycle, Logistics & Services EBIT margin of 6%, Terminals ROIC above 9%) were set against a 2021-2024 history flattered by the pandemic and the Red Sea. Adjusted for the 2027-2028 supply wave, the group ROIC target is optimistic: consensus implies about 1-3% ROIC in 2027-2028, and the 2025-2028 average would be below 7.5% even with 2026 included. The Logistics & Services 6% target has been missed for years. Only the Terminals target is conservative (16% delivered).
  • Management's qualitative view of a "structurally higher rate level" (C-85) is the opposite of conservative.

What it means for the model: expect FY2027 guidance (February 2027) to be set conservatively against a falling market; do not anchor 2027-2029 on the long-term ROIC target.

How specific is the guidance: built from stated building blocks, or vague about how results come about?

D559th of 19

Judgement: Moderately specific. C-85

To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

F5017th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Management's informal commentary is materially more optimistic than both its formal guidance and sell-side consensus, especially for 2027 and beyond. Clerc argues the market has moved to a "structurally higher rate level", that landside bottlenecks and under-investment in ports will "support elevated freight rates even as vessel supply expands", that demand is "surprisingly resilient" (electrification goods, Far East exports), and earlier that "the outlook is much more benign" on a three-to-five-year view. On the Suez return he is cautious and service-specific ("one-third of normal volumes"; a strict "safety threshold"), which implies a slower capacity release than the bears assume. Against consensus FY2027 EPS of 124.69 (−75%), this commentary implies a much shallower downturn.

Why: (1) a genuine belief in the landside-constraint thesis, which is real for APM Terminals; (2) management has been right about the persistence of disruption since 2024; (3) an incentive to support rate expectations with customers and the valuation with investors.

Our view: the commentary is directionally too optimistic for 2027-2028, because Maersk's own order of 500k TEU undermines the argument that vessels cannot be used (Bernstein's point), and because congestion absorbs capacity episodically, not structurally.

What it means for the model: do not let management's tone lift 2027-2028 rates; use it to define the upside scenario.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

C-527th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Five things are worth knowing.

  1. Ad hoc guidance changes between results: Danish disclosure rules require immediate announcement when expectations change materially, so upgrades and downgrades arrive between results (29 June 2026). Watch for one before the February 2027 results, and in 2027 a downgrade could come mid-quarter.
  2. Guidance is on "underlying" EBITDA and EBIT plus company-defined FCF (after leases) and capex. There is no EPS guidance.
  3. A new Head of Investor Relations (Martin Dunwoodie, from 2026) and a new CFO, which together could change the style of guidance from 2027.
  4. The structure: A and B shares (B shares carry no votes); DKK listing, USD reporting; buybacks include pro-rata purchases from the Foundation.
  5. Lightly explained accounting choices (the vessel useful-life change).

Next results: Q3 2026 on 5 November 2026.

What it means for the model: build quarterly, mark rates to market frequently, and expect guidance to move intra-quarter.

C-74

To what extent have sell-side estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts, and has company guidance recently been rebased lower, or more aggressively kitchen-sinked or sandbagged?

F4015th of 19

Judgement: The downcycle is only half in the numbers. The Buy share is unchanged at 8%. Revisions this large and this broad are the opposite of a cut.

  • Ratings: about 60% Sell or Underweight (Bloomberg in August: 1 Buy, 12 Hold, 14 Sell); FactSet mean rating Underweight; targets about 13-15% below the price and rising (+41% over three months).

What it means for the model: the sell-side numbers for FY2027 are not a conservative floor. A rebase is still ahead (likely with the FY2027 guidance in February 2027).

6c

Case simplicity

7 answers
Judgement38C-7940D-0260D-0335D-0425D-0562D-0658
Group judgement

Risks

F388th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The third set's seven tests return one consistent picture. The earlier sets are congruent because they share a single causal chain, and that chain rests on precedent (2016, 2023) rather than on any capability Maersk would need to acquire; the only capability that could overturn it is the one management claims and the evidence does not support, that landside bottlenecks will hold rates up against a 20% fleet expansion. The drivers of the forecast are concentrated to the point of being one variable, the Ocean base freight rate, with a certain cause (the orderbook) and an uncertain one (the timing of the Suez return), and with three small below-EBIT items that decide the sign of EPS. That concentration makes direction highly predictable and level poorly predictable: the FY2027 EBITDA cone is 3.7-13bn around 7.4bn, the sell-side's FY2027 EPS range is -22 to +497, and the probability-weighted centre of the cone is the base case, with the downside more likely and the upside larger. The company's disclosure gives the three operating numbers the model needs each quarter and the constants to convert them, but not the rate assumption, the contract mix or the below-EBIT lines; the weekly external data fill the first two gaps, nothing fills the third, so EBITDA and EBIT are forecastable and EPS is not. The share price moves with the container cycle at twice its peer's amplitude and sits at 0.8x book, a late-cycle level, so the multiple's direction is as predictable as the rate's and its path as unpredictable as positioning. For the memo's riskiness topic: the case is simple (one driver), fragile in level (a factor of three on 2027 earnings), trackable in near real time, and skewed to the downside in price because the price already discounts the upside tail. The predictability of the forecasts over 18 months is low on level and high on direction, and the direction is down.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

F4017th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The perimeter is broad: 130 countries, 500+ ports, about 60 terminals in 29+ countries, three segments with Logistics & Services split into three units, and 100,000+ customers. It is narrowing at the margins (Svitzer, Maersk Training) while adding infrastructure (Panama Canal Railway, Da Nang). Breadth does not help predictability, because one variable (the Ocean freight rate) dominates group earnings, and the diversified segments (about 4bn of combined EBITDA) are too small to offset it. Breadth adds organisational complexity (a slow matrix) and FX and geopolitical exposure.

What it means for the model: predictability is low and comes from Ocean; the extra granularity of Logistics & Services and Terminals adds accuracy only at the margin.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

D605th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The expected change here is a cyclical reset (FY2026 EBITDA of about 12.8bn to a two-year trough of about 6.3-7.1bn, with EPS near zero or negative), and past delivery makes it highly plausible: Maersk has produced exactly this shape twice in the last decade (2016 and 2023), in both cases faster than its own guidance anticipated, and its present delivery (the 2026 beats) is the rate's delivery, not the company's. The part of the forecast that relies on Maersk's strengths is the floor, not the fall, and the floor rests almost entirely on strengths the business demonstrably has: a Gemini unit-cost edge measured in the accounts, a Terminals franchise that held returns above its hurdle through 2016 and 2023, a net-cash balance sheet, and a proven capacity-withdrawal playbook. The only parts that require strengths Maersk has not demonstrated are the Logistics & Services margin path (toward 5.8%, against a 6% target missed for a decade) and the Solutions recovery, and the second set already discounts both and sizes them at a few hundred million. The asymmetry is that the one strength management claims and we do not credit, the ability of landside bottlenecks to hold rates up, is the only one that could make the forecast wrong on the upside.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • The fall needs no strengths, only precedent. 2016: Maersk Line's loaded rate fell 18.7% to 1,795 per FFE, unit cost fell 11.5% (mostly bunker) and the business swung from a 1.3bn profit to an underlying loss of 384m for the year; the dividend was cut by half and no new buyback was launched. 2023: Ocean EBIT fell from 29.2bn to 2.2bn, with Q4 2023 at -920m at a loaded rate of 1,925 against a unit cost of 2,280; guidance for 2024 was set at 1-6bn of EBITDA and the buyback suspended. October 2015: guidance cut after demand growth collapsed to 0-1%. The second set's FY2027 path (EBITDA -40%, EBIT -79%) is milder than 2022-2023 (EBITDA -75%, EBIT -90%) and comparable to 2015-2016. Nothing in the forecast asks the cycle to do something it has not done.
  • The floor rests on demonstrated cost delivery. Ocean unit cost at fixed bunker fell 7.1% year on year to 2,333 per FFE in Q1 2026; Gemini benefits of about 950m landed above the 820m-1.1bn target range in year one; bunker consumption improved 9% and asset turns four points in Q4 2025; schedule reliability of about 90% against an industry 50-60% has been sustained for seven quarters. In 2016 Maersk idled vessels, returned charters, cut 4,000 roles (250m of SG&A) and pushed unit cost below 2,000 per FFE for the first time; in 2023 it cut 10,000 roles and network costs 14% before FX. The second set's self-help of about 0.8bn by FY2028 is below both precedents; the risk is that it is too small, as the second set says.
  • The floor rests on a Terminals franchise with a tested downturn record. Revenue per move rose 4.3% in 2019 and 7.1-7.7% in Q2 2026; ROIC was 10.5% in 2023 and 14.8% in Q2 2026 against a 9% hurdle; the 2016 trough still earned 6.3%. The second set's flat FY2027 Terminals EBITDA explicitly removes the congestion-linked storage income (the 2023 precedent: revenue per move -8.2% as dwell times normalised; about 220m of congestion storage income in 2021 deflated back to 2019 levels). Two cautions the Consumer should hold: capacity utilisation fell to 81% in Q2 2026 from 86% as new capacity opened ahead of volume, and Morgan Stanley puts APM Terminals' five-year average EBITDA margin (34%) below the pure-play industry's 45%. The floor is durable but not immune.
  • The parts that need strengths not yet shown, and how the forecast treats them. Logistics & Services: organic growth of 3.2% in 2025 against a 10% target, an EBIT margin of 4.6-4.8% against 6%, the margin component of the 2025 incentive scoring zero, acquired platforms still on separate IT, Solutions at 1.7%. The second set models 5.4% in FY2027 rising to 5.8%, i.e. it assumes partial success under new leadership (CFO Erni, Solutions head Plantaz, both from Kuehne+Nagel lineages). Peer precedent (DSV's contract-logistics conversion ratio of 26.1%; CEVA's slow margin lift after footprint expansion) says margins can be lifted but slowly. If Logistics & Services stayed at 5.1%, FY2028 EBIT would be about 85m lower: immaterial to the case. Forwarder retention: as in D-01, immaterial.
  • The strength management claims and the sets do not credit. Clerc's view that terminal and landside constraints will blunt the usability of new vessels is the one thesis that would make the forecast wrong on the upside. The evidence gathered for this set is mixed: port congestion absorbed 8.5-12.6% of the fleet in August-October 2026 and Deutsche Bank's proprietary congestion index showed no easing in late October; against that, Drewry data cited by Morgan Stanley put port utilisation outside China at 60-70%, APM Terminals' own utilisation fell to 81%, and Bernstein's point stands that a carrier that believed the thesis would not order 500k TEU. The sets treat this as the upside scenario (20%); I would not credit it in the base case either, but it is the single item the Consumer should name as the thesis's main vulnerability.
  • Present delivery is not evidence of acquired strength. The 2026 beats (Q2 EBITDA +44% against consensus; loaded rate 15% above consensus; two guidance raises) coincide with Hapag-Lloyd (two raises), ZIM (+30% at the midpoint), COSCO (Q3 net profit +81%), HMM (Q3 operating profit 23-42% above consensus) and NYK (+56% to its full-year forecast). This is the market's delivery. The one company-specific element, a 22% loaded-rate increase in Q2 against Hapag-Lloyd's 9%, reflects spot exposure, which cuts the other way in 2027.

What it means for the model and the memo: no input changes from this question. For the memo, the Consumer can state that the forecast's downside leg relies on no company capability at all and its floor relies on capabilities already in the accounts; the forecast is more exposed to management being right about landside constraints than to management failing to deliver anything.

D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?F358th of 8full answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

F258th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The cone is very wide on level, narrow on direction, and its skew depends on what is being measured. On earnings, the cone around FY2027 is about 3.7-13bn of EBITDA (our 7.4bn in the middle), about -2.4bn to +7bn of EBIT, and roughly -150 to +450 of EPS; the probability-weighted mean of the second set's three scenarios (50% base, 30% deeper at about 3.7bn, 20% disruption-extended at about 13bn) is about 7.4bn, so the base case is the mean, and the earnings skew is two-sided: the downside tail is more probable, the upside tail is larger and arrives faster. On the share price, the skew is one-sided to the downside, because the price (0.8x book, the 24-month-forward P/E at the 100th percentile of its ten-year range, +80% in twelve months) already discounts something close to the upside scenario, while a fair trough valuation sits 35-45% below it. The case is therefore predictable in direction and timing-of-direction (rates lower in 2027 than in 2026 with a probability I put above 85%; the first year-on-year decline in group revenue in Q2 2027; the first FY2027 guidance below consensus), unpredictable in level (a 3.5x range on FY2027 EBITDA), and predictable in the sign of the share-price skew.

How wide, in evidence:

  • The sell-side range is the width. FY2027 EPS from -22 (Jyske Bank) to 497 (Barclays), with a FactSet mean of 172 and a median of 125; FY2028 EBITDA from 1.5bn (Jyske) to 10.2bn (Clarksons), with -383 to +294 of EPS; even the three most active bears disagree on 2028 (DNB Carnegie -116, Morgan Stanley +31, Deutsche Bank +60). The dispersion report identifies what separates the ends: the high end assumes prolonged disruption keeps effective capacity constrained through 2027; the low end assumes a rapid and complete Suez normalisation into the 2027 delivery wave. No house differs on the orderbook; every difference is about timing.
  • The history of dispersion is the same width. In 2015-2016, 2019 and 2022-2023, outcomes diverged from forecasts made 12-18 months earlier by amounts of this order, and in each case one driver explained the miss. Maersk's own FY2026 consensus EPS went from a mean of -55 to +487 within the last twelve months; FY2027 from -71 to +172. A range that wide in the current year, with two months left, is the best measure of how little the level can be forecast.
  • The company's own range is wide. The FY2026 guidance range has been 2bn wide on EBITDA at every revision; the February 2024 initial range was 1-6bn, five billion wide. Maersk itself does not forecast its level within a factor of two at the start of a year.
  • Timing mechanics widen the near quarters. A renewed disruption restarts the route and congestion loops within weeks (the disruption report: a six-day Suez blockage in 2021 backed up hundreds of ships; the Hormuz closure in early 2026 forced carriers that had returned to Suez back round Africa within days). A full Suez return releases about 7% of effective capacity (Morgan Stanley; 6% on AlixPartners' estimate; up to 10% of theoretical capacity on a former CMA CGM commercial officer's estimate) over roughly Q4 2026 to Q2 2027. Container transits were at 54% of pre-disruption levels on 5 October and 59% by late October; the Suez Canal Authority expects all Maersk services back by March 2027, security permitting. Both tails can be reached within two quarters.

How skewed, and why the answer differs by lens:

  • On fundamentals, modestly to the downside in probability, to the upside in size. The deeper case (a 2016-type price war as MSC, CMA CGM and COSCO fill new tonnage; FY2027 rate about 1,800; FY2028 EPS about -250 to -300; an Ocean impairment certain) is more probable than the upside because the orderbook is contractual, scrapping is near zero, the industry holds 26-30bn of net cash with which to "play chicken", and because the cone's lower half has been hit in every supply-wave precedent. The upside (a renewed Red Sea closure or a Hormuz escalation; FY2027 rate near 2,600; EPS 400-450) is a quarter-to-quarter possibility with a base rate that the last three years make hard to dismiss; and when it arrives, it adds about 5.6bn of EBITDA against the deeper case's 3.7bn subtraction.
  • On the share price, to the downside. The first set's cross-checks give about -35% to -45% to a fair trough (0.5x book; a sum of the parts with Ocean at nil), about -50% in a stress case, and about +20-25% if disruption persists through 2027 and the shares reach 1.0x book. The 52-week low (DKK 11,778) was set when FY2027 consensus EPS was negative; the price has since risen 94% while the median FY2027 EPS has risen to 125, i.e. the market has moved from pricing the bear case to pricing something between the base and the bull. Three further asymmetries: the sell-side's targets are 13% below the price and are being raised, so a positive surprise is partly in them; the stock is a crowded momentum position (relative strength at the 97th percentile; Deutsche Bank's note on quant deleveraging and short-squeeze dynamics; Jefferies on style rotations punishing crowded trades) in a risk-off environment where defensives lead cyclicals; and the main upside event, a disruption, is also the event that reminds the market that Maersk's earnings are hostage to it.
  • Below EBITDA, the skew is to the downside for EPS specifically. The three below-EBIT items (tax floor, interest decay, buyback end) subtract about 60 a share in FY2028 whichever scenario occurs; in the upside scenario they are lost in a large number, in the base and deeper scenarios they decide the sign. Consensus EPS of 62 for FY2028 (and 119 for FY2029 on two brokers) sits above our base case by more than the whole of these items.

How the width narrows over the 18 months (the schedule the Consumer should write into topic #9): 5 November 2026 (Q3 actuals; Q4 commentary; the FY2026 range) narrows FY2026 to within about 0.5bn; December 2026 to February 2027 (Asia-Europe contract settlements; the pace of Gemini's remaining Suez returns; Hapag-Lloyd's and Asian carriers' commentary) narrows H1 2027; early February 2027 (first FY2027 guidance; the buyback decision; the FY2026 annual impairment test) narrows the level of 2027 to the company's own range, which will itself be 2-3bn wide; the spring Transpacific season (May 2027) settles the contract half of 2027; H2 2027 is where the deliveries arrive (3.29m TEU in 2027, 5.23m in 2028 on Jefferies' schedule) and where the deeper and base cases separate. By the February 2028 results the FY2028 cone will still be about 2x wide.

What it means for predictability, in one line: the forecast's direction and the share-price skew are predictable enough to build a memo on; the level of FY2027 EPS is not forecastable to within a factor of three, and the memo should present it as a range keyed to the rate rather than as a point.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

C623rd of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: Helpful enough for the one variable that matters, and better than most European cyclicals, because the company publishes quarterly the three numbers that the model is built on (loaded volume, average loaded rate per FFE, unit cost at fixed bunker), gives explicit sensitivities, reports segment EBITDA and the three Logistics sub-units, and must announce guidance changes between results under Danish rules. Its main weaknesses are that the decisive inputs behind guidance (the rate assumption, the Suez assumption, the contract-spot mix in the quarter) are not disclosed, that surcharges and congestion-linked income are bundled inside freight revenue, and that the items that decide the sign of EPS (tax, financial items, impairment headroom) are disclosed annually or not reconciled. For the next six months this matters less than it will later, because the external data on the one variable are weekly, public and good, and because the company's own silence on the rate path is filled by Hapag-Lloyd, the listed Asian carriers (monthly revenue) and the forwarders. The case can be monitored almost in real time from outside; the company's disclosure is a quarterly confirmation of what the indices already said.

What the company discloses, and how to use it (the monitoring kit for the next six months):

  • Quarterly (next: 5 November 2026, then early February 2027, then May 2027): Ocean loaded volume (m FFE), average loaded rate (per FFE), unit cost at fixed bunker (per FFE), bunker price and consumption, the operated fleet, Ocean revenue split into freight and "other including hubs", Ocean EBITDA and EBIT; Terminals volume, revenue per move, EBITDA margin and ROIC; Forwarding, Solutions and Landside revenue and EBIT margin; group guidance on underlying EBITDA, EBIT, company free cash flow and capex, with the market-volume assumption and the four sensitivities (100 per FFE, 100 a tonne of bunker, 10% USD, 100k FFE). Read the loaded rate against our quarterly path (Q3 2026 about 3,230 per FFE on the second set's path, which I now think slightly low; Q4 2,880; Q1 2027 2,400; Q2 2,100) and against the prior-year quarter (Q4 2025: 2,046; Q1 2026: 2,081; Q2 2026: 2,746). A Q4 2026 rate above about 3,000 says the disruption-extended scenario is live; below about 2,700 says the base case is arriving early.
  • Between results: Danish disclosure rules forced the 29 June 2026 ad hoc upgrade; the same mechanism will force any downgrade in 2027 as soon as management's expectation leaves the range. The absence of an ad hoc statement in a quarter is itself information.
  • Annually (early February 2027): the Ocean impairment headroom (1.1bn at end-2025 against 4.1bn a year earlier; pre-tax discount rate 9.8%, terminal growth 2.1%; a 0.5-point change in the discount rate moves value by about 2.7bn), the goodwill allocation after the Logistics reorganisation (4.6bn, to be split across Forwarding, Solutions and Landside for the first time), committed leases not yet commenced (9.3bn), green-fuel offtakes (5.9bn), the Key Audit Matters on the vessel-life change, and the German tax audit status. These decide reported (not underlying) EPS and the balance sheet, and the February 2027 report is the only place to read them.

Where the disclosure fails the thesis, and what fills the gap:

  1. No rate or Suez assumption behind guidance. Guidance is "highly dependent on" the Red Sea duration, but the assumed timing is never stated, so the implied H2 run-rate has to be inferred (the guidance midpoint of 11.5bn implied 2.9-3.9bn a quarter in H2 2026 against the 4.6bn a quarter consensus needed). Filled by: Morgan Stanley's weekly Suez Canal transit tracker (54% on 5 October, 59% in late October), Gemini's own service-by-service announcements (six services back by September; SE1 in late October), the Suez Canal Authority's transit counts (276 Maersk transits in January-September, +89%).
  2. No quarterly contract-spot split, and no disclosure of what reprices when. Maersk said 45% of volume was on long-term contracts in late 2025 and targeted 50% for 2026; the first set used 44%. Asia-Europe annual contracts settle around January, Transpacific around May; roughly half of volume reprices in H1 2027 and the rest is continuously exposed. Filled by: procurement expert calls (shippers moving to spot; 6-12 month terms preferred), DNB Carnegie's and Jefferies' contract-season commentary, and Hapag-Lloyd's disclosed 50-65% contract share as the slower-moving comparator.
  3. Surcharges and congestion income bundled. Demurrage, detention, storage and emergency surcharges sit inside Ocean freight revenue and Terminals revenue without a line, so the decay of pass-through revenue (about 290 per FFE of Cape and bunker recovery in the second set's FY2027 bridge) cannot be tracked directly. Filled by: Maersk's published tariff changes (war-risk surcharges cut 67% in January 2025 and 80% in February 2025 are the precedent for the speed), Hapag-Lloyd's "revenue not assigned to trades" line (demurrage and detention, -46% in 2023), and port dwell-time data.
  4. Below EBIT, no reconciliation. Tax is not split between tonnage-taxed and normally taxed activities, cash taxes are not reconciled to the charge, minorities and joint-venture income are disclosed but not guided, and there is no EPS guidance. Q1 2026 showed the consequence: EBIT beat consensus by 37% and EPS missed by 67%. Filled by nothing; the Consumer should weight EBITDA and EBIT prints over EPS prints in judging delivery.
  5. Gemini's cost-sharing with Hapag-Lloyd and the L&S overhead allocation are undisclosed (Morgan Stanley, 30 January 2026; Bernstein, 14 August 2026). This matters for the floor, not for the six-month view.

External indicators, ranked by lead time: weekly SCFI and Drewry WCI (spot; the SCFI was 3,687 in late October and +0.2% week on week on 30 October; the CCFI, the blended contract-and-spot index, was 1,876 on 23 October, +89% year on year), which lead Maersk's realised rate by about one quarter with a beta of roughly 0.4-0.5; Suez transits (weekly); Linerlytica and Alphaliner idle-fleet and blank-sailing data (idle capacity 2.2% in October 2026 against 6.0% in mid-2020 and 7.4% in 2016; a rise above 3-4% is the first sign of withdrawal); monthly revenue from Evergreen and Yang Ming (September +64% and +70% year on year, both noting European softening); Hapag-Lloyd's quarterly results and guidance (raised on 28 September to 3.9-4.4bn of EBITDA); DSV and Kuehne+Nagel sea-freight volumes (DSV -3% in Q3, Kuehne+Nagel +5%); port congestion (4.3m TEU waiting in August; Busan at 80% utilisation); Clarksons scrapping and delivery data; Brent.

What the 5 November print will and will not tell the Consumer: it will settle Q3 (I expect about 4.7bn of underlying EBITDA and a loaded rate around 3,300), the new FY2026 range (I expect about 12-13.5bn), and the Q4 exit rate in management's words; it will not settle 2027, because management will not guide it until February and will describe the Suez return service by service. The questions worth asking on the call are the Q4-to-date loaded rate, the share of Asia-Europe volume now on Suez routings, the contract share targeted for 2027, and whether the buyback is being considered for renewal.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

D584th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The transmission from the market to EBITDA and EBIT is unusually clear, and the company itself supplies the constants; the transmission from EBIT to EPS is clear in mechanism but noisy in practice, and the company gives no help with it; and the company's own stated aims mostly do not trace into the forecast at all, because the aims that are specific (Gemini reliability, volume with the market, Terminals ROIC) are already achieved and small in earnings terms, while the aims that would matter (Logistics & Services at 6% and 10% growth; ROIC above 7.5% through the cycle; a stable fleet of 4.1-4.3m TEU) have been missed or contradicted and carry no dated milestones. The practical result is that the memo should trace the case from outside in (index to realised rate to Ocean EBITDA to EPS) and treat management's stated objectives as a list of items that will not move the numbers within 18 months.

The external transmission, link by link, with its constant and its noise:

  1. Capacity balance to spot rate. Supply: fleet growth 7.4% in 2027 and 13.2% in 2028 (Bernstein), or 9.5% and 12.7% (Clarksons via Deutsche Bank), plus about 7% of effective capacity from a full Suez return, less whatever slow steaming re-absorbs (Costamare: slow steaming is abandoned below about USD 100 Brent; DNB Carnegie: idling and slow steaming could cut effective growth to about 3%). Demand: 2-4%. Noise: congestion (8.5-12.6% of the fleet in Q3 2026) and weather. This link has no constant; it is the uncertainty.
  2. Spot index to Maersk's realised rate. Beta of about 0.4-0.5 with a lag of about one quarter: in Q1 2026 Maersk's rate fell 14% against -32% for the Drewry index; in Q2 2026 it rose 32% quarter on quarter against +55% for the SCFI; in 2016 the lag broke down because Maersk's North-South lanes (31.6% of volume then, 31% now) fell faster than the East-West indices. Noise: the contract share (45-50%) and its reset dates (January for Asia-Europe, May for Transpacific), and management's stated intention to lift the contract share toward 75% via Gemini (Morgan Stanley, 30 January 2026), which if achieved in a falling market would slow H1 2027 and lock in lower rates for all of 2027.
  3. Realised rate to Ocean EBITDA and EBIT. The company's constant: 100 per FFE is about 1.3bn of annual EBIT (about 1.36bn on FY2027 volume); the company's Q2 slide figure of 0.7bn is the rest-of-2026 effect, not annual. Volume is nearly irrelevant (100k FFE is about 10m). Pass-through moves revenue and cost together: about 290 per FFE of Cape and bunker recovery leaves with its costs in FY2027, so revenue falls about 16-17% while profit-bearing rate falls about 380 per FFE. Noise: bunker (100 a tonne is 0.2-0.3bn net of recovery), carbon costs (EU ETS at 100% in 2026; about 0.5bn a year by 2027 on Morgan Stanley's estimate; recovery falling from 80-90% to about 70% as the market loosens), and charter cost stickiness (9.3bn of committed leases not yet started).
  4. Group EBIT to EPS. Joint-venture income about 0.4bn (inside EBIT); net interest and other from +50m to -150m in FY2027 and -600m by FY2030 as cash falls; tax close to a fixed 0.4-0.5bn (tonnage tax on Ocean, normal tax on Terminals and Logistics, Pillar Two a top-up of 40m in 2025); minorities about 0.1bn; shares flat at about 14.34m from 2027. Constant: 100 per FFE of rate is about 95 of EPS. Noise: FX and derivative gains (510m of trading-derivative gains in 2025; dividend-hedge losses in H1 2026), which can move EPS by 30-40 a share in a year, and the one-offs that sit outside underlying EPS but inside reported EPS (restructuring; a probable Ocean impairment at the FY2027 test).

The company's stated aims, traced:

  • FY2026 guidance (EBITDA 10.5-12.5bn; EBIT 4.5-6.5bn; FCF above zero; capex 10-11bn for 2026-2027): traces cleanly to Ocean rate times volume, with the sensitivities given, and to the lease-inclusive FCF definition the company has used since 2023 (repayments of lease liabilities, 863m in Q2 2026 alone, are deducted). The gap between consensus FCF (4.75bn, FactSet definition) and "above zero" is definitional, as both sets said and as the AlphaSense guidance report confirms.
  • Gemini: about 90% reliability, benefits of 820m-1.1bn: achieved (950m; 90%); traces to unit cost at fixed bunker (-7.1% in Q1 2026). Its forward contribution is defensive (a 3-7% cost edge over non-Gemini carriers), not incremental.
  • Volume in line with the market: achieved in 2026 (+9.3% in Q1, +4.1% in Q2); worth about 10m of EBIT per 100k FFE; irrelevant to EPS.
  • Logistics & Services: 6% EBIT margin and 10% organic growth: missed every year (4.6-4.8% and 3.2% in 2025); Bernstein calls the pair "increasingly unrealistic"; no dated milestone. The second set traces 5.1% to 5.8% over four years, worth about 0.2bn of EBIT, about 14 a share.
  • Terminals ROIC above 9%: achieved (14.8% in Q2 2026). Traces to revenue per move (+7.1-7.7%) and volume (+4%); the storage component normalises in 2027.
  • ROIC above 7.5% through the cycle: 5.7% in 2025; the forecast gives 1-3% or less in 2027-2030; the target is not traceable to anything management controls within the horizon and is likely to be reframed at a capital-markets event under the new CFO.
  • A stable fleet of 4.1-4.3m TEU: operated capacity 4.67m TEU in Q2 2026 and 26 ships of 18,600 TEU on order for 2029-2030 (about 5bn, back-loaded: roughly 20% on signing and 50% on delivery on a standard Chinese yard schedule). The aim is contradicted; what traces is capex of about 5bn a year and depreciation rising from 2029.
  • Net zero by 2040: traces to capex (dual-fuel newbuilds at prices about 46% above pre-COVID) and to 5.9bn of green-fuel offtakes whose pricing basis is undisclosed; a cost line, not an earnings line, within the horizon.

Where traceability is weakest, and what to do about it: the quarterly path within 2027 (because contract timing is undisclosed), the pass-through share of the rate (because surcharges are bundled), and EPS in low-profit quarters (because the below-EBIT items are not guided). The memo should present milestones as rate thresholds (the loaded rate at which Ocean EBIT crosses zero is about 2,200-2,300 per FFE all-in; the group EBIT-zero rate in FY2028 is about 1,900 plus other revenue of about 290), not as management objectives.

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

F2217th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading Which perception- or sentiment-driven risks are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market

Judgement: Sentiment risk is high, and it is skewed to the downside because the stock is a crowded momentum and cyclical position at the top of its valuation range in a risk-off market. Several triggers could move the shares 10-20% in days regardless of Maersk's own results.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Headline triggers: Middle East ceasefire or peace news; Suez Canal Authority statements (it expects full Maersk services by March 2027); Gemini or rival routing announcements; weekly SCFI or Drewry index falls; Morgan Stanley's Suez Canal Transit Tracker.
  • Peer read-across: Hapag-Lloyd's Q3 results, COSCO, Evergreen, ONE and HMM results and guidance; any peer warning on 2027 rates.
  • Positioning: shares +80% over twelve months and +33.7% over three; relative strength in the 97th percentile; the shipping cohort +82% over twelve months; the Sector digest calls the group "at or near the dearest points of their ranges". The Fitness for Fighting environment is risk-off, defensives lead cyclicals, and the Momentum tilt moved to Underweight; a rotation out of cyclical momentum would hit Maersk regardless of fundamentals.
  • Sell-side: about 60% of analysts rate it Sell or Underweight and the mean target is 13% below the price; a first big FY2027 cut by an influential bear (Morgan Stanley, Bernstein, DNB Carnegie) could start a de-rating. Earlier history: short interest reached 33% of free float in August 2025, so the stock is prone to violent positioning moves in both directions.
  • Event risk at results: on 5 November 2026 a guidance raise smaller than consensus implies (see C-68) could be "sold"; in February 2027 the first FY2027 guidance, likely conservative, is a high-risk moment.
  • Macro: French and Italian sovereign stress, credit widening, the US 10-year at 5.10% and oil at 111 all feed the risk-off regime.

What it means for the model: not a model input. It means the set four analyst should expect the price to react to the supply narrative before the earnings change.

6e

Track record (guidance raise/sell-side upgrades) ~L2-3Y

4 answers

C-61How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?F5017th of 19full answer under 4b Management quality
C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

D5510th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Under Clerc, Maersk is a consistent sandbagger in rising-rate environments: it sets conservative initial guidance and upgrades it mid-year, often by ad hoc announcement. The upgrades are marks-to-market of freight rates, not the product of internal improvement. In falling-rate environments the record is soft guidance and misses.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Raises: in 2024 (Red Sea, several times), on 7 August 2025, and twice in 2026 (29 June, ad hoc, and 13 August), with the EBITDA midpoint doubling from 5.75bn to 11.5bn. DNB Carnegie: Clerc "consistently under-promises and over-delivers"; the Change Forces digest classifies management as a "sandbagger" archetype.
  • Initial bars are set low: in February 2026 Maersk guided to an EBIT loss and about −1.5bn of FCF.
  • Downturn behaviour: in February 2024, "Q4 miss. Soft guide. Buy back suspended" (Morgan Stanley); in 2015, before Clerc, profit guidance was cut by more than 15% in the rate collapse.
  • Clerc's tone is measured, not promotional ("we're going to manage it with a keen eye on the bottom line").

What it means for the model: treat initial annual guidance as a conservative floor in up-markets and as roughly realistic or still conservative in down-markets. The upgrade pattern carries no information about structural improvement.

C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

D5014th of 19

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: Maersk beats when rates surprise upward and misses when they surprise downward. Its beats are exogenous, not executional. In conditions comparable to the next 18 months (falling rates into a supply wave: 2015-2016 and 2023), the record is misses and cuts.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Last year: Q4 2025 in line ("in-line quarter, broadly in-line guide", Bernstein); Q1 2026 beat on EBITDA (+5.9%) and EBIT (+36.8%) but missed on net profit (−67.5%, minorities and financial items) and EPS (4 against 27); Q2 2026 a large beat (EBITDA +44%, EBIT 1,571m against 540m, loaded rate 15% above consensus); FY2026 guidance raised twice.
  • Three years: 2024 raised several times (Red Sea); 2025 raised in August; 2023 ended with a Q4 miss, soft 2024 guidance and a suspended buyback (February 2024).
  • Five years: 2021-2022 huge beats (pandemic); 2023 normalisation with misses at the turn.
  • Comparable conditions: 2015 profit guidance cut by more than 15%; Q4 2023 miss.
  • At net-profit level, minorities and financial items add noise even when EBIT beats.

What it means for the model: past beats say nothing about 2027 delivery; delivery depends on the rate assumption. Expect misses against consensus in quarters where Suez capacity returns faster than modelled.

P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

F3516th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: The record says Maersk's delivery is the freight rate's delivery: it beats when rates surprise upward and misses when they surprise downward, and its guidance is a conservative mark-to-market in rising markets and soft in falling ones. Neither management's record nor the sell-side's tells us much about 2027-2028 that the rate path does not. So the forecasts should carry weight in proportion to how much of each year's rate is already visible. FY2026 deserves high weight (one quarter of genuine uncertainty; our EBITDA range about 11.6-13.2bn). FY2027 deserves moderate-to-low weight on level (EBITDA cone of about 3.7-13bn around our 7.4bn) but high weight on direction. FY2028-FY2030 deserve low weight on level and moderate weight on shape. Against consensus, lean every year from FY2026 to FY2029 down, with the largest relative lean at net income and EPS rather than at EBITDA. Against our own base case, the skew is two-sided but not symmetric: the downside (a 2016-type price war) is more probable, the upside (renewed disruption) is larger and faster.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Company record in comparable conditions. Falling-rate years: 2015 profit guidance cut by more than 15%; Q4 2023 a miss with soft 2024 guidance and the buyback suspended (February 2024). Rising-rate years: 2024 raised several times, August 2025 raised, 2026 raised twice (EBITDA midpoint from 5.75bn to 11.5bn) and probably again on 5 November. Management's informal commentary ("structurally higher rate level", landside bottlenecks supporting rates) has been more right than the bears since 2024 but contradicts its own 500k TEU order.
  • Sell-side record. Estimates follow spot: FY2027 EPS was revised +459% in three months and the mean was negative three to six months ago. In 2023 consensus was too high at the turn. The dispersion is the signal: FY2027 EPS ranges from -22 (Jyske) to 497 (Barclays), FY2028 from -383 to 294.
  • Where the company's own record should move our numbers:
  • Cost and network lines: Gemini delivered 950m against an 820m-1.1bn range and Ocean unit cost at fixed bunker fell 7% in Q1 2026. Lean self-help slightly up (we include about 0.8bn by FY2028; 1.1-1.3bn is plausible if losses deepen).
  • Terminals: ROIC 16% against a 9% target, records every quarter. High weight; lean neutral.
  • Logistics & Services: the 6% EBIT margin and 10% organic growth targets have been missed for years; the margin component of the 2025 incentive scored zero. Lean down from management's target; our 5.4-5.8% already does so.
  • Ocean volume: Maersk grew 9.3% in Q1 2026 by pricing spot aggressively to fill Gemini, and lost share structurally for eight years. Lean volume slightly below market in FY2027-FY2028.
  • Below EBIT: Q1 2026 net profit missed consensus by 67.5% on minorities and financial items while EBIT beat by 37%. Tax, minorities and financial items are where errors concentrate in low-profit years; weight EBITDA and EBIT forecasts above EPS forecasts.
  • Quarterly lean for the next 18 months. Q3 2026: risk to our 4.45bn EBITDA is slightly upward (realised rates could exceed our 3,230 if contract repricing caught up fully with Q2 spot). Q4 2026 and H1 2027: risk is downward against consensus and roughly balanced against our numbers. H2 2027: balanced against ours, with the price-war tail.
  • Probability framing (our judgement). Base case about 50%; a deeper case (rates 10-15% below our path in 2027-2028; FY2028 EPS of about -250 to -300) about 30%; a disruption-extended case (FY2027 rates near 2,600; FY2027 EPS about 400-450) about 20%.

What it means for the model: use our base case for the central forecasts, present FY2027 as a wide range keyed to the rate, and treat consensus as too high in every year to FY2029, most of all below EBIT.

Financial forecasts

F2116th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts are negative for an investment case on every horizon beyond the next quarter, and the reasons connect. The external picture from the first set (the largest supply wave on record arriving as the Red Sea buffer unwinds, in an industry with no exit discipline) translates through extreme price leverage into Ocean EBITDA falling from 8.6bn in FY2026 to 2.3bn in FY2028, and through a fixed depreciation charge into Ocean EBIT losses from Q2 2027 to FY2030. The genuinely good parts of Maersk (Terminals, Gemini cost, a strong balance sheet) cushion the fall but do not change its direction: non-Ocean EBIT of 2.3-2.9bn and self-help of about 0.8bn hold group EBIT just above zero. Below EBIT, the items that flattered EPS for five years (interest income, low tax rates, buybacks) reverse at the same time, so EPS falls further than any operating line and is negative in FY2028-FY2029, while company-basis free cash flow is negative for four years and the balance sheet moves to modest net debt including leases. Our base case is below consensus in every year (EBITDA by 12-17%; EPS by 25% in FY2026 and by far more thereafter), and consensus is not a floor: the bearishness of the sell-side lives in its ratings, not in its FY2026-FY2027 numbers. The main counterweight is the speed with which disruption can return; it would delay this path, not cancel it. For the third-set analyst, the case is highly concentrated (one variable, the Ocean rate), its direction is highly predictable, its timing within 2027 is moderately predictable, and its level is poorly predictable: each 100 per FFE is worth about 95 USD of EPS against a base-case FY2027 EPS of 43.

C-49What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?F2019th of 19full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F2516th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Over 2019-2025 the items below operating profit and the buyback were a tailwind to EPS: interest income on a large cash pile, currency and derivative gains (510m of trading-derivative gains in FY2025), tax rates of 2.4-14.7%, and a share count down 27%. Over 2027-2030 they turn into a headwind of about 50-80 USD per share a year: a tax charge of about 0.4-0.5bn that does not fall with profit (about 30 per share), net interest and other financial items turning negative (10-42 per share), minorities of about 0.1bn (7-8 per share), and no buyback. Only two items help: the vessel-life depreciation benefit (0.7bn, about 49 per share, almost untaxed) and joint-venture income of about 0.4bn. Together they explain why our EPS is close to zero or negative in FY2028-FY2029 even with group EBIT positive, why net income falls further than EBIT, and why the gap to consensus is wider at EPS (-25% in FY2026, then -66% to -176%) than at EBITDA (-12% to -17%).

Item by item:

  • Depreciation and the vessel-life change. The change from 20 to 25 years lowered depreciation by 175m a quarter (351m in H1 2026) and about 700m a year. It persists: the benefit on the existing fleet decays only slowly as ships pass the age at which they would have been fully depreciated under the old policy. We disagree with the AlphaSense EPS-bridge report's claim that newbuild depreciation will "completely reverse" it by 2030: the 26 dual-fuel ships (about 5.5-6bn) add their own depreciation of roughly 0.2-0.25bn a year from 2029-2030, which is a separate, additive charge. Total D&A: 6.25bn (FY2026), 6.45bn, 6.55bn, 6.75bn, 7.0bn. Without the life change, FY2028 EPS would be about -96 instead of -47.
  • Joint ventures and associates (inside EBIT). About 0.37-0.46bn a year, mostly terminal joint ventures such as Tanjung Pelepas (APM Terminals 30%; record 14m TEU in 2025). Stable; slightly lower in FY2027 as congestion income fades.
  • Net interest and other financial items: +0.05bn (FY2026), -0.15bn, -0.35bn, -0.50bn, -0.60bn (see P-02). FX and derivative swings (+595m translation and 510m derivative gains in FY2025; dividend-hedge losses in H1 2026) add noise of a few hundred million either way; we assume nil.
  • Tax: about 1.0bn (FY2026; H1 already about 0.58bn), then 0.45bn, 0.40bn, 0.42bn, 0.48bn. Ocean earnings sit under tonnage tax, so Ocean losses give no tax relief, while Terminals and Logistics & Services profits (about 2.6-3.2bn of EBIT) are taxed normally, and Pillar Two adds a top-up (40m in FY2025, higher in Q2 2026). The FY2019 precedent (377m of tax on 657m of pre-tax profit) shows the charge is effectively fixed at the trough. A German tax audit on the allocation of shipping income is a further, unquantified risk.
  • Minorities: about 0.17bn (FY2026) and 0.10-0.12bn thereafter (for example Gujarat Pipavav, in which APM Terminals holds 43%). Minority-held terminal businesses stay profitable when Ocean loses money, so the leakage rises as a share of a shrinking total.
  • One-off items (excluded from underlying): restructuring of about 0.2bn in 2026; a probable Ocean impairment at the FY2027 test; possible logistics goodwill impairment. These hit reported EPS and equity, not our underlying EPS.
  • Share count and buyback: about 1bn of buybacks in FY2026 (about 2% of shares); January 2027 tail only, then nothing in our base case; average shares 14.55m (FY2026), then 14.34m flat. The Foundation sells pro rata, so control is unaffected either way.
  • Dividend: 40% of underlying net profit, as for FY2025 (DKK 480 a share, 1.1bn): about USD 150 a share for FY2026 (about DKK 1,000; 2.15bn paid in March 2027), about 17 for FY2027, nil for FY2028 and FY2029 under the payout policy (a token dividend is possible because the family owner values dividends), about 21 for FY2030. Consensus FY2026 DPS of 186 assumes higher earnings, not a higher payout.

Scenarios around the base case (our judgement):

  • Deeper (about 30%): a 2016-type price war as MSC and CMA CGM fill new tonnage, with full Suez normalisation by early 2027. FY2027 rate about 1,800, FY2028 about 1,650; group EBITDA about 3.7bn and 3.1bn; FY2028 EPS about -250 to -300; net cash excluding leases near zero by 2029; an Ocean impairment certain.
  • Disruption-extended (about 20%): a renewed Red Sea closure or Hormuz escalation re-absorbs 5-10% of capacity. FY2027 rate about 2,600; EBITDA about 13bn; EPS about 400-450; the downturn shifts to 2028-2029 rather than disappearing, because the orderbook still delivers.

What would change the base case, and when it will be visible: the Q3 2026 realised rate and Q4 commentary (5 November 2026); the pace of Suez returns by Gemini and by the holdouts (Evergreen, HMM, Yang Ming, ONE); Asia-Europe contract settlements (December 2026 to February 2027); the FY2027 guidance and the buyback decision (early February 2027); idle capacity rising above 3-4% and scrapping above 1% of the fleet a year (the sign the trough is forming); Brent, which moves both cost and recovery.

C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

F4519th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The scope is modest, it sits in two areas, and it is congruent with the strategy. Terminals: EBITDA of about 2.0bn growing about 5-7% a year on capacity (+30%), pricing and Gemini hub volumes, roughly +0.3-0.4bn of EBITDA by 2029. Logistics & Services: margin from 5.1% toward the 6% target on about 17-18bn of revenue (+0.15-0.2bn of EBIT), plus a Solutions recovery from 1.7% (+0.1-0.2bn). Smaller items: customs and digital services, dual-fuel carbon cost advantages as EU ETS and FuelEU tighten, interest income. Total: perhaps 0.5-0.8bn of EBIT by 2029 (our estimate), against an Ocean swing that is an order of magnitude larger.

Peer precedents: DSV's integration record (synergies averaging 29% above target across its last three deals) shows logistics margins can be lifted, but Maersk's own record (10% growth and 6% margin targets missed for years) says slowly. Hapag-Lloyd's Ventus programme (1.1bn of run-rate savings) and Strategy 2030 (20% unit cost reduction against 2022) show carriers can cut cost, which Gemini already delivered for Maersk.

What it means for the model: add the Terminals and Logistics & Services improvements gradually; do not count on them to change the 2027-2028 group trajectory.

C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

F2518th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: The reported growth engine has not decelerated. It has accelerated: revenue growth went from −2.6% in Q1 2026 to +20% in Q2, and consensus FY2026 growth is +18% against a 2019-2025 average of about 5.6% a year. But the input force that produced the acceleration (capacity absorbed by route disruption) is slowing fast: Suez transits doubled between Q1 2026 and October, and six Gemini services are back on the Suez route. The opposing input (fleet supply) is accelerating. This is the classic gap between a lagging output and a turning input. Reported deceleration should appear from Q4 2026, and revenue should decline in FY2027 (consensus −7.5%).

What it means for the model: do not extrapolate Q2-Q3 2026; turn the rate path down from Q4 2026.

Credibility:

  • FY2026: needs H2 EBITDA of about 9.3bn (Q3 2024-peak run-rate in both quarters). Q3 is plausible; Q4 is a stretch. Achievable to slightly high.
  • FY2027: EBITDA of 8.7bn is below 2023 and 2025 (both 9.6bn) and implies Ocean EBIT of about −0.3 to −0.5bn. That is consistent with a gradual Suez normalisation and 7.4% fleet growth. If normalisation is fast, the Q4 2023 run-rate (839m a quarter of group EBITDA, before Gemini and Terminals growth) points to downside of several billion. If disruption persists, upside of 3-5bn. Roughly balanced, with a negative skew.
  • FY2028: EBIT of 0.8bn includes about 0.7bn of depreciation benefit, so the economic EBIT is about zero. Ocean EBITDA of 4.3bn is a moderate trough, not a 2016-style one. Downside if rates revisit the 2016 lows (DNB Carnegie). The broker spread is huge (FY2028 EBITDA from 1.5bn to 10.2bn; EPS from −383 to +294).
  • Revenue: FY2028 Ocean revenue consensus (35.7bn) is above FY2023 (32.1bn) and FY2025 (33.0bn), which implies rates near 2025 levels. Once the Cape surcharges (about 6bn a year) go, revenue is likely too high even where EBITDA is right.
  • Non-Ocean EBITDA of about 4bn is credible (Terminals growing; Logistics & Services improving).
  • Cash: consensus OCF ≈ EBITDA assumes neutral working capital. Lease repayments (about 4.5-5bn) are missing from FactSet FCF, so company-basis FCF is negative in FY2027-2028 (Morgan Stanley agrees).
P-04After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?F2516th of 16full answer under 5a Pricing power
P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F2016th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: The net impact is a step down to a lower and flatter earnings level than any period since 2019, concentrated in one driver and amplified by the items below operating profit. Over FY2027-FY2030, average revenue (about 54bn) is roughly equal to the FY2023-FY2025 average (53.5bn), but average EBITDA (7.3bn) is about 30% lower than FY2023-FY2025 (10.4bn on the brokers' basis), average EBIT (1.1bn) about 70% lower (3.8bn), and average EPS (about 9) about 97% lower (262). Essentially all of the EBITDA difference comes from the Ocean base freight rate (net of pass-through surcharges); cost inflation and lost hub income roughly cancel against self-help, congestion relief and non-Ocean growth. Between EBITDA and EPS, the swing in tax, financial items and buybacks takes away a further 30-60 USD per share a year. Against the 2016 precedent, the trough is deeper in EBIT terms (because of lease depreciation and lower fuel-adjusted rates) but the balance sheet is far stronger. The only lines that improve on history are Terminals EBITDA (2.15-2.55bn against about 1.9bn in FY2025) and Logistics & Services margins (5.4-5.8% against 4.8%).

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Revenue: lifted in FY2026 (+16.4%) by rates and surcharges, then down 16.6% in FY2027 as rates fall and surcharges and hub income leave; flat in FY2028; growing 5-7% in FY2029-FY2030 on volume (Maersk's newbuilds) and modest rate recovery. FY2027-FY2028 revenue is about 10-11% below consensus, mainly because consensus keeps "other including hubs" revenue and the freight rate near 2025 levels.
  • EBITDA: 12.35bn, 7.42bn, 6.32bn, 7.13bn, 8.50bn; margins 19.7%, 14.2%, 12.3%, 13.2%, 14.6% against 17.7-21.9% in FY2023-FY2025.
  • EBIT: 6.50bn, 1.34bn, 0.17bn, 0.81bn, 1.96bn. In FY2028 the vessel-life benefit (0.7bn) is more than four times group EBIT.
  • Net income and EPS: 5.38bn (EPS 370), 0.62bn (43), -0.68bn (-47), -0.22bn (-15), 0.76bn (53).
  • Cash: company-basis free cash flow +2.0bn, then -2.0bn, -2.7bn, -2.2bn, -0.6bn; capex 4.8-5.6bn a year (the 2029-2030 newbuild instalments and the 3bn Terminals programme keep it high); lease repayments 3.5-3.9bn.
  • How concentrated the drivers are (for D-03). The FY2026-to-FY2028 EBITDA decline of 6.0bn breaks down as: Ocean base-rate erosion (the rate fall net of pass-through surcharges and fuel recovery) about -6.3bn; lost hub and other Ocean revenue net of its cost about -0.7bn; cost inflation about -0.8bn; offset by congestion-cost relief +0.7bn, Ocean self-help +0.7bn, volume +0.15bn and Terminals and Logistics growth +0.25bn. One variable carries the case; the others roughly cancel.

What it means for the model: a lower plateau, not a V: FY2028 and FY2029 are both trough years, and FY2030 recovers only to roughly FY2027 levels.

Forecasts and upgrades

8

Probably upgrades this financial year to

–not yet rated

Memo topic 7, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

F308th of 8

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Guidance, consensus and the narrative reflect our assessment in direction and not in level, and the difference between the two is where the next 18 months of news will come from. On guidance: FY2026 will be raised tomorrow to a range that sits between the current top (12.5bn) and consensus (14.0bn), which our revised figure of about 12.8bn matches; FY2027 will be guided in February 2027 wide and low, at or below our 7.4bn and well below the 8.7bn consensus, with negative free cash flow and, more likely than not, no new buyback; the mid-term targets will be missed, not raised. On consensus: estimates sit at the peak of a spot-driven upgrade cycle (FY2027 EPS +459% in three months) that reverses from the Asia-Europe contract season and the February guidance; the cuts are largest where the narrative is thinnest, below EBIT, and every year from FY2027 to FY2029 is cut in the base and deeper cases, which together carry 80% of the probability. On the narrative: the sell-side has articulated the supply wave, the Suez return, the orderbook, the fleet order and the landside debate completely, and the ratings (8% Buy) and the shape of consensus show it; what it has not articulated, except at DNB Carnegie and in pieces, is the stacking of the tax floor, the interest decay, the depreciation benefit and the buyback end that makes trough EPS negative, nor the impairment, the fixed lease and fuel commitments or the quarterly shape of 2027. Investors who own the shares at 0.8x book and the 100th percentile of the P/E range are positioned for the disruption-extended scenario; the price, not the sell-side, is where our assessment is least reflected. For an investment case in the company the gaps are unfavourable: the near-term favourable gap (a guidance raise) is expected and closes tomorrow; the 18-month gaps (guidance below consensus, consensus cut, the below-EBIT narrative arriving) all close downward; and the only favourable gap, a 2029-2030 recovery narrative built on scrapping and the end of the delivery wave, lies beyond the horizon in which the memo expects the price to find its trough.

Guidance

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

F408th of 8

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: Over the next 18 months there are six earnings events (5 November 2026; early February 2027; May 2027; August 2027; November 2027; early February 2028) and, under Danish rules, any number of ad hoc changes between them. My expectation, with probabilities, is: a raise tomorrow (about 85%), to a FY2026 range whose midpoint sits below the 14.0bn consensus (about 70% conditional on a raise); a first FY2027 guidance in February 2027 that is set wide and low, below consensus and at or below our 7.4bn midpoint (about 80%), accompanied by negative company-basis free cash flow guidance and, more likely than not, no new buyback; a FY2027 range that is held in May 2027 (Q1 2027 will still show year-on-year growth because Q1 2026 was a depressed base); and a 2027 outcome that is "met within a low range" in the base case (about 50%), cut in August or November 2027 in the deeper case (about 30%), or raised mid-year in the disruption-extended case (about 20%). The pattern that governs all six events is the one both earlier sets described: Maersk marks guidance to spot rates, sets initial ranges that are extraordinarily wide and low when the outlook is uncertain (1-6bn in February 2024; an EBIT loss in February 2026), and lets the market discover the rest. In the secondary question, the mid-term targets will not be raised; ROIC above 7.5% through the cycle and the Logistics & Services 6% margin will be missed through the horizon and are candidates for reframing at a capital-markets event under the new CFO, while the Terminals ROIC target will be met; the fleet "target" of 4.1-4.3m TEU has already been abandoned in practice.

Event by event:

1. 5 November 2026 (Q3 2026 results). Guidance on 13 August: underlying EBITDA 10.5-12.5bn, underlying EBIT 4.5-6.5bn, company free cash flow above zero. H1 underlying EBITDA was 4.74bn, so the range implies H2 of 5.8-7.8bn (2.9-3.9bn a quarter), while the 14.0bn consensus needs about 9.3bn (4.6bn a quarter). The evidence since the second set was written says Q3 was at least as strong as the second set's 4.45bn and probably stronger: COSCO's Q3 net profit rose 81% year on year; HMM's Q3 operating profit beat consensus by 23-42% on container rates up 44%; Evergreen's and Yang Ming's September revenue rose 64% and 70%; ZIM raised its 2026 EBITDA range by 30% at the midpoint in October; Hapag-Lloyd raised on 28 September (described by DNB Carnegie as a catch-up to Maersk's August raise); NYK lifted its full-year forecast by 56%; the CCFI, the blended index, was +89% year on year on 23 October against a Q4 2025 Maersk loaded rate of 2,046; Deutsche Bank raised its FY2026 EBITDA to 14.4bn on 3 November expecting "another guidance revision"; Bernstein's 25 September call feedback said an upgrade "looks likely". I lift our Q3 to about 4.7bn (revenue +27%, margin 26%) and Q4 to about 3.3bn (revenue +24%, margin 20%), which puts FY2026 at about 12.8bn, above the top of the current range. On that basis management must raise or at least lift the bottom: the most likely new range is about 12-13.5bn (midpoint about 12.75bn) with EBIT of about 6-7.5bn. Drivers of a larger raise (to a midpoint of 13.5bn or more, about 25%): a Q3 above 5bn on full catch-up of contract rates to Q2 spot and congestion-driven storage income; drivers of no raise (about 15%): a cautious reading of the Q4 transpacific slide (Morgan Stanley, 3 November: the peak season is close to an end, with discounted spot on US routes) and the Suez re-entry. Outcome for the market: the FY2026 number will land between guidance and consensus; the market reaction will turn on Q4 and 2027 commentary, where management's known optimism on landside constraints is likely to be restated and the sell-side's known scepticism restated in reply. I judge the event neutral on numbers and two-sided on price, with "sell the news" the slightly more probable reaction after +80%.

2. Early February 2027 (FY2026 results; first FY2027 guidance; capital allocation). This is the event that matters. Precedents for first-year guidance in a falling market: February 2016 (an underlying result "significantly below" 2015, a 50% dividend cut, no new buyback); February 2019 (EBITDA guided about 20% below consensus, per Jefferies); February 2024 (EBITDA 1-6bn against a 9.6bn prior year, buyback suspended, dividend from DKK 4,300 to 515). In each, the initial range was set below consensus and proved either right (2016) or conservative (2019, 2024). For FY2027 consensus is EBITDA 8.7bn, EBIT 2.05bn and EPS 125; our base case is 7.4bn, 1.34bn and 43. I expect a range of roughly 5-8bn of EBITDA (the width Maersk uses when it cannot see the rate), an EBIT range straddling zero, negative company-basis free cash flow, capex of about 5-6bn, and a market-volume assumption of 2-4%. That is below consensus by 10-30% at the midpoint and roughly at our base case. Drivers of a higher range (about 20%): a December-January Asia-Europe contract season that settles near 2026 levels (COSCO's expectation that its 2027 long-term rates land higher year on year is the live signal), a renewed Red Sea closure, or congestion persisting through the winter. Drivers of a lower range (about 25%): Asia-Europe contracts settling 30% or more below 2026 (DNB Carnegie's reading of industry expectations), the last Gemini services returning to Suez by March, or an early price response from MSC and CMA CGM to fill tonnage. Capital allocation: dividend at 40% of underlying net profit (about USD 160 a share, about DKK 1,050, on my revised FY2026 figure; consensus 186 on higher earnings); buyback not renewed in the base case (60-65%), a token programme in the alternative; a possible first Ocean impairment flag in the annual report's headroom disclosure (headroom was 1.1bn at end-2025; the FY2026 test will use a 2026 base and should pass; the risk is the FY2027 test a year later).

3. May 2027 (Q1 2027). Q1 2027 revenue still grows about 7% year on year on our path because Q1 2026 was priced at 2,081; the realised rate (about 2,400) will be visibly down from Q4 2026, but the guidance range will be wide enough to hold. Probability of holding: about 75%; of a cut to the lower half: about 15% (if the Transpacific season opens weak and Suez is fully normalised); of a raise: about 10%.

4. August 2027 (Q2 2027). The first quarter of year-on-year decline (about -16% on revenue), the quarter in which both contract seasons have reset and the 2027 deliveries (3.29m TEU for the year) are arriving. In the base case the range is held or narrowed toward the midpoint (about 50%); in the deeper case it is cut (about 30%), probably by ad hoc statement before the results, as in June 2026 but in the opposite direction; in the disruption-extended case it is raised (about 20%). The self-help announcements the second set expects from H2 2027 (blank sailings, charter redeliveries, a cost programme) would accompany a cut and would be read by the market as confirmation of the downturn, not as mitigation.

5. November 2027 (Q3 2027) and early February 2028 (FY2027 results; first FY2028 guidance). By November 2027 the 2028 delivery peak (5.23m TEU) is in view and scrapping is still negligible; a Q3 2027 in line with a lowered range is the base case. February 2028 is likely to bring FY2028 guidance of an EBIT loss or a range straddling zero, a probable Ocean impairment (non-cash, outside underlying results), a dividend at or near nil (the second set's base case) and, in the deeper case, a dividend cut of the 2016 kind (50%) or suspension. The probability that FY2028 guidance is set below the then-prevailing consensus is high (about 70%), because consensus FY2028 EBIT of 0.8bn consists almost entirely of the vessel-life depreciation benefit and assumes Ocean EBITDA of 4.3bn against our 2.3bn.

The secondary question: targets and ambitions 18 months to three years out.

  • ROIC above 7.5% through the cycle: 5.7% in 2025; about 9% in 2026; 1-3% or lower in 2027-2030 on our inputs. Will be missed over any window that includes 2027-2028. Likelihood of being raised: nil; of being reframed or dropped (for example at a capital-markets day under CFO Erni, who joined in February 2026): moderate (about 40% within 18 months). A reframing would be read as a reduction.
  • Logistics & Services EBIT margin above 6% and organic growth above 10%: missed for years (4.6-4.8%; 3.2% growth in 2025); our path reaches 5.8% in 2029. Bernstein calls the pair unrealistic and notes that 6% is above best-in-class for most asset-light products. Likelihood of being met within three years: low (about 20%); of being reset lower or re-based on the new Forwarding/Solutions/Landside structure: moderate.
  • Terminals ROIC above 9%: 14.8% in Q2 2026; will be met every year of the horizon, with the 2016 precedent (6.3%) as the floor in a deep downturn. Likelihood of being raised: low; it does not need to be.
  • Fleet of 4.1-4.3m TEU: operated capacity 4.67m TEU; 483,600 TEU on order for 2029-2030. Already abandoned in practice; a formal restatement to a growth target is likely at any strategy update (about 60%). In the memo's terms this is a reduction in capital discipline rather than a raise in ambition.
  • FY2026-2027 capex of 10-11bn: will be met (H1 2026 actual 1.9bn; back-loaded yard instalments keep 2027 inside the range). Beyond 2027 there is no capex guidance; the 14.6bn of commitments, 9.3bn of uncommenced leases and 5.9bn of fuel offtakes say the outer years will not be guided lower.
  • Net zero by 2040 and the Gemini reliability standard: will be reaffirmed; neither moves earnings within three years.

What would change these probabilities, in order of weight: the Q4 2026 exit rate disclosed tomorrow (above about 3,000 per FFE shifts weight toward a higher FY2027 range; below about 2,700 toward a lower one); the Asia-Europe contract settlements by January; the number of Gemini services still on the Cape in March 2027; idle capacity rising above 3-4% of the fleet (the first sign that carriers are withdrawing, which stabilises rates sooner); and any Red Sea or Hormuz escalation, which resets everything within weeks.

The gap between our assessment and what guidance reflects: for FY2026 our view (about 12.8bn) is above the top of current guidance, so the near-term gap is favourable and will close tomorrow; for FY2027 there is no guidance, and our view is that it will be set at or below our base case, so the guidance the market will see in February 2027 will be more negative than the consensus the market holds today. On an 18-month view the gap is unfavourable for a long case.
