P-09After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?F2016th of 16full answer under 7b Margins/growth vs. past
I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

F278th of 8

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: Consensus is at the top of an upgrade cycle that has been the largest in the European market (FY2027 EPS +459% in three months; FY2026 EPS from a mean of -55 to +487 within a year) and that has been driven entirely by spot rates persisting longer than the brokers assumed. The next 18 months reverse it. I expect: FY2026 consensus EBITDA roughly held after 5 November (about 14bn against our 12.8bn, with the median drifting down 3-7% as the new guidance range brackets below it, and dispersion narrowing as the 11-13bn and 15-18bn outliers converge); FY2027 consensus EBITDA held or nudged up after 5 November on the Q4 carry-over, then cut by 15-25% after the February 2027 guidance (from 8.7bn toward 6.5-7.5bn), with EPS cut by more (from 125 toward 30-70) as brokers finally model the tax floor, the interest decay and the end of the buyback; FY2028 consensus (EBITDA 7.5bn, EPS 62) cut through 2027 as contracts reset and deliveries arrive, toward 5.5-6.5bn and an EPS near zero or negative; and the net breadth of revisions, currently overwhelmingly positive, turning net negative between December 2026 (contract-season news) and February 2027 (guidance), then staying negative through 2027 in the base and deeper cases. The secondary answer is the same in direction: FY2029 consensus rests on two brokers (EBITDA 8.6bn, EPS 119) and will be cut as coverage broadens to a year of continued trough, while any FY2030 numbers that appear will be set at a recovery level that is probably about right in level and early in timing. Only the disruption-extended scenario (about 20%) keeps revisions positive into mid-2027, and even then the outer years are cut once the orderbook's arithmetic reasserts itself.

Where consensus stands against our view, and why it will move:

  • FY2026 (consensus EBITDA 14.0bn, EBIT 8.1bn, EPS 490; ours now about 12.8bn, 6.9bn and about 400). The gap is a H2 run-rate question: consensus needs about 4.6bn a quarter; our revised path has 4.7bn in Q3 and 3.3bn in Q4. The Q3 print will validate the upper half of consensus and the Q4 exit rate will undermine its lower half; a guidance range of 12-13.5bn will pull the highest estimates (UBS at 18.2bn, Goldman Sachs at 15.6bn) down and the lowest (Sydbank 11.8bn, Deutsche Bank's former 11.5bn, since raised to 14.4bn) up. Net: a median between 13 and 14bn, i.e. a small cut or a hold. EPS consensus of 490 is further above our 400 than EBITDA is, because the brokers' tax and financial-item lines are generous; the first Q1 2026-style miss below EBIT is the risk.
  • FY2027 (consensus EBITDA 8.7bn, EBIT 2.05bn, EPS 125 median and 172 mean; ours 7.4bn, 1.34bn and 43). The median EBITDA is below 2023 and 2025 (both 9.6bn) and implies Ocean EBIT of about -0.3 to -0.5bn, so it already contains a downturn; what it does not contain is the speed (our H1 2027 realised rates of 2,400 and 2,100) or the below-EBIT squeeze. The brokers who will move the median are the ones at 10-13bn (Clarksons 13.1bn, Goldman 11.2bn, UBS 10.4bn, Fearnley 10.6bn, one restricted house at 10.4bn), whose numbers assume disruption-level rates through 2027; the February guidance will force them down, and the bears (DNB Carnegie 7.8bn, Morgan Stanley 7.3bn, Deutsche Bank 7.5bn, Jyske 5.1bn) will not move up. EPS: the mean of 172 sits 38% above the median of 125 because of the same bulls; the median is itself above our 43 by about 80 a share, of which about 30 is tax, 15-20 is net financial items and buyback accretion, and the rest is EBITDA. DNB Carnegie is the only house whose below-EBIT lines resemble ours (a flat 480m tax charge; net financial items turning to an expense); its FY2028 EPS of -116 is the one consensus input that already reflects the squeeze.
  • FY2028 (consensus EBITDA 7.5bn, EBIT 0.8bn, EPS 62; ours 6.3bn, 0.17bn and -47). Consensus EBIT consists almost entirely of the vessel-life depreciation benefit (about 0.7bn), so the economic consensus is break-even; the broker spread (EBITDA 1.5-10.2bn; EPS -383 to +294) is the widest of any year. Our 6.3bn sits below the median because our trough rate (1,905 per FFE, about 1,400 fuel-adjusted) is set at the marginal carrier's cash cost; DNB Carnegie (4.4bn) is below us. The cut here comes in two steps: when FY2027 guidance resets the base in February 2027, and when the 2027 contract seasons and the delivery schedule become 2028 budgets in Q4 2027. Revenue consensus (57.3bn against our 51.5bn) is the line most certainly too high, because consensus keeps "other including hubs" revenue (3.85bn) and freight revenue near 2025 levels while the Cape and bunker surcharges (about 290 per FFE) and congestion-linked hub income leave.
  • FY2029 and beyond (two brokers: revenue 60.1bn, EBITDA 8.6bn, EBIT 2.4bn, EPS 119; ours 54.2bn, 7.1bn, 0.8bn and -15). The two visible estimates assume the recovery begins in 2029; our loop analysis (scrapping peaking 2028-2029, tightening in 2030) says 2029 is a second trough year. As the 18 houses roll their models forward, the FY2029 median will form well below 8.6bn, probably between 6.5 and 7.5bn, which will register as a cut even though no individual broker has reduced a number. The Consumer should not describe the FY2029 consensus as a view; it is two brokers' extrapolation.

The breadth of revisions, and when it turns. Today: FY2027 EPS +128% over one month, +459% over three; EBITDA +19% and +52%; sales +6% and +12%; target prices +10% and +41%; Buy share 8% and unchanged; the dashboard's revision-momentum score 13 and a pinned sell-side earnings-momentum rating of A. This is the signature the first set's digests call "estimates rising on stretched valuations across the group": the peak. The turn comes when the first FY2027 number is cut by an influential house. The candidates and their triggers, in likely order: Deutsche Bank and DNB Carnegie after the Asia-Europe contract settlements in December-January (both have explicitly tied their 2027 numbers to that season); Morgan Stanley on its Suez transit tracker reaching about 70-80% (it has already warned of 50% EBITDA declines in 2027 and negative free cash flow); Bernstein after the February guidance (its FY2026-2027 EPS has moved by hundreds of dollars within weeks in both directions this year, so it is the most reactive); the bulls (Clarksons, Barclays, Goldman Sachs, UBS) only on the guidance itself. Probability that net revisions to FY2027 are negative over the three months to end-February 2027: about 75%; over the twelve months to November 2027: about 85%. The exception is a renewed disruption, which would keep revisions positive and widen the already-extreme dispersion further.

Ratings and target prices move differently from estimates. With about 60% of analysts at Sell or Underweight and a mean target 13% below the price, there is little room for ratings to deteriorate; what will happen instead is that targets, which have been raised 41% in three months to follow the price, stop rising when estimates turn, and the gap between price and target widens through the price falling rather than the target. A rating upgrade cycle would need the price to fall to the brokers' trough values first (DNB Carnegie's bear case DKK 15,500; Bernstein's targets around DKK 10,700 earlier in 2026). The Buy share of 8% is therefore not a contrarian signal yet; it will become one at a lower price and after the FY2027-2028 cuts, which is the first set's point that the bearishness lives in the ratings and the FY2028 numbers, not in FY2027.

What would make consensus move up instead (the 20% case), and how far: a Red Sea or Hormuz escalation re-absorbing 5-10% of capacity, or congestion persisting through the winter at the August-October level, would hold Q4 2026 and H1 2027 rates near 2,600-2,900 per FFE; FY2027 consensus EBITDA would move toward 11-13bn (the Clarksons and Goldman numbers) and EPS toward 300-450, and the Buy share would rise as the bears capitulated on timing, as they did in 2024 and 2025. The orderbook would then push the cuts into FY2028-FY2029 rather than cancel them.

The gap between our assessment and what consensus reflects: our EBITDA is below consensus by about 9% in FY2026 (after my revision), 15% in FY2027, 16% in FY2028 and 17% in FY2029, and our EPS by about 18%, 65%, 175% and 112%. Consensus is not a floor in any year, the revisions that will close the gap are cuts, and the only year in which consensus and our view are close (FY2026) is the one that is nearly over. The breadth of revisions is at its most positive now and will be negative within a quarter or two. For a long investment case this is unfavourable; for the predictability of the direction of revisions it is clear.

11d

Current multiple

–not yet ratedMemo topic 11, : the Rater's score1 answer
D-0735
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

F358th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The co-movement table supplied for this work holds no price history for Maersk, so this answer rests on the dashboard's return and rating data for the peer set, on the first set's cohort figures, and on the sell-side's commentary on positioning; the Consumer should treat the correlation statements as inferred, not measured, and my first research request asks for the measurement. On that evidence the share price moves with the container freight cycle, not with the logistics sector it is classified in, and it moves with roughly twice the amplitude of its closest listed peer. The multiple is therefore industry-driven in direction and company-driven in magnitude: what the market pays is a bet on rates, and Maersk is the instrument the market uses to express it because it is the most liquid, the most spot-exposed and the most shorted of the carriers. For predictability that is a mixed result. The direction of the multiple over 18 months follows the direction of rates, which is predictable; the amplitude is set by positioning, which is not; and a price-to-earnings multiple is undefined for the years in which the forecast puts EPS near zero, so the only multiple the memo can use with confidence is price-to-book or value-to-invested-capital, whose industry range (0.4-0.6x at troughs, about 1.0x at peaks; 0.81x today) is the relevant anchor.

The evidence, such as it is:

  • Against the sector, idiosyncratic. Over twelve months Maersk is +80% while the dashboard's logistics sector is dispersed from -24% (CTT) to +128% (CMB.TECH); the asset-light forwarders, the natural sector comparators, are DSV +3% and Kuehne+Nagel +50%; the parcel and post names are +11% to +55%. There is no sector factor to co-move with; the "sector" is a label.
  • Against the container industry, co-moving in direction with a much larger beta. Hapag-Lloyd, the Gemini partner with the same network and the same freight market, is +10.7% over twelve months with a D technical-momentum rating, against Maersk's +80% and A. The first set recorded the shipping cohort at +82% over twelve months, so Maersk has tracked the wider shipping complex rather than its direct peer. The divergence from Hapag-Lloyd has identifiable company causes: Maersk's higher spot exposure (56% against 35-50%), which made its Q2 loaded rate rise 22% against Hapag-Lloyd's 9%; the Gemini cost gains accruing to the reported numbers of the larger partner; a liquid free float (about 80m of average daily value traded) against Hapag-Lloyd's tiny float; and positioning (short interest of about 33% of free float in August 2025, squeezed; Deutsche Bank's October 2026 note on quant deleveraging forcing algorithmic short covering through technical levels). In the July 2025 rate inflection HMM rose 39.6%, Maersk 14.4% and Hapag-Lloyd fell 1.9% (iM Securities): same industry, three amplitudes, ordered by float and spot exposure.
  • The multiple's level is company-specific in a way that is unhelpful. Maersk's current P/E is 64.8x (trailing), the 24-month-forward P/E 52.7x, both at the 100th percentile of its own ten-year history; Hapag-Lloyd's P/E in the dashboard is negative; the FactSet five-year median 24-month-forward multiple for Maersk is 22.4x with a range of 5.7x to over 2,500x. These numbers measure the denominator's collapse, not a valuation judgement. On price-to-book, Maersk at about 0.81x FY2026 book sits inside the industry's historical band (0.4-0.5x at the 2016 and 2023 troughs; Deutsche Bank's 0.6x EV/IC for Ocean; above 1.0x only at the 2021-2022 peak), and the Terminals business inside it is valued at under 6x EV/EBITDA against 12x for listed pure-play terminal operators (Morgan Stanley). So the aggregate multiple is industry-driven (a cyclical at 0.8x book in a late-cycle peak), with a company-specific conglomerate discount on the part that deserves a premium.
  • Factor overlay. Maersk sits in the top percentile of relative strength in a market in which, per the first set's environment digest, the momentum tilt moved to underweight and defensives lead cyclicals; Jefferies notes that in style rotations beats fail to drive outperformance while misses are punished. This overlay is neither sector nor industry; it is positioning, and it argues that the next 10-20% move can occur on no company news.

What the Consumer should conclude for the fair multiple (topic #11) and the lifecycle (topic #10): the multiple that should be judged is price-to-book against the industry's own cycle, not P/E against Maersk's P/E history, and on that measure the stock is at a late-cycle peak level with the earnings turn ahead; container stocks have troughed on price six to twelve months before the earnings trough in previous cycles (first set C-84), so a price trough in 2027 ahead of a 2028 earnings trough is the pattern to expect. For predictability: the direction of the multiple is predictable (de-rating from 0.8x toward 0.5-0.6x book as the rate turn becomes visible in the numbers from Q4 2026), the path is not.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

F2518th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What historical precedents, rules of thumb and evidence show about how companies in this sector have outperformed and underperformed, and how far Maersk fits those patterns

Judgement: The evidence is consistent across cycles. Container carriers outperform when capacity is absorbed or when the orderbook is low and scrapping high, and underperform when an orderbook delivers into normalising routes. The money is made buying at about 0.4-0.5x book with low orderbooks, not at about 0.8x book with an orderbook of about 40% of the fleet. Within the group, the outperformers through a downturn have the lowest cost, the strongest balance sheet, contracted revenue, or an asset-light model. Maersk today fits the late-cycle peak pattern almost exactly, and fits none of the trough-entry patterns.

Reasoning and evidence:

  • Precedents in the files: the 2015-2016 downturn brought heavy Ocean losses, two years of depressed rates and a guidance cut of more than 15%, and Maersk traded as low as 0.5x book. 2023 normalisation took group EBITDA to 839m in Q4 2023 (Morgan Stanley in February 2024: "Q4 miss. Soft guide. Buy back suspended"). The 2020 pandemic shock cut the share price by 36%. The disruption upswings (2021-2022, 2024, 2026) produced huge beats and re-ratings.
  • Rules of thumb from the Sector and Industry digests: "split a ship owner's earnings into a premium and a floor: the premium from detours and blockades leaves within weeks of the routes reopening" (a judgement); "estimates rising on stretched valuations across the group reads as a peak"; "the shipping owners' repeated guidance upgrades measure spot rates ... earnings of lower quality than the beats suggest". From the Wisdom Library: lazy cyclical extrapolation, demand pulse reversion, false friends (the vessel-life change, the guidance raises), and competitive intensity as entry minus exit.
  • Maersk's fit to the peak pattern: the shares are up 80% in twelve months and 33.7% in three; relative strength is in the 97th percentile; FY2027 EPS revisions are +459% over three months; the 24-month-forward P/E is at the 100th percentile of its own ten-year history; the orderbook is at a record; Suez transits are recovering; P/B is about 0.8x against a trough of 0.4-0.5x.
  • Within-group pattern: Maersk has outperformed Hapag-Lloyd (+80% against +10.7% over twelve months) because of its higher spot exposure (56%) and Gemini cost gains; the same spot exposure makes it fall faster in the downturn. The Bernstein pair trade (long DSV, short Maersk) and the Sector digest's preference for contracted car carriers express the same pattern.
  • An unusual feature: the usual pattern is that the bear case is qualitative and unmodelled (Wisdom Library, Gold). Here it is inverted. The bear case is fully modelled (consensus EPS −75% in FY2027 and −50% in FY2028; 8% Buy ratings), while the bull case (that congestion and disruption keep absorbing capacity) is qualitative and unmodelled. This is why upside surprises have recurred since 2024. Bearish sell-side ratings are therefore not by themselves a contrarian buy signal; the relevant question is whether the FY2027-2028 numbers are still too high.

What it means for the model: the set four analyst should expect the share price to anticipate the earnings downturn by six to twelve months, with the price trough plausibly in 2027 ahead of a 2028 earnings trough. For the operating model, use 2016 and 2023 as the analogues for the speed of rate decline, and 2019 as the analogue for a moderate trough.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

F2516th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: To a fair trough valuation, the shares could fall about 35-45%, to about DKK 13,000-15,000, with a stress case near DKK 11,000 (about −50%). Upside, if disruption persists into 2027, is about +20-25% (DKK 27,000-28,000). Over 12-18 months the skew is clearly to the downside, and the main uncertainty is timing.

Three cross-checks (our calculations, using consensus book value per share of about USD 4,190 for FY2027-2028 and DKK/USD of 6.67):

  1. Trough price-to-book: past troughs were 0.4-0.5x (Morgan Stanley's bear case uses 0.4x; Maersk "traded as low as 0.5x" in previous panics; Deutsche Bank cited 0.6x in August 2026). 0.5x gives about USD 2,095, or DKK 14,000 (−39%); 0.4x gives about DKK 11,200 (−51%); 0.6x gives about DKK 16,800 (−27%). Today's multiple is about 0.8x.
  2. Sum of the parts with Ocean valued at zero: Terminals EBITDA of about 2.06bn × 10x ≈ 20.6bn; Logistics & Services about 1.91bn × 7x ≈ 13.4bn; net cash excluding leases of 10-13.7bn; less lease liabilities of 12.2bn (mostly Ocean vessels); less minorities of about 1.1bn. That totals about 30.7-34.4bn, or about USD 2,100-2,350 a share, i.e. DKK 14,000-15,700. At today's price the market is effectively paying about 15-20bn for Ocean; at the trough that value can fall to zero. DNB Carnegie's sum-of-the-parts similarly "assigns extremely limited value to Ocean"; its bear case is DKK 15,500.
  3. Market reference points: the 52-week low is DKK 11,778 (when FY2027 consensus was negative); the median and mean sell-side targets are about DKK 19,500-20,000 (13-15% below the price); bear targets range from DKK 6,700 (Morgan Stanley, an older bear case on a lower book value) to DKK 15,500 (DNB Carnegie).

Upside cross-check: if disruption persists through 2027 and FY2027 EPS lands near the top of the broker range (about USD 490-500, Clarksons and Barclays), 1.0x book gives about DKK 28,000 (+22%).

Timing: container stocks usually trough before earnings; with the earnings trough most likely in 2028, the price trough is plausibly in 2027.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

F3019th of 19

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The market narrative over the next 18 months will be written by the supply cycle: Red Sea capacity returning, contract rates resetting lower and the 2027 delivery wave. Maersk's company-specific positives (Terminals growth, Logistics & Services margin progress, a possible larger distribution in February 2027) are real but too small to change the story. The novel positives of 2026 (two guidance raises and a likely third) are in the price, and their source, spot rates, is turning. A new positive staircase becomes plausible only from mid-2028, when the trough and the post-2029 decline in deliveries come into view.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–72

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The two sets are highly congruent, and their congruence is of the useful kind: it comes from one causal chain running through every answer (a visible supply wave meeting the unwinding of a disruption-driven capacity buffer, transmitted through extreme price leverage into Ocean EBITDA, and amplified below EBIT by items that reverse together), not from the answers repeating each other. Where the sets differ, the second set corrected the first with better data (lease repayments of 3.5-3.9bn a year rather than 4.5-5bn; the first year-on-year revenue decline in Q2 2027 rather than Q4 2026; the company's "100 per FFE = 0.7bn" being a half-year figure), and the corrections make the case tighter, not looser. I find three places where the answers jar, one place where the sets jar with the evidence, and one place where I disagree with both; none of them changes the direction of the case.

Where the sets support each other (the critical chain):

  • External forces (first set C-02, C-03, C-10, C-31: fleet growth of 7.4% in 2027 and 13.2% in 2028 against demand of 2-4%; Suez transits recovering; scrapping near zero; 26-30bn of industry net cash) feed directly into the second set's rate path (2,746 to 2,075 to 1,905 per FFE) and its loop analysis (P-05: withdrawal from H2 2027, scrapping peaking 2028-2029, no tightening before 2030). The AlphaSense reports written for this set independently confirm each link: supply response latency of three to six months (Seaspan), scrapping engaging only after a year of widespread losses (2015-2016 precedent), and consensus expecting scrapping to engage from 2028.
  • Competitive advantage (first set C-41, C-47: Terminals a genuine moat, Gemini a shared cost edge, the balance sheet a capacity to suffer) feeds the second set's earnings floor (non-Ocean EBIT of 2.3-2.9bn) and its Ocean cost advantage (EBITDA positive, EBIT negative at the trough). The AlphaSense report on demonstrated strengths confirms the break-even arithmetic: Q4 2023 Ocean EBIT of -920m at a loaded rate of 1,925 and a unit cost of 2,280, and an all-in Ocean break-even of about 2,200-2,300 per FFE today, which is exactly where the second set's FY2028 rate plus other revenue (2,195) sits.
  • Management (first set C-57, C-70: strong operator, weak capital allocator, a sandbagger in rising markets) feeds the second set's guidance expectations (a raise on 5 November; FY2027 guidance set low in February 2027) and its capex path (5bn a year regardless of the cycle).
  • Economic strength (first set C-49, C-54, C-75: EPS a pure function of rates; the vessel-life change 88% of FY2028 consensus EBIT; a FY2027 EBIT cone of -3bn to +8bn) feeds the second set's P-01 and P-08 (EPS near zero for three years; the per-share bridge for FY2028).

Where the answers jar:

  1. Capital returns in February 2027. The first set (C-19, C-22) contemplated a larger buyback or a special distribution in February 2027, "well founded on the cash balance"; the second set assumes no new programme and a flat share count from 2027. The sell-side splits the same way (Morgan Stanley projects a 2bn programme on balance-sheet headroom; DNB Carnegie, Jefferies and Bernstein say a repeat is unlikely, with Bernstein's company-basis free cash flow of about 200m for 2027). I side with the second set as the base case, because every precedent of a soft outlook (no new programme after March 2016; suspension in February 2024 despite 4.7bn of net cash and a strong FY2023) points the same way, and because the FY2027 guidance announced on the same day will show negative company-basis free cash flow. But I put the probability of some programme (most likely a token 0.5-1bn, justified by 15.5bn of net cash excluding leases and the owner's preference for distributions) at about 35-40%, not nil. The Consumer should treat this as the one two-sided event in February 2027.
  2. The 5 November 2026 event. The first set judged it "roughly balanced"; the second set "modestly negative against consensus" because its H2 2026 EBITDA (7.6bn) sat well below the 9.3bn consensus requires. The evidence since 26 October (peers' Q3 prints and index levels, set out in I-01) says the quarter was stronger than the second set assumed, and I lift our Q3 and Q4. On the numbers the event is now roughly neutral; the risk is in the reaction to Q4 and 2027 commentary, not in the FY2026 figure.
  3. Scores. The first set scored financial leverage at 75 ("negligible and very comfortable") while the second set showed net debt including leases of about 7bn by 2030 and net financial items turning from +50m to -600m. Both are right on their own terms, but the Consumer should not read the first set's 75 as a statement about EPS: the balance sheet removes solvency risk and simultaneously stops supporting earnings.

Where the sets jar with the evidence: both sets and all three bearish houses have been wrong on timing for three years (the Red Sea in 2024, tariff front-loading in 2025, Hormuz and congestion in 2026), and management's "structurally higher rates" view has been right on timing each time. The sets handle this honestly with a 20% disruption-extended scenario, but the Consumer should note that the record of normalisation forecasts in this industry is poor (the AlphaSense dispersion report finds a renewed disruption arriving within twelve months of each forecast normalisation in 2021, 2024 and 2026). This argues for humility on when, not on whether.

Where I disagree with both sets: on volume. The first set wanted Maersk at market growth, the second set put it one point below market (1.8% in FY2027, 2.2% in FY2028) for forwarder diversion. Customer evidence gathered for this set (a food company's procurement director expecting 2027 ocean volumes up 6-8% and naming Maersk and Hapag-Lloyd as better positioned than ZIM; a home-furnishings retailer seeing Maersk "holding a slight edge" as rates ease; Kuehne+Nagel's sea volumes +5% in Q3 with share gains on outbound Asia) suggests Maersk's reliability edge keeps direct shippers even as forwarders divert. I would hold volume at the market's 3%. It changes nothing material (100k FFE is about 10m of EBIT), so I do not change the input; I record it so the Consumer does not read a sub-market volume line as evidence of commercial damage.

Internal consistency checks that pass: the second set's FY2028 trough rate is tested against Q1 2026 (base rate about 1,350 fuel-adjusted at break-even in a tighter market), against 2016 (1,795 nominal, about 1,500 fuel-adjusted) and against Q4 2023; the non-Ocean floor is tested against 2019 (joint-venture income of 185m and 187m of dividends received in a weak year) and against Terminals' 2016 (EBIT per TEU -33%, ROIC halved to 6.3%) and 2023 (revenue per move -8.2%) precedents; the tax floor is tested against 2019 (377m on 657m of pre-tax profit) and Q4 2025 (78m on 51m). The congruence is earned, not asserted.

What it means for the memo: the Consumer can treat the two sets as one model with one driver and can write the memo's topics #1 to #6 from it without reconciling contradictions; the two open questions to carry are the February 2027 capital-return decision and the timing of the Suez return.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

F308th of 8

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: The direction of the case is fully known and the level is not. Every element of the supply-side thesis (the Suez return and its capacity release, the 2027-2028 orderbook, the absence of scrapping, the 500k TEU order, the cash-rich industry's "game of chicken") is articulated by all five of the most active houses, has been since the first half of 2026 at the latest, and is reflected in ratings (Bloomberg in August: 1 Buy, 12 Hold, 14 Sell; FactSet mean rating Underweight) and in the shape of consensus (EBITDA -38% in FY2027, -14% in FY2028). What is articulated by one or two houses only, and by no one in combination, is the part of our assessment that turns a moderate trough into losses: the fixed tax charge under tonnage tax (DNB Carnegie alone models it, at 480m flat), the decay of interest income with the cash balance (DNB Carnegie alone), the share of 2027-2028 earnings that is the vessel-life depreciation benefit (Bernstein and DNB Carnegie flag it; everyone includes it; nobody shows FY2028 EBIT without it), the 1.1bn Ocean impairment headroom (disclosed by the company; modelled by no broker), the pass-through content of revenue (unmodelled; consensus FY2028 revenue is 11% above ours), and the quarterly timing of the reset (only DNB Carnegie does the H2 2026 run-rate arithmetic). Investor commentary, as far as the evidence reaches, is about the rally, not the fundamentals: the 2026 outperformance (+84% to September on Bernstein's count), the 33% short interest of August 2025 and its squeeze, quant deleveraging and momentum, and the recurring observation that the bears have been early for three years. The narrative that could still move is therefore the below-EBIT narrative, and it moves against a long case; the one dynamic that could move the narrative in the company's favour, management's landside-bottleneck thesis, is already the most debated item in the coverage and is contested by Morgan Stanley, Bernstein and DNB Carnegie with data. For 18 months to three years out, the narrative is thin: two brokers carry FY2029 numbers, nobody carries a 2030 recovery, and the scrapping cycle and the post-2028 decline in deliveries, which are the memo's eventual positive, are discussed only as the far end of the bear case.

Coverage, as the AlphaSense narrative report states it: AlphaSense holds research from about 27 houses; the report draws on the five most active and widely followed (Deutsche Bank, DNB Carnegie, Bernstein, Morgan Stanley, Jefferies), which it puts at about 85% of the influential commentary, and supplements them with company filings, expert calls and trade press; smaller regional brokers, non-English research and technical analysts are left out. FactSet shows 18 contributing brokers, of which the named ones with recent notes are Goldman Sachs, UBS, Fearnley, Clarksons, Barclays, DNB Carnegie, Morgan Stanley, Morningstar, Deutsche Bank, Nordea, Sydbank, Jyske Bank, Arctic, Bernstein and ABG Sundal Collier (the last three mis-aligned in the block, as the first set warned). Views below are credited only where a house's own document was cited in the reports written for this work.

Dynamic by dynamic: who discusses it, how prominently, since when, with what view.

  1. Suez return and the speed of capacity re-entry. Universal and prominent. Morgan Stanley runs a weekly Suez Canal transit tracker (54% of pre-disruption levels on 5 October; 59% in late October) and has framed the return as "supply risk" since 6 July 2026 and as a 7% capacity release since September; Bernstein tracks the six Gemini services and the doubling of transits over the summer; Deutsche Bank models a 0.7% fall in TEU-mile demand in 2027 from a reopening; DNB Carnegie treats it as the trigger for convergence on 2016 lows; Jefferies reports transits at 47% of peak. Known to everyone; the disagreement is only about pace and about whether congestion offsets it.
  2. The 2027-2028 orderbook and the absence of scrapping. Universal. Bernstein's quarterly container compendium (orderbook at 42% of the fleet; scrapping at zero; "play chicken"); Deutsche Bank citing Clarksons (supply +9.5% in 2027, +12.7% in 2028, demand +4%); Jefferies' delivery schedule (3.29m TEU in 2027, 5.23m in 2028; a scrapping cycle "highly likely from 2028" as 1,700 ships pass 25 years); DNB Carnegie (scrapping of 2.0% in 2027 and 3.9% in 2028 against deliveries of 9.2% and 12.6%). Known, modelled, and the source of every Sell rating.
  3. Maersk's 500k TEU order against its 4.1-4.3m TEU fleet target. Widely discussed since September 2026 and contested. Bernstein ("pivoting back to fleet growth?", 9 October; "illogical" given management's own terminal-constraint argument); trade press on the Chinese yards and the about 5bn cost; Morgan Stanley on the capital-return consequences. Known; its cash-flow timing (back-loaded instalments) is not analysed by anyone but the AlphaSense capital-flexibility report.
  4. The vessel useful-life extension (about 700m a year) and its share of 2027-2028 earnings. Moderately discussed. DNB Carnegie (6 February 2026) called it "difficult to justify" because only 13% of the active fleet is over 20 years old and flagged the write-down risk; Bernstein models the 175m a quarter and says that without it 2027-2028 EPS "would drop significantly deeper into negative territory"; Deutsche Bank frames it as alignment with Hapag-Lloyd's 25-35-year policy. What no one publishes is a FY2028 EBIT line without the benefit, which would show the consensus year at about break-even economically. Half known.
  5. The free cash flow definition after leases and the buyback. Widely discussed, with conflicting conclusions. Morgan Stanley (negative free cash flow from 2027; yet a 2bn buyback expected in 2027 on balance-sheet headroom, with leverage staying below 1.5x even at 1bn a year); DNB Carnegie (buybacks beyond early 2027 "highly unlikely"; the 1.5x rating constraint); Jefferies (a 2bn repeat unlikely); Bernstein (company-basis free cash flow of about 200m in 2027). The definitional gap between "above zero" and FactSet's 4.75bn is explained by Deutsche Bank and DNB Carnegie. Known to the sell-side; the February 2027 outcome is a genuine open debate, which is why it is the one two-sided event.
  6. The tax charge under tonnage tax when Ocean is loss-making; Pillar Two. One house. DNB Carnegie models tax flat at 480m through 2028 and explicitly ties its negative FY2028 EPS to the tax drag; the company's own tax report and annual reports give the mechanism (2016: 1.1bn of tax on a 1.9bn loss; 2019: 377m on 657m; Q4 2025: 78m on 51m); Pillar Two is treated as negligible by the company (40m in 2025) and by Hapag-Lloyd (0.9m provision). The German tax audit on the allocation of shipping income is unquantified by the company and unmentioned by any broker. Largely unknown: most brokers' positive FY2028 EPS cannot be reconciled with a 0.4-0.5bn fixed charge.
  7. The path of interest income as cash balances change. One house. DNB Carnegie shows net financial items turning to a net expense of about 70m in 2026; Morgan Stanley notes cash shrinking as capex exceeds operating cash flow. No house publishes the arithmetic (about 40m of pre-tax income per 1bn of cash consumed; interest income falling from about 0.85bn to about 0.35bn by 2030 on our path). Unknown in practice.
  8. The Ocean impairment headroom (1.1bn at end-2025 against 4.1bn). Disclosed in the 2025 annual report with its assumptions (9.8% pre-tax discount rate, 2.1% terminal growth; a 0.5-point discount-rate change is about 2.7bn); DNB Carnegie's useful-life critique implies it; no broker models an impairment, and the comparison with peers' discount rates (COSCO 10.2% pre-tax; ZIM 12.5% post-tax) and with ZIM's 2.06bn write-down in 2023 appears only in the AlphaSense impairment report. Unknown as a dated event; a FY2027-test impairment reported in February 2028 would be a surprise to the narrative even though it would be non-cash.
  9. Forwarders diverting volume because of the integrator strategy. One house, prominently. Bernstein (since at least July 2026; forwarders control about 50% of volumes and "view Maersk as a direct competitor"; the long-DSV, short-Maersk pair) and expert calls; Morgan Stanley's September 2025 note on Logistics & Services "behind the pack" covers the returns but not the diversion. Known to sector specialists, unquantified by anyone (the report lists shipper allocation surveys as the gap).
  10. APM Terminals' storage and demurrage income normalising. Three houses. DNB Carnegie's terminals EBITDA sits 13% below consensus on a swift contraction of storage income; Bernstein (13-14 August) attributes the Q2 yield growth to storage and tariffs; Morgan Stanley covers the same dynamic at ICTSI. Known to the specialists; consensus Terminals EBITDA (2.06bn for FY2027, growing) has not absorbed it.
  11. Management's view that landside bottlenecks keep rates structurally higher. The most debated dynamic in the coverage. Bernstein's 14 August note is titled "pinning hopes on terminal capacity"; Deutsche Bank reports the view and sets Clarksons' supply growth against it; Morgan Stanley contests it with Drewry port utilisation of 60-70% outside China; DNB Carnegie treats congestion as temporary; Jefferies notes that weather-driven bottlenecks offer temporary support only. Fully known, contested, and the explicit basis of the bulls' numbers (Clarksons, Barclays, Goldman Sachs, UBS) and of the price.
  12. The 2026 rally, short positioning and the timing of the downturn (investor commentary). The sell-side describes the rally (Bernstein: +84% to September; DNB Carnegie: "never-ending rally; bears await normalisation", 7 October), the ratings disconnect (Deutsche Bank citing Bloomberg's 1/12/14), the short-squeeze mechanics (Deutsche Bank's quant deleveraging note, 15 October) and the style-rotation risk (Jefferies). Direct investor commentary (social media, letters) is thin in the evidence; what exists says investors "ignored sell-side warnings to chase lucrative short-term rate momentum" and that disruption has become "the new normal". The car-carrier contrast (Wallenius Wilhelmsen's 6.5bn backlog; Hoegh's 80% contract cover; both upgraded by DNB Carnegie) and the forwarder preference (DSV, Kuehne+Nagel) are the sector's expressed alternatives. The timing of the downturn is the live argument: the bears say Q4 2026 to H1 2027; the holders say not until the disruptions end.

What is not in the narrative at all (the items that could move it, all negative for a long case): the combination of items 4, 6 and 7 into a single statement that trough EPS is negative, not +62; the H2 2026 run-rate problem inside FY2026 consensus (only DNB Carnegie's arithmetic); the revenue line's pass-through content; the 9.3bn of committed-but-uncommenced leases and 5.9bn of green-fuel offtakes as fixed 2027-2030 costs; the goodwill reallocation across Forwarding, Solutions and Landside exposing Solutions (1.7% margin) to a stand-alone test; the quarter in which Maersk's reported year-on-year revenue decline first appears (Q2 2027, not Q4 2026, which means the first two prints of 2027 look better than the narrative expects and the next two worse). On the positive side, not in the narrative: the post-2028 decline in deliveries and the scrapping cycle as a 2029-2030 recovery story, the conglomerate discount on Terminals (about 14bn on Morgan Stanley's peer-multiple arithmetic, raised by one house only), and the Gemini cost edge as a reason Maersk's trough is shallower than its peers' (Jefferies and Carnegie argue both sides).

How well known, in one table, for the Consumer's topic #7 and #8: fully known and in the numbers: items 1, 2, 3, 11 (direction), 12. Known to specialists, partly in the numbers: items 4, 5, 9, 10. Known to one house, not in consensus: items 6, 7. Not known as events: item 8, the leases and offtakes, the goodwill reallocation, the quarterly shape of 2027, the 2029-2030 recovery.

For 18 months to three years out: the narrative is a bear case without an end. DNB Carnegie's convergence on 2016 lows, Morgan Stanley's "net losses for the sector from 2028" and Bernstein's "capacity chicken until cash reserves are exhausted" describe the trough but not the exit; Jefferies' scrapping cycle from 2028 and the thin 2029 consensus are the only forward-looking positives. When the memo reaches its lifecycle topic (#10), the Consumer can say that the stock is in a late-cycle "reinforcing" phase on price and momentum, that the narrative is already positioned for "stalling or collapsing" on fundamentals, and that the "bottoming" narrative (scrapping, falling deliveries, a cleaner balance sheet, Terminals re-rated) does not yet exist in print; it will be written from mid-2028.

The gap between our assessment and the narrative: the narrative already contains our direction and most of our supply-side evidence, so there is no information advantage in being bearish on rates; what it does not contain is our level (EPS below zero in 2028-2029 rather than +62 and +119), our timing (Q4 2026 and H1 2027 rather than "2027"), and the balance-sheet events (impairment, buyback end, dividend to nil). Those are the dynamics the narrative will absorb over the next 18 months, and each absorption is negative for the price. For a long case this is unfavourable; the only favourable gap is the far-dated recovery story, which is 18-30 months from being written.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 25 October 2026, 22:52 to 23:15 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Maersk (MAERSK.B-DK): answers to the first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Prepared 2026-10-25 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years). All money in USD unless stated; the share price is in DKK.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Orientation for the Consumer: the case in one paragraph, the transmission mechanism, the data traps

The case in one paragraph. Maersk is a capable operator (the Gemini network with Hapag-Lloyd, an outstanding terminals business, a net-cash balance sheet) sitting inside a structurally poor, volume-first industry that is at a disruption-driven cyclical earnings peak in the second half of 2026. Against it stands an unusually visible supply wave (container fleet growth of about 4.6% in 2026, 7.4% in 2027 and 13.2% in 2028 on Bernstein's figures, against demand growth of 2-4%), and the Red Sea capacity buffer is already unwinding (Suez container transits at 54% of pre-disruption levels by early October 2026; six Gemini services back on the Suez route). Group earnings are a function of one variable, the average loaded freight rate. The direction of that variable over 2027-2028 is close to certain; its timing and depth are not. The share price (DKK 22,870; +80% over twelve months; dashboard ratings of A for technical momentum, A for sell-side earnings momentum and F for valuation) prices the company at about 0.8x FY2026 book value, well above the 0.4-0.5x of past troughs, while only 8% of analysts rate it a Buy. For the model: FY2026 is a peak year that will very probably be guided up again on 5 November 2026; FY2027 is the reset year; FY2028 is the most probable trough; FY2029 consensus rests on two brokers.

The transmission mechanism to model. We derived this from the Q1 and Q2 2026 disclosures, and it is approximate:

  • Group EBIT = Ocean EBIT + Terminals EBIT (run-rate about 1.7-1.8bn a year: Q1 2026 EBIT of 436m at a 33.2% margin) + Logistics & Services EBIT (about 0.8bn a year: 173m in Q1 and 217m in Q2 2026, a 5.1% margin) less unallocated costs (about 0.2-0.3bn). Non-Ocean EBIT is therefore about 2.3-2.6bn a year: this is the earnings floor.
  • Ocean EBIT ≈ loaded FFE × (average loaded rate − bunker cost per loaded FFE − other Ocean cost per loaded FFE). In Q1 2026 the arithmetic is 3.203m FFE × (2,081 − about 437 bunker − about 1,700 other) ≈ −192m, the reported Ocean EBIT. In Q2 2026 it is 3.361m FFE × (2,746 − about 625 bunker − about 1,840 other) ≈ +935m. The "other cost per FFE" rose by about 140 between the quarters (congestion, Cape routing, charter hire); it already includes the roughly 175m-a-quarter benefit of the vessel-life change.
  • The company's disclosed sensitivity confirms the scale: 100 per FFE on the average loaded rate moves annual EBIT by about 1.3bn (about 13m loaded FFE a year). Volume barely matters by comparison: the stated sensitivity is about 10m of EBIT per 100k FFE. Model the rate first, the bunker cost second, the volume third.
  • Consensus decoded through this mechanism: FY2027 consensus group EBIT of 2.05bn implies Ocean EBIT of roughly −0.3 to −0.5bn; FY2028 consensus group EBIT of 0.8bn implies Ocean EBIT of roughly −1.6 to −1.8bn. FY2026 consensus group EBIT of 8.1bn implies a second-half Ocean loaded rate of roughly 3,100-3,200 per FFE, about 15% above Q2's 2,746.
  • Below EBIT: net financial items are roughly neutral to slightly positive (interest income on about 18.5bn of cash offsets lease and debt interest; pre-tax profit exceeded adjusted EBIT in 2023, 2024 and 2025); tax is low under the tonnage tax regime (reported 3-15% in 2021-2025); minorities are material in weak quarters (Q1 2026 net profit to shareholders of 53m against pre-tax profit of 292m); the share count falls about 2% a year on the current 1bn-a-year buyback.

Data traps found in the files (do not trip on these):

  1. FactSet's capital-structure snapshot labels market capitalisation "326,627 USDm"; that figure is in DKK. Market value is about USD 50bn (14.6m diluted shares × USD 3,428), and enterprise value including lease liabilities is also about USD 50bn because cash (18.5bn) roughly equals debt including leases (17.5bn).
  2. The per-broker rows in the financials block are misaligned: "Arctic Securities", "Bernstein Research" and "ABG Sundal Collier" repeat the Goldman Sachs, UBS and Fearnley numbers exactly. Bernstein's own published FY2026/FY2027 EPS is 553.17/85.75 (latest Earnings Review). Use the section-3 medians, and do not attribute per-broker figures by name without checking.
  3. FactSet's quarterly net working capital swings from about −9.6bn to about zero at every Q4. This is an artefact of half-year/annual lines, not seasonality; do not model it.
  4. Terminals segment revenue history (3.96bn for FY2025) and consensus (5.69bn for FY2026) are on different bases (Q2 2026 Terminals revenue alone was 1.45bn). Do not read +44% growth.
  5. The dashboard's sell-side revision levels are in DKK (FY2027 EPS "1,149.77" is the USD 172 mean; the target price "19,965" is DKK).
  6. Free cash flow: the company's definition deducts lease repayments (about 1.18bn in Q1 2026 alone, so roughly 4.5-5bn a year); FactSet's does not. The company's FY2026 guidance of "FCF above zero" and FactSet's consensus FCF of 4.75bn are therefore not in conflict.
  7. The vessel useful-life change (20 to 25 years from 1 January 2026) lowers depreciation by about 700m a year in every year from 2026, not only in 2026. History before 2026 is not like-for-like on EBIT, and the benefit sits inside every broker's FY2027-2028 number.
  8. The FY2026-2028 consensus medians for net income and EPS come from different broker sets (net income ÷ EPS gives 15.0m shares for FY2026 and 13.1m for FY2027-2028). Do not derive a share count from them; use 14.6m diluted shares falling about 2% a year.

Valuation snapshot used throughout: price DKK 22,870 (USD 3,428; implied DKK/USD 6.67); 52-week range DKK 11,778-25,380; consensus (FactSet median) EPS 490.00 (FY2026), 124.69 (FY2027), 62.09 (FY2028), giving P/E of 7.0x, 27.5x and 55x; EV/EBITDA about 3.6x, 5.8x and 6.7x; price-to-book about 0.81x on FY2026 book value per share of 4,257; 24-month-forward P/E 52.7x, at the 100th percentile of its own ten-year history on the dashboard's measure; mean target price USD 2,993, 13% below the price.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S (Maersk), MAERSK.B-DK (Nasdaq Copenhagen; reports in USD): an integrated container logistics group with three segments: Ocean (container shipping, about 65% of 2025 revenue, operated in the Gemini Cooperation with Hapag-Lloyd), Logistics & Services (reorganised from April 2026 into Forwarding, Solutions and Landside) and Terminals (APM Terminals).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (the closest listed liner and Maersk's Gemini partner; its own cost programme is a comparator); COSCO Shipping Holdings (the largest listed state-backed liner, with its own downturn cost record); Evergreen Marine, Yang Ming and HMM (listed Asian liners with different fleet ownership and charter mixes); ZIM (a charter-heavy, high-cost liner being acquired by Hapag-Lloyd, an example of the opposite cost structure); MSC (private, the largest carrier by capacity); CMA CGM (private, bond-issuing, integrated with CEVA Logistics, the closest integrated comparator); Ocean Network Express (private, a Japanese-owned joint venture with published results); DSV and Kuehne+Nagel (asset-light forwarders, comparators for Logistics & Services cost flexibility); DP World and International Container Terminal Services (terminal operators, comparators for APM Terminals' cost structure). Inputs: What share of a container liner's cost base is fixed per sailing (vessel depreciation, charter hire, crew), variable per box (terminal handling) or variable per voyage (bunker, canal fees), and how does Maersk's disclosed Ocean cost split (Q1 2026 interim report: terminal handling 39%, network costs excluding bunker 27%, bunker 19%, SG&A 9% of Ocean operating costs) compare with peers? How did Ocean EBIT, EBIT margin and unit cost per FFE move at Maersk and peers in the 2015-2016 downturn, in 2019 and through the 2023 normalisation, and how fast did management withdraw capacity (blank sailings, idling, slow steaming, charter redeliveries) in each? How should the company's disclosed sensitivities be interpreted for modelling (Deutsche Bank Research: a USD 100/FFE change in loaded rates moves annual EBIT by about USD 1.3bn; company: USD 100/tonne of bunker moves EBIT by about USD 0.2-0.3bn; 100k FFE moves EBIT by about USD 10m), and are they symmetric in a falling market? How much of the Cape of Good Hope diversion cost (Morgan Stanley: about USD 500m a month for the network) unwinds when services return to Suez, how fast, and did surcharge revenue fall in step in past normalisations? Are the Gemini cost benefits structural (company, Q2 2026: about USD 950m of benefits; Q1 2026: Ocean unit cost at fixed energy −7% year on year), and how do hub-and-spoke unit costs behave at lower utilisation or with more Suez routings? What share of Maersk's fleet is chartered (about 43%), what are the remaining durations and day rates of its charters (for example three 9,500 TEU vessels extended at USD 41,000 a day to 2031), and how much can be redelivered in 2027-2028? What did the 2026 corporate programme (1,000 corporate roles; savings reported as USD 130m and as USD 180m) and the 2023 headcount reduction (from about 110,000 to 100,000) actually deliver, and what came back? How does the vessel useful-life change (20 to 25 years from 1 January 2026; about USD 700m lower annual depreciation per the company) change the fixed-cost base, and does it raise future impairment risk? In Logistics & Services, why did the Solutions EBIT margin fall to 1.7% in Q2 2026 from 3.8%, which costs are start-up or "whitespace" costs, and what have DSV, Kuehne+Nagel and CEVA shown about cutting warehouse and contract-logistics costs? How much operating leverage do APM Terminals' costs carry against throughput and utilisation (88-89% utilisation in late 2025)? What did Hapag-Lloyd's Ventus programme (about USD 1.1bn of targeted run-rate savings) deliver, and on what timetable? Focus: How Maersk's Ocean margin responds per unit of freight-rate change as the Red Sea capacity buffer unwinds and scheduled 2027-2028 vessel deliveries arrive, and how much of the cost base management can genuinely take out (capacity withdrawal, charter redelivery, overhead, network efficiency), how fast, judged against the 2015-2016, 2019 and 2023 precedents.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (Gemini partner with a higher contract share, about 55% of volume, so a direct comparator for contract against spot exposure); COSCO Shipping Holdings, Evergreen Marine, Yang Ming and HMM (listed liners with different spot exposures and route mixes); ZIM (high spot exposure, Transpacific-heavy); MSC (private, the largest carrier, described in broker research as pricing aggressively); CMA CGM (private, integrated with CEVA, sells to both forwarders and direct shippers); DSV and Kuehne+Nagel (forwarders that buy ocean capacity and resell it, so they see carrier pricing from the customer side); DP World and International Container Terminal Services (terminal pricing comparators for APM Terminals). Operating conditions: Red Sea and Suez Canal disruption and its unwinding (2024-2026 diversions and the 2026 return of services to Suez); supply-wave years with low scrapping (2015-2016, 2023, and the scheduled 2027-2028 delivery peak); bunker price spikes (2022, and the 2026 Strait of Hormuz disruption with Brent above USD 100); demand shocks and tariff front-loading followed by air pockets (Q2 2020; the 2025-2026 tariff cycles); port congestion and weather disruption (2021-2022; the 2026 typhoon season); annual contract negotiation seasons held in falling spot markets; terminal tariff setting in tight against slack throughput markets. Inputs: What is Maersk's mix of contract and spot volumes (company and Bernstein: about 44% long-term rate products, about 56% spot-exposed), and how have contract durations, indexation and bunker adjustment formulas changed (customer interviews describe quarterly and monthly terms replacing annual contracts; surcharges revised every 14 days)? How have Maersk's realised loaded rates moved against market indices in each condition (for example, Q1 2026 average loaded rate −14.3% year on year against −32% for the Drewry World Container Index, per Bernstein's Q1 2026 review), and what explains any gap? Is there evidence of a sustained reliability premium (expert interviews claim 5-10%; one expert call of 13 June 2025 says that about 95% of the time standard customers demand the cheapest rate), and did it hold in loose markets? How quickly were surcharges withdrawn when costs fell in the past (for example, port disruption surcharges halted after the January 2025 US East Coast labour agreement), and how much of the roughly USD 500m a month of Middle East cost recovery is likely to be clawed back in rate negotiations? How concentrated are Maersk's customers (top 200 customers about 46% of revenue during peak periods; forwarders up to 50% of ocean volumes), and is there evidence that forwarders are shifting volume away from Maersk because of its integrator strategy? Where are 2027 Asia-Europe and Transpacific contract rates settling, and how do shippers' tender behaviours compare with 2016 and 2023? How has APM Terminals' revenue per move behaved through past liner downturns (company: +7.1% to USD 385 in Q2 2026; +6.8% FX-adjusted in 2025), and how are concession tariffs set or regulated? Can EU ETS and FuelEU costs be passed through in a loose market? How do forwarders' gross profit per unit and Maersk Logistics & Services pricing behave when ocean freight rates fall? Focus: Whether Maersk realises any rate premium or contract protection against market indices when capacity loosens, how quickly the 2026 spot and surcharge gains reverse through 2027 contract and spot pricing, and whether APM Terminals' pricing power holds through a liner downturn.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Maersk's earnings path over 2027-2029 depends on industry feedback loops (profits funding newbuild orders, cash buffers delaying scrapping, congestion absorbing capacity, losses forcing capacity withdrawal), and their timing decides the timing, depth and duration of the next phase of the freight cycle. Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (Gemini partner, listed, its own order and capital-return decisions); COSCO Shipping Holdings (state-backed, behaviour in past price wars); Evergreen Marine, Yang Ming and HMM (listed Asian liners with differing orderbook-to-fleet ratios); ZIM (the only large listed liner reported in net debt, being acquired by Hapag-Lloyd); MSC (private, the largest orderbook and a share-first strategy); CMA CGM (private, fleet growth and logistics acquisitions); DSV (an asset-light forwarder that benefits from falling freight costs); Global Ship Lease and Seaspan (charter owners whose fleet utilisation and charter rates show the tonnage side of the loop). Inputs: The profit-to-orderbook loop: how did windfall cash in 2021-2022 and 2024-2026 translate into newbuild orders (orderbook reported at about 13-15m TEU, about 39-44% of the fleet, by DNB Carnegie, Jefferies, Deutsche Bank and Bernstein in Q3 2026), including Maersk's own order of about 26 large LNG dual-fuel vessels for 2029-2030 delivery? The cash-buffer loop: does the industry's net cash (Bernstein: about USD 26-30bn) delay scrapping and capacity discipline (Deutsche Bank: about 5k TEU scrapped in 2025), and what level of losses triggered discipline in 2016 and 2023? The loss-to-withdrawal loop: how fast did blank sailings, idling, slow steaming, charter redeliveries and scrapping engage in past downturns, and in what order? The congestion loop: high volumes and port bottlenecks absorb effective capacity (Bernstein and industry sources: about 8.5% of the fleet in 2026; Jefferies: 12.6% in port queues in August 2026), lifting rates and encouraging front-loading; how does it reverse? The route-disruption loop: diversions raise rates and fuel burn while creating an incentive to return to Suez once it is safe (CMA CGM returned first; Gemini moved six services back by September 2026); how fast did capacity re-enter in comparable reopenings? The integrator-forwarder loop: does Maersk's end-to-end strategy push forwarders to rival carriers, lowering Maersk utilisation and pushing it further toward direct shippers? The terminals loop: does Gemini's hub-and-spoke design concentrate volume in Maersk-owned hubs (company: Ocean volumes into own terminals +26% year on year in Q1 2026), raising utilisation, pricing and further terminal investment? The contract-duration loop: does volatility shorten contracts, raising spot exposure and volatility further? The capital-return loop: do peak-cycle buybacks and dividends reduce the cash available to order ships, or not? Focus: Which loop dominates the timing of the next phase of the freight cycle and its end, specifically at what point and through what mechanism the container industry has historically started withdrawing capacity, and whether congestion and route-disruption loops can absorb the scheduled 2027-2028 deliveries.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Maersk's earnings per share over 2027-2029 will be shaped by non-operating and accounting items (the vessel useful-life change, minorities, financial income, tonnage tax, buybacks and lease accounting) that are large relative to the low operating profit consensus expects in those years. Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (tonnage tax, lease-heavy charter fleet, comparable accounting choices); COSCO Shipping Holdings (minorities and terminal joint ventures); Evergreen Marine (a listed liner with large financial income from its cash); DSV and Kuehne+Nagel (buyback- and dividend-driven EPS models); CMA CGM (private bond issuer, comparable lease and tax disclosures). Inputs: Depreciation: the vessel useful-life extension from 20 to 25 years effective 1 January 2026 (company: about USD 700m lower depreciation in 2026, about USD 175m a quarter); how the benefit evolves as the fleet ages and as newbuilds (2029-2030) and terminal capex add depreciation; and whether peers made similar changes. Impairment: the risk to Ocean vessel values and to goodwill from logistics acquisitions (more than USD 8bn since 2019, per Bernstein, including LF Logistics and Pilot Freight) in a downturn, and the impairment tests the company discloses. Net financial items: interest income on cash and deposits (USD 18.4bn at Q1 2026), lease interest (USD 176m in Q1 2026), FX and derivative effects (the 2025 annual report shows USD 510m of gains on currency derivatives held for trading and a positive USD 595m translation effect). Tax: the tonnage tax regime and reported tax rates (3-15% in 2021-2025); taxable non-tonnage activities (Logistics & Services, Terminals); the effect of global minimum tax rules. Minorities: Q1 2026 net profit to shareholders of USD 53m against pre-tax profit of USD 292m (Bernstein); which subsidiaries and terminal joint ventures carry minority interests, and how their share behaves through the cycle. Associates and joint venture income from Terminals. Share count: the buyback of up to DKK 6.3bn from February 2026 (second phase up to DKK 3.15bn from August 2026), treasury-share cancellations (share capital reduced by more than DKK 1.1bn in Q2 2026), the Foundation's pro-rata participation, and the dividend policy of 30-50% of underlying net result. Leases: lease liabilities (USD 12.2bn at Q2 2026), lease repayments against the capex guidance (USD 10-11bn for 2026-2027 combined), and the difference between the company's free cash flow definition and the brokers' definitions. Definitions: "underlying" EBITDA and EBIT against reported, and the basis brokers use for adjusted EPS. Focus: How large and how persistent the non-operating and accounting items are relative to the low operating profit expected in 2027-2028, above all the vessel-life depreciation benefit, minorities, financial income and the buyback, and whether any of them could change the sign of reported earnings per share.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 26 October 2026, 11:11 to 11:31 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Maersk (MAERSK.B-DK): answers to the second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Prepared 2026-10-26 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets. Money in USD unless stated (the share price is in DKK). "Our base case" means the forecasts recorded in the forecast-inputs form; every figure below is consistent with that form.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Orientation for the Consumer

The base case in one paragraph. FY2026 underlying EBITDA of about 12.35bn, the top of the 10.5-12.5bn guidance and 12% below FactSet consensus of 14.0bn. Q3 2026 is very strong (about 4.45bn); Q4 2026 is where consensus is too high, because the Suez return and the Asia-Europe rollover hit realised rates within one quarter. FY2027 EBITDA is 7.4bn (consensus 8.7bn), FY2028 6.3bn (consensus 7.5bn) is the trough, FY2029 7.1bn stays near the trough, and FY2030 8.5bn is the first real recovery. Earnings per share (underlying, after minorities, our definition close to the brokers' basis): 370, 43, -47, -15 and 53 for FY2026-FY2030, against consensus of 490, 125, 62 and 119 (FY2029 on two brokers). Company-basis free cash flow (after leases): +2.0bn, -2.0bn, -2.7bn, -2.2bn, -0.6bn. Net cash excluding leases falls from about 15.5bn at end-2026 to about 4.7bn at end-2030; including about 12bn of lease liabilities the group moves from net cash to net debt in 2027.

The model's spine. Ocean EBITDA = loaded FFE x (average loaded rate + other Ocean revenue per FFE - Ocean operating cost per FFE before depreciation). Everything else is small or slow by comparison.

Ocean driver FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Loaded volume (m FFE) 13.36 13.60 13.90 14.30 14.90
Average loaded rate (USD per FFE) 2,746 2,075 1,905 1,960 2,060
Other Ocean revenue per FFE (hubs, other) 408 300 290 290 290
Ocean operating cost per FFE before D&A 2,524 2,110 2,030 2,050 2,080
Ocean EBITDA (USDm) 8,600 3,600 2,300 2,860 4,000
Ocean EBIT (USDm) 4,200 -950 -2,300 -1,890 -950

Non-Ocean EBIT (Terminals including joint ventures, plus Logistics & Services, less unallocated costs) is 2.3bn in FY2026 and 2.3-2.9bn in FY2027-FY2030: the earnings floor the first set identified, which we confirm.

Where we stand against consensus, and why, in four points.

  1. FY2026: consensus needs H2 2026 EBITDA of about 9.3bn (4.6bn a quarter); we have 7.6bn. Q3 can come close to the consensus run-rate; Q4 cannot, with six-plus Gemini services back on Suez and Asia-Europe spot rolling over since September.
  2. FY2027-FY2028: our EBITDA is 15-16% below consensus because base freight rates fall to roughly the 2016 trough in fuel-adjusted terms, and the decline is fast (2023-like) in H1 2027.
  3. The gap widens sharply below EBIT, and this is the least appreciated part of the case: a tax charge that does not fall with profit (about 0.4-0.5bn a year), net financial items that turn from a support into a drag as cash is consumed, and a buyback we expect to stop in February 2027. These items, not Ocean alone, take FY2028-FY2029 EPS below zero.
  4. Revenue falls further than consensus in FY2027 (-16.6% against -7.5%), but about half of that extra fall is pass-through surcharge and hub revenue that leaves with matching cost. Revenue is a poor guide to profit in this downturn.

New data points and corrections the Consumer should carry forward:

  • The company's "USD 100 per FFE = 0.7bn of EBIT" (Q2 2026 slides and press release) is a rest-of-2026 sensitivity, not an annual one. Half a year of loaded volume (about 6.7m FFE) x 100 = 0.67bn. The annual sensitivity is about 1.35bn (13.4-13.9m FFE), as the first set and Deutsche Bank state. The AlphaSense report on operating leverage reads the gap as brokers assuming more spot exposure; that reading is wrong.
  • Lease repayments are about 3.5bn (FY2025 actual) to 3.9bn (FY2026: 1.2bn in Q1 and 0.86bn in Q2), not the 4.5-5bn a year the first set extrapolated from Q1 alone. Company-basis free cash flow is therefore about 0.6-1bn a year better than the first set implied, though still negative from 2027.
  • At low profit, Maersk's tax rate is meaningless; the absolute charge is what matters. In FY2019 tax was 377m on pre-tax profit of 657m (a 57% rate); in FY2023 it was 319m; Q2 2026 alone was 377m with a higher Pillar Two charge. Ocean profit sits under tonnage tax and Ocean losses give no relief, while Terminals and Logistics & Services profits are taxed normally.
  • The first year-on-year revenue decline comes in Q2 2027, not in Q4 2026 or Q1 2027 as the first set expected. Q1 2026 was a depressed base (loaded rate 2,081, revenue 12.97bn), so Q1 2027 still shows growth (about +7%) even as the sequential decline is under way.
  • Bernstein's FY2026 EPS appears as 553.17 in the first set's latest Earnings Review and as 306.44 (25 September 2026) in the EPS-bridge report. Do not quote either by name without checking the date.
  • The form's "Net financial items" line, by necessity, carries three things: the share of profit of joint ventures and associates (about +0.4bn a year, mostly Terminals), net interest and other financial items, and minority interests. The form has no separate lines for them; Part 4 of the form lists each one.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: Financial forecasts

Notes the Financial Analyst must read before entering Part 1:

  • EBITDA basis: the company's "underlying" EBITDA, which is the basis the brokers use (FY2025: 9,572 on the brokers' basis, against FactSet operating EBITDA of 9,200). Implied EBITDA: 12,350 (FY2026), 7,420, 6,320, 7,130, 8,500.
  • Depreciation and amortisation is total D&A, including the amortisation of acquired intangibles. For Maersk neither the company's underlying EBIT nor the brokers' adjusted EPS adds that amortisation back, so leaving it in keeps our figures on the consensus basis. The amount is not separately disclosed in our files (we estimate about 0.2bn a year); if the model requires D&A excluding it, reduce each year by about 200 and our EPS cross-check rises by about 14 a share. D&A includes the vessel useful-life benefit (about 700 a year lower than under the old 20-year policy).
  • Net financial items carries three things, because the form has no separate lines: (1) the share of profit of joint ventures and associates, which Maersk reports inside EBIT (+400, +370, +400, +430, +460); (2) net interest and other financial items, including lease interest of about 700 a year (+50, -150, -350, -500, -600); (3) minority interests (-170, -120, -100, -110, -120). The model's "pre-tax profit" is therefore after minorities, and "pre-tax profit less tax" equals net profit to shareholders.
  • Tax rate: the rates are derived from absolute tax charges of -1,000, -450, -400, -420 and -480 divided by the model's pre-tax profit (6,380; 1,070; -280; 200; 1,240). Maersk's Ocean profit is under tonnage tax and Ocean losses give no relief, while Terminals and Logistics & Services profits are taxed normally, so the charge is close to fixed at low profit (FY2019: 377 of tax on 657 of pre-tax profit). The FY2028 rate is negative only because pre-tax profit is negative; with the convention tax = -rate x pre-tax profit, it produces a charge of 400. If the model floors or caps the rate, override it with the absolute charges above.
  • Dividend per share is in USD, for the fiscal year's earnings (paid the following March), at 40% of underlying net profit: FY2026 about DKK 1,000. Nil for FY2028-FY2029 because underlying net profit is negative.
  • Share count: no change other than buybacks; Maersk funds incentive plans from treasury shares.

Buybacks less new shares issued for cash -980 -100 0 0 0

Working capital is the balance at the end of each fiscal year as a percentage of the last twelve months' revenue.

Notes on Part 2:

  • Capital spending: H1 2026 actual 1,937; FY2026 plus FY2027 of 10,400 sits inside the company's 10-11bn guidance for the two years combined. FY2028-FY2029 carry instalments on the 26 LNG dual-fuel vessels (2029-2030 delivery) and the Terminals capacity programme.
  • Working capital: year-end FactSet balances (receivables plus inventories less payables) were +1.4% of revenue at end-2024 and -0.3% at end-2025; the mid-year figures of about -17% are a FactSet artefact of half-yearly lines and must not be used. FY2026 rises to 1.0% on higher freight receivables and bunker inventory values (Q1 2026 working-capital outflow of 913); a partial release follows in FY2027.
  • Lease payments are principal repayments only (FY2025 actual 3,500; Q1 2026 1,200 and Q2 2026 863). Lease interest (about 700 a year) is inside net financial items, so it must not be deducted twice.
  • Buybacks: the DKK 6.3bn programme (about USD 1bn) ends on 29 January 2027; FY2026 includes H1 actual of 591. We assume no new programme in February 2027, following the February 2024 precedent when the outlook turned and company free cash flow guidance is negative.

Part 3. The next 18 months, one column for each of the company's reporting periods

Input Quarter to 30-Sep-26 Quarter to 31-Dec-26 Quarter to 31-Mar-27 Quarter to 30-Jun-27 Quarter to 30-Sep-27 Quarter to 31-Dec-27
Revenue growth (%) 25.3 22.3 7.4 -16.0 -27.4 -24.3
EBITDA margin (%) 25.0 19.3 17.0 14.4 13.5 11.3

Revenue growth is against the same period a year earlier.
