The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Ocean, the driver of nearly everything. Ocean EBITDA = loaded FFE x (average loaded rate + other Ocean revenue per FFE - Ocean operating cost per FFE before D&A).
Ocean driver FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Loaded volume (m FFE) 13.36 13.60 13.90 14.30 14.90
Average loaded rate (USD per FFE) 2,746 2,075 1,905 1,960 2,060
Other Ocean revenue per FFE 408 300 290 290 290
Ocean cost per FFE before D&A 2,524 2,110 2,030 2,050 2,080
  • Rates: Q3 2026 realised rates catch up with the Q2 spot surge; from Q4 2026 the return of Gemini services to Suez (six by September 2026; a full Maersk return expected by the Suez Canal Authority by March 2027), easing port congestion (8.5-12.6% of the fleet in queues in August-October 2026) and fleet growth of 7.4% (2027) and 13.2% (2028) on Bernstein's figures push rates down; contracts (44-45% of volume, quarterly and monthly terms) transmit spot within about a quarter. FY2028 is the trough at roughly the marginal carrier's cash cost; scrapping and idling bring stabilisation in 2029 and a modest recovery in 2030.
  • Volume: Maersk grows about 2% a year in 2027-2028, about one point below market demand (forwarder diversion, share loss to MSC and CMA CGM), then 3-4% as its own 2029-2030 newbuilds arrive.
  • Ocean costs: Cape-diversion network costs fall away with the matching surcharges (pass-through); bunker per FFE falls from about 610 (FY2026, Brent about 95) to about 530 (FY2027, Brent about 90, shorter routing) and about 490 (FY2028, Brent about 80), recovered through the Fossil Fuel Fee at 80-90% in 2027 and about 70% in 2028; congestion costs ease; labour, handling and carbon costs inflate by about 2-3% a year; self-help (network, overhead) builds to about 0.8bn of EBITDA by FY2028.
  • Sensitivities: 100 per FFE on the average loaded rate is about 1,360 of EBITDA and EBIT in FY2027 (about 95 USD a share, almost untaxed); 100 dollars a tonne of bunker is 200-300 net of recovery (company); 100k FFE of volume is about 10 of EBIT (company); 10% on the USD is about 300 (company). The company's "100 per FFE = 0.7bn" in its Q2 2026 materials is a rest-of-2026 figure, not an annual one.
  • Terminals: volume +3-4% a year, revenue per move +3-5% a year, flat EBITDA in FY2027 as congestion-related storage income normalises, then +5-7% a year from the 30% capacity programme.
  • Logistics & Services: revenue dips about 3% in FY2027 with lower freight pass-through, then grows 4-5% a year; EBIT margin from 5.1% to 5.8%.
  • Below EBIT: joint-venture income about 0.4bn a year; interest income falls with the cash balance (net cash excluding leases about 15.5bn at end-2026, 11.0bn, 7.9bn, 5.5bn, 4.7bn); tax close to a fixed 0.4-0.5bn at low profit; minorities about 0.1bn.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • FY2026 guidance (13 August 2026): underlying EBITDA 10.5-12.5bn; underlying EBIT 4.5-6.5bn; company free cash flow above zero; capex 10-11bn for 2026-2027 combined; global container volume growth 2-4%.
  • Our inputs: underlying EBITDA 12,350 (top of the range; consensus 14,007 is 12% above the top); underlying EBIT including joint ventures about 6,500 (top of the range; consensus 8,137); company free cash flow about +2,000; capex 4,800 plus 5,600 = 10,400 for 2026-2027 (inside the range).
  • What we expect on 5 November 2026: a raise or narrowing of FY2026 guidance toward roughly 11.5-13bn of EBITDA, below current consensus.
  • What we expect in early February 2027: first FY2027 guidance with a midpoint below our 7,420 (Maersk sets initial guidance low in uncertain markets; in February 2026 it guided to an EBIT loss), negative company free cash flow guidance, and no new buyback.
  • Standing targets: ROIC above 7.5% through the cycle (our inputs imply ROIC of about 1-3% or below in FY2027-FY2030; not met); Logistics & Services EBIT margin of 6% (not met in our horizon; 5.8% by FY2029); Terminals ROIC above 9% (met).

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • EBITDA and EBIT: the company reports "underlying" EBITDA and EBIT, which exclude restructuring and integration costs, gains and losses on disposals and impairments. The brokers' consensus is on this underlying basis (FY2025 brokers' basis EBITDA 9,572 against FactSet operating EBITDA 9,200). Underlying EBIT includes the share of profit of joint ventures and associates (about 0.4bn a year) and the vessel useful-life benefit (about 0.7bn a year); neither the company nor the brokers adjust for the latter, so every FY2026-FY2028 figure, ours included, contains it.
  • EPS: brokers' adjusted EPS is close to underlying net profit to shareholders over shares outstanding. FY2025: brokers' basis 181.63, reported basic 178.99, FactSet adjusted diluted 148.17. Our cross-check EPS is underlying net profit after minorities over average shares outstanding (14.55m in FY2026, 14.34m thereafter), including the vessel-life benefit and excluding restructuring and any impairment. Consensus net income divided by consensus EPS gives inconsistent share counts (different broker sets); do not derive shares from consensus.
  • Free cash flow: the company's definition is operating cash flow less gross capex, less lease repayments, less lease interest (Q2 2026: 549). FactSet's definition is operating cash flow less capex, before any lease payment (consensus FY2026 4,754). Our cross-check is on the company's definition; on FactSet's definition our figures would be about 6,600, 2,500, 1,600, 2,000 and 3,600.
  • Dividends: declared in DKK; FactSet's DPS is dated by period and converted to USD (FY2025: 72.58, which is DKK 480).
  • Market value: FactSet's capital-structure snapshot labels market capitalisation "326,627 USDm"; that figure is in DKK (about USD 50bn).

Free cash flow: company definition, after lease repayments and lease interest, before acquisitions and distributions. FY2026 free cash flow is slightly below the arithmetic of Parts 1 and 2 (about 2,150) because H1 2026 actuals (-874 in Q1, +549 in Q2) show a larger in-year working-capital outflow than the year-end balance implies.

Research requests for the next set

Company: A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S (Maersk), MAERSK.B-DK (Nasdaq Copenhagen; reports in USD): an integrated container logistics group with three segments: Ocean (container shipping, about 65% of 2025 revenue, operated in the Gemini Cooperation with Hapag-Lloyd), Logistics & Services (reorganised from April 2026 into Forwarding, Solutions and Landside) and Terminals (APM Terminals, including joint-venture terminals such as Tanjung Pelepas).

Request: T-D1

Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (Gemini partner, listed, higher contract share, its own Ventus cost programme); COSCO Shipping Holdings (state-backed, its 2016 and 2023 downturn record); Evergreen Marine, Yang Ming and HMM (listed Asian liners with different cost and charter structures and slower Red Sea returns); ZIM (charter-heavy, high-cost, being acquired by Hapag-Lloyd); MSC (private, largest carrier and orderbook, volume-first); CMA CGM (private bond issuer, integrated with CEVA, first to return to Suez); Ocean Network Express (private Japanese joint venture with published results); DSV and Kuehne+Nagel (asset-light forwarders, the comparison for Logistics & Services margins); DP World and International Container Terminal Services (terminal operators, the comparison for APM Terminals). Operating conditions: the return of container services to the Suez Canal (Gemini moved six services back by September 2026; the Suez Canal Authority expects a full Maersk return by March 2027); the 2027-2028 vessel delivery peak with very low scrapping (Bernstein, 9 October 2026: fleet growth of 7.4% in 2027 and 13.2% in 2028); easing port congestion after the 2026 typhoon season; annual contract seasons held in falling spot markets; bunker prices driven by the Strait of Hormuz disruption (Brent about USD 111 in the week of 23 October 2026); a demand air pocket after tariff front-loading. Inputs: Can Maersk hold an Ocean cost per FFE advantage over non-Gemini carriers once capacity is loose, given the company's Q1 2026 disclosure of Ocean unit cost at fixed bunker down 7% year on year and Gemini benefits of about USD 950m (Q2 2026)? How much of Maersk's Ocean cost base falls away with Cape-of-Good-Hope routing (DNB Carnegie, 8 May 2026: about USD 500m a month of added network cost), and does surcharge revenue fall faster or slower than that cost, judged by the 2025 precedent of war-risk surcharges cut 67% in January and a further 80% in February? What did Maersk's Ocean EBIT, average loaded rate and unit cost do in 2016, 2019 and 2023, and in Q4 2025 and Q1 2026 (average loaded rates of USD 2,046 and 2,081 per FFE), and what does that say about Ocean's break-even rate today? Has Maersk ever delivered its Logistics & Services EBIT margin target of 6% or its 10% organic growth target, and what did DSV, Kuehne+Nagel and CEVA show about lifting contract-logistics margins after warehouse start-up losses (Maersk Solutions margin 1.7% in Q2 2026 against 3.8% a year earlier)? Has APM Terminals' revenue per move held through past liner downturns, and how much of its 2025-2026 growth (revenue per move +7.1% to USD 385 in Q2 2026) is storage and demurrage income tied to congestion? How quickly did Maersk cut capacity and cost in 2016 and 2023 (blank sailings, slow steaming, charter redeliveries, the 2023 headcount reduction from about 110,000 to 100,000), and how much of the saving came back? Focus: Whether Maersk's demonstrated strengths (Gemini cost and reliability, Terminals pricing, the balance sheet) are enough to keep its Ocean earnings above peers' and any Ocean losses smaller and shorter than theirs in the 2027-2028 supply wave, and whether the Logistics & Services margin recovery management promises has any precedent in its own record.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Hapag-Lloyd (Gemini partner with a 50-65% contract share, the direct comparison for how fast realised rates fall); COSCO Shipping Holdings (state-backed, endures losses without idling); Evergreen Marine, Yang Ming and HMM (listed Asian liners, still avoiding the Red Sea in September 2026); ZIM (high spot exposure, net debt); MSC and CMA CGM (private carriers whose fleet additions set the price); Ocean Network Express; Global Ship Lease and Seaspan (charter owners whose redelivery and rechartering data show when liners cut capacity); DSV and Kuehne+Nagel (forwarders that gain when freight costs fall). Operating conditions: Suez normalisation, partial or complete; the 2027 and 2028 delivery waves; congestion persisting or clearing; a renewed Red Sea closure or a Strait of Hormuz escalation; a 2016-style price war; oil above or below USD 100 a barrel; contract seasons in falling spot markets. Inputs: How dispersed were container carriers' earnings outcomes against forecasts made 12-18 months earlier in 2015-2016, 2019 and 2022-2023, and which single driver explained most of each miss? How wide is the current sell-side range for Maersk (FactSet, 25 October 2026: FY2027 EPS from USD -22.30 at Jyske Bank to USD 496.76 at Barclays; FY2028 EBITDA from USD 1.5bn to USD 10.2bn), and what rate and Suez assumptions separate the high and low forecasts? How sensitive is Maersk's EBIT to the average loaded rate (Deutsche Bank and Morgan Stanley: about USD 1.3bn per USD 100 per FFE a year), and is that sensitivity symmetric in a falling market? How quickly did idle capacity, charter redeliveries and scrapping respond to losses in past downturns (scrapping of 197k TEU in 2015 and 657k TEU in 2016; idle capacity 6.0% of the fleet in mid-2020 and 2.2% in September 2026), and at what level of industry losses did each begin? How fast did capacity re-enter in comparable route reopenings, and how much of it was absorbed by slower steaming on the shorter route? How often did a renewed disruption (2021, 2024, 2026) arrive within 12 months of a forecast normalisation, and how much capacity did each absorb? Focus: How much of the difference between Maersk's 2026 peak earnings and its 2027-2028 earnings depends on the freight rate alone, and how wide and how skewed the range of outcomes for 2027 is, given that both a fast Suez normalisation and a renewed disruption are plausible within months.

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 5 November 2026, 07:20 to 07:40 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Maersk (MAERSK.B-DK): answers to the third and fourth sets of questions

Prepared 2026-11-04 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo. Money in USD unless stated; the share price is in DKK (DKK 22,870 on 2026-10-23, the dashboard's price). The Q3 2026 results are due tomorrow, 5 November 2026; where that print would change a judgement below, I say which judgement and in which direction.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Orientation for the Consumer

The steer in one paragraph. The first and second sets form one coherent case, and the third set's tests confirm it: Maersk's earnings over the next 18 months are a function of one exogenous variable, the Ocean base freight rate, whose direction (down from Q4 2026) is about as predictable as anything in this industry gets and whose level in 2027 is not. The case is therefore simple to monitor and hard to forecast: the plausible FY2027 EBITDA range is about 3.7-13bn around our 7.4bn, and because the vessel-life benefit, a fixed tax charge, decaying interest income and the end of the buyback sit between EBITDA and EPS, the sign of FY2028 EPS is decided by about 150-200 per FFE of rate. Against the market: FY2026 guidance will very probably be raised tomorrow (I now expect the year to land at about 12.8bn of EBITDA, above the top of the current 10.5-12.5bn range and below the 14.0bn consensus); FY2027 guidance in February 2027 will very probably be set below consensus and at or below our base case; and sell-side estimates for FY2027-FY2029 will be cut, most of all at EPS. The bearish direction of the case is fully articulated by the sell-side (8% Buy ratings; three of the five most active houses rate the shares Sell or Underperform and the other two Hold) and largely ignored by the share price (+80% in twelve months). What nobody articulates in combination is the below-EBIT squeeze that turns the "moderate trough" of consensus (FY2028 EPS +62) into losses. The dynamics that are not yet in the narrative are negative for a long case.

The model gap, the inputs I change, and a warning on the model's return calculation.

  • The one flagged cross-check gap (FY2029 EPS: the second set's -15.00 against the model's -16.61) is a convention artefact, not a difference of view. The second set encoded a fixed tax charge of 420 as a rate of 210% on its own rounded pre-tax profit of 200; the model's pre-tax profit is 216.4 (its EBITDA of 7,146.4 exceeds the second set's rounded 7,130 because the growth and margin inputs carry one decimal), and 210% of that 16 of extra profit adds 34 of tax. I change the FY2029 tax rate to 194.0% so that the charge is 420 on the model's base; EPS becomes about -14. No other year is flagged, and the FY2026-FY2028 model figures match the cross-check within rounding.
  • I raise Q3 and Q4 2026 (see I-01 and I-02): the Part 3 growth and margin inputs for those two quarters, and with them FY2026 revenue growth, EBITDA margin, tax rate and dividend per share, and the FY2027 and H2 2027 growth rates, which are measured against the new base. FY2026 EBITDA moves from 12.35bn to about 12.8bn; the FY2027-FY2030 levels are unchanged. Tomorrow's Q3 print supersedes this change: the Consumer should read the actual against about 4.7bn of underlying EBITDA for the quarter.
  • The model's return at the guideline fair multiple (-105.8% at the 18-month column, with a negative value per share) is a mechanical artefact of applying a 22x price-to-earnings multiple to negative two-year-forward EPS. It carries no information. The memo's topics #11 and #12 should rest on the first set's book-value and sum-of-the-parts cross-checks (a trough of about DKK 13,000-15,700 at 0.5x book or with Ocean valued at nil; about DKK 28,000 at 1.0x book if disruption persists through 2027) and on a mid-cycle earnings figure, not on the P/E of a trough year. Likewise, the model's year-end FY2026 net cash including leases of -3.4bn differs from the second set's +3.3bn because the model's cash-flow lines carry no new leases; use the second set's cash path (net cash excluding leases of about 15.5bn at end-2026 falling to about 4.7bn by end-2030).

Three facts that arrived after the second set and that the Consumer should carry forward:

  1. Israel sent the Hapag-Lloyd acquisition of ZIM "back to the drawing board" (Women's Wear Daily, 1 October 2026). The first set's assumption of a Q4 2026 close, and the integration noise inside Gemini from 2027, is now less likely and later.
  2. Maersk discloses USD 9.3bn of lease liabilities committed but not yet commenced (to start between 2026 and 2029) and USD 5.9bn of e-methanol and bio-methanol offtake commitments, on top of the USD 14.6bn of capital commitments at Q2 2026 (2025 annual report and Q2 2026 interim report, via the AlphaSense report on capital flexibility). No broker model we can see prices any of the three. Together they say that the 2027-2030 cost base is more contractually fixed than either set assumed, which supports the second set's decision not to let self-help change the trajectory, and raises the chance that the charter cost per slot stays high into the trough.
  3. The 2027 contract season has opened with carriers seeking increases on Europe (Morgan Stanley, 21 October 2026), COSCO expecting its 2027 long-term contract rates to land higher year on year, partly through floating bunker surcharges (Morgan Stanley, 30 October 2026), and shippers shifting volume to spot (expert calls, 3-4 November 2026). This is the counter-evidence to a fast reset in H1 2027. It bears on timing within 2027 rather than on the FY2027 level, because spot, not contract, sets the level once the delivery wave and the Suez return meet; a contract book signed in December at today's levels would, however, make Q1-Q2 2027 better and Q3-Q4 2027 worse than the second set's quarterly path.

The third set: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S (Maersk), MAERSK.B-DK (Nasdaq Copenhagen; reports in USD): an integrated container logistics group with three segments: Ocean (container shipping, about 65% of 2025 revenue, operated in the Gemini Cooperation with Hapag-Lloyd), Logistics & Services (reorganised from April 2026 into Forwarding, Solutions and Landside) and Terminals (APM Terminals, including joint-venture terminals such as Tanjung Pelepas).

Request: OWN-1

Question: How have A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S's share price, price-to-book and EV-to-invested-capital multiples, and sell-side consensus estimates moved relative to each other and to container freight-rate indices (SCFI, CCFI, Drewry World Container Index) around the last three container-shipping cycle turns (2015-2016, 2022-2023 and 2024-2026): by how many months did the share price lead or lag the freight-rate peak, the consensus-estimate peak and the reported earnings trough in each cycle; what price-to-book and EV-to-invested-capital multiples marked the share-price peaks and troughs for Maersk and for Hapag-Lloyd, COSCO Shipping Holdings, Evergreen Marine, HMM and ZIM; and over the last twelve months what have been the rolling six-month correlation and beta of Maersk's share price to those listed carriers, to DSV and Kuehne+Nagel, to Wallenius Wilhelmsen and Hoegh Autoliners, to the freight-rate indices and to the European momentum and cyclical factors, so that it is clear how far Maersk's price and multiple move with the container industry, with the logistics sector, or idiosyncratically?

Request: OWN-2

Question: What is the current state of investor positioning in, and investor narrative about, A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S shares after their rise in 2026: short interest and securities-lending utilisation by month since August 2025 (when short interest was reported at about 33% of free float), the changes in the largest institutional holders and in index, quantitative and momentum-fund exposure over 2026, the stakes of A.P. Møller Holding and the A.P. Møller Foundation, retail participation on Nasdaq Copenhagen, and what hedge-fund letters, investor presentations, Twitter/X and Substack commentary and the Danish and international financial press say about why investors hold or short the stock, what they expect from the Q3 2026 results on 5 November 2026 and the February 2027 full-year results, and when they expect the container freight downturn to begin; and how did short interest and ownership behave around the February 2024 buyback suspension and the February 2017 dividend cut?

Request: OWN-3

Question: How much of the container-ship capacity absorbed by port congestion in 2026 is structural rather than weather- or disruption-driven, and does the evidence support or refute A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S management's view (Q2 2026 earnings call, 13 August 2026) that terminal and landside constraints will keep freight rates structurally higher as the 2027-2028 vessel orderbook delivers: what are terminal utilisation, container dwell times and berth productivity at the main hubs (Shanghai, Ningbo, Singapore, Tanjung Pelepas, Rotterdam, Antwerp, Los Angeles/Long Beach, Busan) in 2025-2026 against 2019 and 2021-2022 (Drewry data cited by Morgan Stanley on 2 September 2026 put port utilisation outside China at about 60-70%), what terminal capacity is scheduled to open in 2027-2028 at those hubs and across the APM Terminals, DP World, PSA, Hutchison and COSCO Shipping Ports networks, how quickly congestion-absorbed capacity and freight rates normalised when congestion cleared in late 2022 and 2023 and after the March 2021 Suez Canal blockage, and what do terminal operators, port authorities and liner network planners say about whether congestion in 2026 reflects a shortage of terminal capacity or the temporary effects of typhoons, Cape of Good Hope routing and tariff-driven front-loading?
